Chapter Fourteen
ZYJII
I released
her ankle in order to grip her heel—at least, I thought it was her heel—and twist her foot this way and that. It was rigid. Almost no flex at all. I’d never seen anything quite like it. She had five toes, for one, all clustered together at the end.
“How do you grip anything with those tiny stubs?” I asked.
When she continued to watch me with a steady gaze, I looked around, then used my tail to pull her stick to us. I set it on the bank, beyond the waters edge, then released her to stand. I pointed at her very strange foot, then down at mine, and used my toes to pick up her weapon.
She snorted, the corners of her lips lifting in an expression I recognized. It eased something inside me to know her people smiled, just the same as mine. Probably for much the same reasons. She gestured at me, and I set the stick back down on the ground.
Instead of trying to wrap her stubby toes around it, she slid her whole foot beneath it, and
flick!
The stick was suddenly in her hand.
I cheeped in surprise despite myself. My tail looped and curled in a gesture that meant
again
, but she ignored it.
No, not ignored it. She didn’t understand it.
Right. A female without a tail wouldn’t see the silent words spoken with one.
“Again,” I said, and repeated the motion with my tail. Then used the rest of my body to playact setting the stick down and doing…whatever it was she’d done with her foot to make the stick hover in the air.
Smirking, she obeyed. This time, I kept my eyes locked on her tiny foot. She got her toes under it, and made a kind of scooping motion,
tossing
the stick straight up into her hand. I made a low sound of appreciation.
I gestured at her to stay, patting at the empty air with both hands, and said, “Wait.”
Leaving her with her stick, I bounded off, hunting quickly for and finding a lengthy branch that’d dropped from a nearby tree. It wasn’t as straight as hers, but I wasn’t planning to use it for anything important.
She let out a little chuckling huff when I returned and showed her my stick, chest puffed.
“Show me again,” I said. I looped my tail around, and then accented the request with my hands as best I could. “Again.”
The two patches of fur on her brow went up, but she did as requested. When I set my stick down beside hers, and attempted to get my foot beneath it, her brow fur went up again. I decided that meant surprise.
This time, when she did her stick-foot-toss, she did it slowly. She did the whole swoop moving as slowly as a sleepy krisvreep, her stick tilting and rolling off her foot rather than flying.
So, the speed was as important as the motion itself.
We spent a fair amount of time kicking sticks. We took occasional breaks to tend to our meat. Her tiny spits were done much sooner than either the smoked strips or the chunks steaming in leaves. When I finished tending the strips, I joined
her, crouching low. When she sat right on the ground, I followed suit. It gave me an excuse to let my tail curl around her, close but not touching.
She held up one of the sticks to me, her strangely flat teeth already sunk into the meat pierced on the stick in her other hand.
I accepted it with some hesitation. She probably didn’t realize what it meant for a female to give a male Mrriiv food. She was sky-fallen. It didn’t mean anything coming from her.
I told myself that, but my instincts stirred as I accepted the offering. Warmth pooled in my belly before spreading outward. But I could ignore it, so I did.
I took a careful bite of the meat she’d given me, and made a sound of surprise.
“Hot,” I said around my full mouth, trying to keep from burning my tongue. “Good, though.”
It was a little dry, but that happened to mlyrk meat if it wasn’t wrapped in wet leaves and steamed. That was why it made such good jerky.
“This would be better sprinkled with ground riiki,” I added around another bite. “But then,
everything
is better with ground riiki. I usually keep a pouch of it on me, but I used the last of it on my breakfast, and haven’t made more yet.”
I had a whole basket of the small nuts gathered, shelled, and already roasted. I just needed to do the tedious work of grinding them into a fine powder.
Which I explained to my sky-fallen female between bites.
Her avid gaze remained on me as I talked. She made a thoughtful kind of sound, as if she’d understood me, but…that wasn’t possible. Was it?
Setting aside her first stick, she leaned forward, placed a hand on her chest, and said clearly and distinctly, “Rynn.”
I tilted my head, ears pricked forward so hard I felt their quivering at the base of my tail.
Hesitantly, I pointed at myself. “Rynn?”
She shook her head, side to side. She tapped her chest. “Rynn.” Then she gestured at me, tipping her head in a silent question.
Oh! My wing-hands rustled as I straightened my shoulders and puffed my chest. I tapped myself, just as she had, and said, “Zyjii.” Moving more cautiously, I leaned toward her, reaching out to brush my fingerpads over her chest. “Rynn.”
She muttered something that sounded like
kloes iinuf
, before she beamed at me and said, “Yes!”
I blinked, ears shooting straight back in surprise. That word I recognized. She’d…she’d just used one of
my
words!
“Rynn,” she said. My ears came back up, because it sounded different when she said it. She tapped herself. “Rynn.” The light brush of her fingers over my chest made my skin tighten and tingle. “Zyjii.”
“Yes,” I agreed.
She grinned at me, clearly pleased with herself. I was pleased, too. My sky-fallen female had a name, and now she knew mine.
Rynn knew how to play. I didn’t know why that surprised me.
We finished eating, and then returned to the river to wash the grease from the fur on our hands. Well,
I
washed the grease from my fur. She washed it from her skin. Naked, vulnerable skin. It made me wonder why she didn’t wear more clothes. All of her arms, her stomach, and most of her legs were left exposed.
As I studied her, she removed the extra waist wrap to dunk it in the cool river water. She squeezed out the excess water down the back of her neck. She made a soft little moan that made my tail twitch.
Her head twisted as she patted the damp fabric over her shoulders, and promptly caught me staring.
One of her brow furs crept upward in an expression I didn’t understand. Slowly, she dipped her hand into the river. And then she flung water at me.
It hit me in the face, startling a sputter out of me. I shook myself, my wing-hands rattling in affront, and she laughed. The sound—low with a rusty quality that said maybe she didn’t do it often—stroked over me. I let my lips wrinkle in a play snarl. She dipped her hand for another barrage, not noticing my tail until it shoved her in the back, toppling her into the river.
She immediately shot to her feet and whirled toward me, her long mane flinging droplets on me. I lifted a hand to shield my face, laughing so hard I almost couldn’t breathe.
“Y-you look like a half-drowned vriiryn kit!” I gasped.
She made the most adorable little growl. Crouching, she shoved her cupped hands into the water and tossed them up, sending an entire flood straight at me. I let out an alarmed cheeped and darted to the side, but she was immediately right there, catching my arm and using it—and my precarious balance—to pivot me around and shove me into the water.
We played for a while, splashing and dunking each other. I hadn’t laughed so hard in…a great while. I’d forgotten how good it felt. For the first time in a very long time, I didn’t have to worry about how my people would react if they stumbled across me doing something they considered strange or inappropriate.
Rynn helped me forget all that.
Eventually she retreated, hands held up in front of her, palms toward me, speaking quickly in her musical language. When I
followed her out, she smiled, then wrung the water from her mane.
I stopped just short of her to shake myself. She let out a startled cheep as fine droplets showered her, and then she growled at me. I grinned, ears held at their most innocent slant. Huffing, she wiped the spray from her face, gave me a mock glare, then went check on the cook fire.

Chapter Fifteen
LYNN
Zyjii
and I spent most of the rest of the day by the river, tending the fire pit and keeping the local wildlife away from our smoking meat. Since the weird purple-teal river grass did most of the work for us in that regard, we killed time by messing around with our staffs.
He was absolutely enamored with my little trick of kicking my staff into my hand, and spent most of his time trying to figure out how to do it himself. It took some work—what with the basic structure of our feet being so vastly different—but by the time the meat finished smoking, he’d managed three successful kick-grabs in a row.
He held his practice stick out to me with a broad grin on his face, his tail swishing behind him languidly and those weird batwing-hands kind of…fluttering.
I grinned back at him and gave him a round of applause.
Quiet
applause, because who knew what kind of predators might be attracted to loud, sharp sounds. Best not to risk it.
His ears slanted the way they did when he was curious or confused about something, so I didn’t think he understood what
my clapping was for, but he puffed up his chest nonetheless. He was kind of adorable.
It made me want to keep him. Which was a ridiculous notion. He was a sentient, sapient being. Not a pet.
…I wondered if he’d let me feel his ears. Maybe give them a good scritch, right at their base.
Shaking the thought away, I followed him back to the fire and watched him test the smoked strips. He chuffed at them, tail twitching in clear annoyance. I gathered that meant they weren’t ready yet. The steamed packets, however, earned a toothy grin.
“Here,” he said. He added something else, but my translator only picked up ‘hot’ and ‘good.’
The more he talked, the quicker it’d learn, and lucky me, Zyjii liked to talk
a lot
.
I sat next to him and accepted the tightly wrapped leaf packet. I opened it carefully, and good thing too, considering the amount of steam it released.
It didn’t smell quite as good as the roasting skewers had, but the meat was a helluva lot more tender. The addition of the foraged vegetables and his special seasoning blend?
I moaned. I couldn’t help it.
He watched me with avid curiosity, the tip of his tail flicking restlessly. Or maybe that was a sign of pleasure. When I glanced at him, he grinned, all sharp teeth and smug satisfaction.
He dug into his own meal and we ate in relative silence. The jungle, already a noisy place, became downright boisterous as the sun began to set.
We finished eating, washed our hands clean in the river again—he looked annoyed by the grease that clung to the fine fur on his fingers, and I decided I might have to introduce him to the use of chopsticks—and then he check the smoking meat. He grunted in satisfaction and began pulling the strips from the long spits and laying them out on cleaned leaves to cool.
As darkness settled, Zyjii hurried to portion the dried meat out so we could each return to the relative safety of our camps. I assumed he camped but…I studied his odd feet and decided ‘camp’ probably wasn’t the right word. He most likely denned up in the trees.
He tried to give me the lion’s share of food, but I shook my head at him. I tried to give him the larger portion, but his ears snapped back against his skull and he bared his fangs at me, wings rattling.
I glared right back.
I relented when he finally divided it in half. It was more meat than I could eat myself before it spoiled—even jerked, it wouldn’t last forever in such a humid climate—but I figured I could use it to bait traps if it started going off.
We parted ways after that. He took to the trees like the arboreal creature he was. I trudged back to my meager shelter along the ground, paying particular attention to deeper shadows where the glow of the various bits of flora didn’t reach.
At least the glow meant my path was well-lit enough I didn’t have to worry about walking into a bramble patch, or catching my foot on a raised root and wrenching my ankle, or blundering into any number of hazards that tended to hide in the dark.
I made it back to my shelter, safe and sound without a fuss. I hung up the smoked meat with my other supplies, discarded the idea of getting a fire going yet again, and bundled up to sleep.
The next morning, I stood atop the branch I hung my supplies from and felt a weird twinge of disappointment. I didn’t know why I expected Zyjii to replenish my fruit basket. He clearly had his own things to do and foods to gather.
“Idiot,” I chided myself.
I was more than capable of taking care of myself. I didn’t even know why I expected him to bring me more fruit during the night after he’d spent the entire day smoking meat with me.
Ignoring the disappointment that continued to make my chest feel all tight and uncomfortable, I hauled up one of the leaf packets, pulled out a couple strips of dried meat and added it to the last of the fruit in yesterday’s basket. Protein and plant matter. The breakfast of champions.
Bundling up my breakfast, I climbed down the tree and headed back to my shelter. I kind of wanted to attempt stewing one of the fruits that had a similar crunchy consistency to apples, except I didn’t have a pot. Maybe I could roast it? Roasting made most things better.
Still focused on thoughts about how to improve and vary my current diet to keep things interesting and tasty, I hauled myself up the trunk of the fallen tree that made the floor of my shelter, and straightened.
“Morning.”
I jerked hard enough I nearly fell backward off the trunk. Zyjii’s eyes widened and his tail lashed forward, wrapping around my waist about the same time I caught my balance. I clutched my chest and panted for a second.
“Rynn?” Zyjii asked, all hesitance and concern.
“Sweet cartwheeling Jesus, you startled me,” I rasped.
His ears lifted slightly, and then further when I smiled wryly. I did my best to repeat his greeting, but I knew it wasn’t right. There was an underlying purr to it that my human vocal cords simply couldn’t emulate.
“Morning.”
It must have been understandable, because he beamed. He lifted a basket and started chattering at me, understandable now
thanks to my super fancy translation chip and spending most of a day with him.
“I brought you new things to try,” he said, shifting the fruits around to show me. “I thought I could eat with you this morning, so you can tell me which is your favorite.” His busy fingers paused and his ears wilted. “I guess you can’t tell me anything yet, can you?” Then he brightened. “I’ll just have to see if I can figure it out from your reactions. Here.”
Looked like he hadn’t made his delivery last night because he planned to join me for breakfast. Why did that flip the disappointment into glittery joy?
I took the basket from him, and started to gesture to my unused fire pit, but he reached behind him—around one of those wings—and tugged something free. Grinning with shy pride, he offered me a spear like the one he’d used on the river monster. Only this one’s haft was more narrow. Something that’d fit my smaller hands.
“For you,” he said.
I sucked in a breath that sounded suspiciously like a happy gasp. “You brought me a weapon? A hunting spear?”
“To hunt with,” he agreed, though I knew he had no idea what I’d said. “Your little rock-thrower won’t work against big things like the
mlyrk
.”
The translator had no suggestion for that last word, but I didn’t need one. ‘Mlyrk’ obviously meant ‘ugly river monster.’
“Not that you need to hunt big things,” Zyjii continued. His ears went back and his tail unwound from my waist to switch behind him. “You shouldn’t hunt big things. It’s dangerous. This is
definitely
better protection than throwing rocks, though.”
Setting down the basket with our combined breakfast offerings in it, I reached out to stroke a hesitant finger over the spear’s haft.
“You like it?” he asked. “If not, I can bring you shard-rock so you can make your⁠—”
I snatched it from him. He watched me, more curious than wary, as I petted the spear’s haft and crooned over it.
“Oh, you gorgeous piece of weaponry, you. You and I will create beautiful mayhem together. Yes, we will.”
Taking my nonsense as approval—which it very much was—Zyjii grinned at me.
“Breakfast?” he suggested. “Then maybe I can show you some of the forest?”
Sounded like a plan to me.

Chapter Sixteen
LYNN
While we wandered,
I set a few traps. Did I need fresh meat on top of all the smoked stuff? Maybe not, but a woman can only eat so much jerky before she gets sick of it.
Besides, Zyjii found the whole process fascinating. From the way he dropped on the river monster like a furry cannonball, I gathered his people were ambush predators. Humans had to be more tricky about our hunting, especially when hunting alone. And I found I didn’t mind the company.
We spent all day together. He pointed out a plant that sent hair-fine tendrils across the jungle floor. When something stepped on them, a large bulb shot out of the shadows, splitting open like a gaping maw before snapping shut around its prey.
That wasn’t the only carnivorous plant. He pointed out a cluster of mushrooms that picked themselves up and scampered away from the sunlight when he pushed back the fern leaves shading them.
“Those only eat already dead things,” Zyjii explained. “Many different birds will bring them insects to share. Once they eat, they change color from white to red—” he pointed out one mushroom larger than the others, its color closer to magenta
than red “—and then the birds will eat them.” He tilted his head to look up at me. “They are only edible when they are red. Any other color is bad.”
“Considering how dangerous eating unknown fungus is on my planet, I’m not about to go experimenting here,” I said wryly.
He flicked his tail, ears quivering, but hopped up and moved on to the next thing he wanted to show me.
The shadows darkened as day slipped toward night, and I got more antsy. I wanted to make it back to my shelter before full dark. Things changed a lot at night, and my day spent with Zyjii meant I’d learned how very lucky I’d been to avoid coming into contact with any number of viciously dangerous plants while bumbling about.
He seemed content to follow me back, especially when I stopped in at the traps to see if they’d worked as intended. We found two that had broken, one that remained untouched, but the last had snagged the same kind of critter I’d nailed at the river yesterday. Before the river monster attempted to eat me.
I held up one of my catches by the vine that had successfully snapped its thick neck. The creature was as long as my arm, including the tail, with eight stubby legs. It had fur
and
scales, its broad, flat head reminiscent of a rattlesnake’s, but with long whiskers.
Lizard or rodent, I wondered.
Both, I finally decided with a nod. A lizard-rodent. Lizdent? Rozard? Ooooh, rozard. Nice.
“That’s a
kicluck
,” Zyjii said. When I lifted my eyes away from my catch to him, he lifted his hands as if holding an imaginary skewer and chomped at the air. “They’re good to eat.”
A kicluck, huh? I frowned down at the strange thing. Decided I liked ‘rozard’ better.
“Rozard,” I said.
He blinked. I pointed at it. “Rozard.”
His ears came up and quivered. He bit his lip, one snaggletooth fang peeking out in a manner far too cute for a buff arboreal alien capable of hauling around a giant river monster.
“Rozard,” he said, parroting me near perfectly.
“Good to eat,” I said in my best approximation of his cheeping, growling language. Then repeated in English, “They’re good to eat.”
His head tipped first one way, then the other.
“Tey rr gud to eet,” he repeated. Frowned.
“They are good to eat,” I said, making an effort to enunciate clearly.
He said it in his language, then tried parroting me again. “Tey are good to eat.”
The
are
was more of a rolling growl than an actual word, and he was no where near getting the
th
sound right, but⁠—
“Close enough,” I assured him, and smiled.
He let me use one of his knives to dress my catch myself, then we returned to my campsite, foraging mushrooms and wild veggies along the way. He got the fire going while I skewered the rozard on one stick, and the foraged stuff on another.
I liked sharing meals with him, I decided. He could chatter the day away—and believe me, he had—but he also knew how to appreciate the silence that came with enjoying a meal. The fact he could barely understand me made no difference to him. Or to me.
Zyjii’s friendship was a gift. A rare and precious treasure I’d protect with tooth and nail.
Zyjii showed up the following morning, bringing with him another basket of nuts and fruit, along with a stone knife. I crooned over it, then stuck the sheath in the waistband of my skirt. A skirt I’d already torn up the sides to improve ease of movement.
Zyjii frowned over the placement of the sheath, but I didn’t have a belt. I’d make do until I rigged something better. Maybe with the button-up blouse I no longer bothered with at all because it was too hot.
After breakfast, he took the lead as we headed into the jungle again.
“I want to show you where to find
riiki
nuts,” he explained. “They’re my favorite, and they go good with everything. They’re hard to find, though, since they won’t grow in the tangle of tunjun roots, or too close to the river.”
“Picky,” I said.
‘Tunjun’ were the massive behemoth trees that made up the majority of the jungle’s arboreal flora. They were definitely the biggest. The others were all smaller, and tended to cluster along the relatively open spaces along the river bank.
Zyjii’s favorite riiki nuts grew on trees that occupied small spaces of sunlight where tunjun roots and the canopy overhead remained cleared.
He plucked a couple nuts up from the ground, their spike-covered shells perfectly intact if a little muddy. He cracked one open ate and the pale pink nut meat inside with happy little purring sounds.
He froze when he noticed me watching him, an amused smile twitching at my lips. Giving me a slightly sheepish grin, he held out the other half of the nut to me.
“Tey are good to eat,” he reassured me. In English.
I blinked. Holy shit. He remembered.
“Good to know,” I said.
And that was how I learned Zyjii had the ability to pick up English at a terrifying speed. He only had to repeat a word once to remember it forever, even if he didn’t understand it’s exact meaning at the time.
Instead of taking the shell from him—the thing looked more like a sea urchin than a nut, and I was willing to bet those spines were barbed and probably irritated fragile human skin like thistle—I plucked the nut meat free, gave it a tentative sniff, then a careful bite.
Nutty spice nearly blew my head off. It was a bit like cloves, but with the bite of good, fresh-cracked black pepper, with a hint of earthy paprika thrown in to really round things out.
“Good,” I wheezed. “But
strong
.”
“Good.” He used the English word, then asked in his own tongue, “that means ‘good,’ yes?”
“Yes.”
He stared at me as I pulled a bag of filtered water from my makeshift sling—pack sling not pebble throwing weapon sling—and took a few big gulps. I’d used his word without really thinking about it, since it was one of the few I could actually say correctly.
“You understood me,” he said.
I paused my guzzling—that spice was still attempting to beat the shit out of my tastebuds—and gave him wide eyes.
His ears went through a whole dance, swiveling in opposite directions, perking up, pressing flat back against his skull, before finally settling on a cautiously curious slant.
“You understand me,” he said. “How much? Everything? How?”
I wheezed. Now those were questions I had no way to answer. Not easily.
“I understand,” I said. First in his language, then English. “As for how…”
I shrugged. No way could I explain I had a microchip planted in my brain that learned and translated alien languages. Honestly, the little piece of tech was so far beyond my understanding it still felt like magic to me. To someone like Zyjii who lacked any kind of context for what a microchip was…
Yeah. Not happening.
“But you understand me,” he repeated intently.
I flailed for a moment, then shrugged again, a little helplessly.
“Yes.”
His smile was slow as the rising sun and just as brilliant.
I hadn’t thought it possible, but Zyjii chattered even more now that he knew I knew his language. At least on an aural level. Speaking it was another problem all together, but we made do.
My first impression about him being an intensely curious creature turned out to be well founded. For every thing he pointed out about the jungle, he asked me at least two questions. He didn’t even seem to care that I couldn’t answer them in any understandable manner. Figuring things out for himself delighted him almost more than the few answers I could give.
Every morning, he turned up with a basket of fresh fruit. There was usually one new thing for me to try, along with a few of my favorites. After a week of daily breakfasts, I realized he
never brought his favorites. That didn’t seem fair—I wanted him to enjoy these breakfasts together as much as I did.
So I planned a little surprise for him.
It meant getting up before sunrise and hunting for things I’d only eat as a last resort, but whatever. It was the only thing I could think of to do to repay him for everything he’d done for me.
I had everything laid out and ready for him when he finally swept into my shelter on wings that were more like a bat’s than I’d initially thought. He’d told me he couldn’t actually fly. Not like the many jungle birds—some of which were twice his size and subsisted entirely on river monsters like the one we’d killed. What he did was glide. More like a flying squirrel than a bat.
“Morning,” I said with all the casualness I could muster. In the somewhat messy blend of English and his language—he called his people Mrriiv, but his language had no name—I added, “I got something for you.”
“For me?” he asked.
I carefully lifted the huge leaf I’d used to cover my offering, then whisked it out of the way.
Seeing his eyes go wide and his wings flutter madly behind him made the extra time and effort I put into his breakfast worth it. So worth it.
The insects were as long as my hand from heel to middle-fingertip and vaguely resembled crickets, but with damselfly wings as colorful and patterned as a butterfly’s. They came in jewel toned green, yellow, and a glossy, almost translucent white.
Our second day together, he’d snagged one from the air and sank his sharp teeth into it with the same happy purr he made when he ate riiki nuts. I figured it had to be some kind of special treat. Or maybe just one of his favorite foods.
“For you,” I agreed.
He crept closer, then crouched, reaching out to touch a finger to one of the brightly colored insects like he couldn’t believe it was real.
“I don’t think you understand what this means to me,” he said softly. His tail lashed slowly behind him as he stared another moment before lifting his moss green eyes to mine. “Thank you, Rynn. I’ll enjoy them.”
“Good.” Embarrassed for reasons I couldn’t quite explain, I huffed and busied myself sorting through the breakfast options he’d brought. “Next time, bring some of
your
favorites. Even if it’s something I don’t like.”
There were some words in there he didn’t know yet, but he knew enough to get the gist. His grin was a little sheepish.
“It’s, uh, kind of habit,” he said. “To bring a female her favorite things to eat.”
I scowled. “Do the females ever do the same for males?”
“Yes,” he said simply.
A knot of tension unwound from my shoulders. Good to know it wasn’t a gender specific expectation. I hated those things.
Look,
you
try being a girl raised by a survivalist nut, whose friends were all either hunters, outdoorsmen, or other survivalist nuts. If I had a penny for every time a man tried to tell me to leave a basic task—like starting the campfire, or splitting firewood, or pitching my own damn tent—to the menfolk, I’d be rich.
“Does that mean I should be the one to bring your favorites?” I asked, just to be sure.
He sucked in a startled breath and nearly choked. I passed him one of my water bags, and he tipped the contents into his mouth, guzzling. Only to grimace and curl his lip at the bag.
“Why does your water taste bad?” he demanded.
“The pouch,” I explained.
He sniffed the super incredible biodegradable plastic-like bag and made an exaggerated
blech
face.
“It’s perfectly good water!” I said defensively.
He shoved the water bag at me, and stood. “Today, we’re finding jinveep.”
He tapped one of the gourds hanging from his extra broad belt for emphasis. I grunted, not nearly as miffed as I pretended. Super advanced or not, the plastic bag still made the water taste like plastic, and I’d be happy to use something else.
That didn’t stop me from grumbling.
“It’s good water,” I muttered under my breath.
“Maybe,” he said. His dubious look made me want to laugh. “But water kept in a jinveep tastes better.”
I snorted. No doubt.
Bending over, he plucked up three of the big insects I’d caught for him, tucking them away in a pouch to save for later. Straightening, he held his hand out to me with a charming grin.
“Come on,” he said. “Let’s go find
tasty
water gourds.”

Chapter Seventeen
ZYJII
I heard
the hunter before I saw them, but only because they wanted me to. I was burrowed hip deep in a teechii plant, trying to get at the elusive fruit. My tail, coiled tightly around a vine, was the only thing keeping me from falling head first into the prickly plant. It was
not
a good position to be in when dealing with one of the village’s hunters.
After a moment of consideration, I decided to ignore them. Maybe they’d find my antics so embarrassing they’d go away. Besides, I’d slept through Vobe’s rising and was already late for breakfast with Rynn. If I wasn’t absolutely certain she’d love the teechii berry’s blend of sour skin and sweet insides, I would have already given up on my quest to harvest a couple of the fruit.
“Zyjii.”
My wing-hands twitched. Of all the hunters in the village, this was the only one I couldn’t embarrass by acting like a brainless fool.
“Jeejee,” I said by way of greeting.
“I came to warn you.”
Of course she had. My sister loved me, but she didn’t seek out my company. Not after someone told her I was a bad influence,
and people would start to wonder what trouble
she
might bring if she spent too much time with me.
She wouldn’t tell me who’d said such a stupid thing, but I had my suspicions.
“Is that why Phex has been poking about my den?” I asked.
Because while my habit of finding trouble might rub off on my sister through some mysterious process no one had satisfactorily explained to me, everyone agreed that Phex was a good influence on me. A stabilizing presence. As if just being around her was enough to keep me in line.
I snorted. They didn’t realize that Phex’s nagging had never once successfully stopped me from poking the typvr hive.
“No,” Jeejee said.
The vine I dangled from bobbed as she clung to the others around it, the only place to land close to my somewhat precarious foraging spot.
Teechii grew in thick knots at the tips of tunjun branches. Branches too thin to hold the weight of a grown Mrriiv. It made getting at them tricky, and getting to their sour and sweet berries even trickier.
I finally spotted one of the brilliant orange berries and growled, triumphant. Now I just had to get to the thing, past a veritable wall of bristling, thorny stems.
“Phex has other problems she’s dealing with,” Jeejee continued. “She’s probably trying to find you so she can convince you to tie your heart-threads together, and⁠—”
I shuddered, hard enough to make the teechii bush twitch around me, threatening to close up tight. I went very, very still, hardly daring to breathe.
“Careful there, brother,” Jeejee chided.
That was the other thing that made foraging teechii dangerous. They weren’t hungry plants, not like the yreki, but they did respond to threats by shrinking in on themselves.
Except for the thorns. Those became as long as a spearhead, the barbed tips brittle so they broke easily, leaving festering sores on the unwary.
Slowly, the teechii bush began to relax again, and I let out a long breath.
“So, I need to avoid Phex with more care than usual,” I said. “I somehow doubt that’s the danger you came to warn me about.”
“We got word from Big Tree Village that a mated pair of grrn recently left their territory, headed in our direction.”
I yelped at the news, and my tail just barely managed to haul me up before the teechii bush snapped into a ball a quarter its original size and bristling with barbed spines. Curling, I managed to grab the vine with my working-hands, all the better to stare at my sister.
“Grrn?” I asked. “Are they sure?”
“Tryv went and checked. It looks like they’ve just found a den and started marking their territory.”
“Where?” I demanded. Because between our Grove Village and Big Tree Village was⁠—
“They’re denning in the salt caves,” she said. “Near the spot where the two tunjun giants fell. You know the place. It’s just off the river, and—Zyjii?”
But I was already gone. Because I did know the place. Lynn had made her den in the almost-cave created where one giant rested against the other. A sheltered place, but too open. And far too close to a pair of grrn freshly mated and looking to fill their larder.
She couldn’t fight them. Couldn’t flee them. I’d seen her climb. She was quick enough, considering her lack of a tail for balance and good sharp claws to dig into the tough bark of the tunjun trees, but she wouldn’t be quick enough to escape a grrn.
It wasn’t too late. I’d get to her in time. Grrn were particular about their dens. They’d spend a lot of time getting it just right before they went hunting.
Please

Let me get to her in time

Chapter Eighteen
LYNN
Two weeks
after we smoked the river monster together, I waited for Zyjii to join me for breakfast—as had become our routine—but finally admitted defeat when he hadn’t made an appearance by midmorning.
An aching sense of disappointment settled in, making my chest feel wonky. I ignored it the best I could as I geared up and headed out. I had traps to check. And I wanted to see if I could find some kind of plant alternative to gut string. I understood the process of making gut string—which was long and smelly and took tools I didn’t have at my immediate disposal—but if I could find an alternative, I’d use it.

huge
with long, non-retractable claws. Knowing something that big was in my territory, benefitting from my traps, put my hackles up.
I hoped I never ran into the monster. Those paw tracks? They were bigger than a brown bear’s, and considering my only weapons were a spear and a pebble-hurling sling?
Talk about a nightmare scenario.
Moving more cautiously, all my senses peeled, I hurried to check the rest of my traps. One yielded a scrawny rozard, and I managed to brain another one of the little critters with the butt of my spear when it darted across my path. I tied them to the vine-belt I’d made for myself and decided to call it a day. I’d have to ask Zyjii about the new predator sign I’d spotted.
Assuming I ever saw him again. Maybe he’d sated his curiosity about me and gone off to…wherever. Back to his people, probably.
That tight feeling in my chest intensified. I paused to breathe through it, and then froze. All concerns about loneliness vanished as the silent jungle settled around me. The jungle was
never
quiet, no matter the time of day, and the sudden cessation of all sound made my ears ring.
A howl shivered through the air. It reminded me of wolf-song, only deeper in pitch, rougher in timbre, with an eery, nails-on-chalkboard undertone that sent chills racing down my spine.
“Please don’t be a predator,” I whispered. A futile wish. The jungle’s silence, and those damn scary tracks, were confirmation enough.
Whatever made that shrieking howl, it frightened everything else. Even the insects.
A rustle, snap, and snuffle sounded from a few scant yards in front of me. I briefly closed my eyes. Because of
course
the unknown scary thing would be between me and the relative safety of my shelter.
A second screaming howl sounded behind me, and I twitched despite myself.
I bit my lip to keep from sighing. Why worry about one scary predator when there could be two out there in the jungle with you. Possibly more than two. Wolves and lions hunted in packs. Why not alien super predators?
At least the second one sounded further away. But the first one. The snuffle got louder. My leg muscles quivered with the need to run, my chest burning as I fought to hold in a terrified gasp when it howled again. I held myself perfectly still, except for my fingers tightening their grip on my spear.
Slowly, oh-so-slowly, I slid one foot back, shifting my weight in preparation to
move

Shit.
I whipped my arm around. Something burst from the undergrowth. I had a split second to aim as a gaping mouth full of dozens of rows of jagged teeth filled my vision. I released the vines with my rozard catches. The maw snapped shut around them. They weren’t even a mouthful. Barely a crumb.
It didn’t matter. They gave me the split second distraction I needed.
I bolted, using my spear to whack hanging vines out of my way or vault over dense bramble patches and fallen tree limbs. My bare feet beat the soft mulch of the jungle floor in a pounding drive that matched the panicked throb of my heart and my sawing breaths. The thing behind me let out a scream loud enough my eardrums threatened to rupture. The ringing silence that followed nearly drowned out the sound of it crashing through the jungle behind me.
I dodged to the side, ducking and leaping amongst the exposed roots of one of the huge tunjun trees. A stitch began to knot my side, and I wished I’d done more to stay in shape. I should have taken up parkour. I could have run and bounced around the
Rsp’rin
like a maniac and no one would have batted
an eye, because they would have simply chalked it up as a weird human thing.
No time to regret the year I’d spent idle. Now I had to
run

Chapter Nineteen
ZYJII
Wing-hands spread wide,
I swooped through the trees. I grabbed a vine, used it to whip myself in a different direction, the speed of the movement threatening to tear the thin skin between my wing-fingers. A foot planted on another vine—this one grown thick and brown, all its softness gone—launched me upwards higher,
higher
, so I could make better use of my wing-hands. I sped so fast the forest around me became a blur, and I was still nearly too late.
Rynn screamed. I threw myself in that direction and nearly glided out of the trees and right off the side of a cliff. A vine snagged by my tail saved me at the last second, and I stumbled as my feet landed on a too-narrow branch.
Breathing ragged, I kept a death-grip on the vine and leaned out to see Rynn on her rump on the ground, one foot dangling in thin air. She, too, had nearly fallen off the unexpected cliff. As I watched, she scrambled to her feet, her spear in hand as she spun to face the two grrns. They closed in on her from either side, leaving her nowhere to run.
Baring her teeth, she let out a hunting cry of her own, tossed her spear to shift her grip on it, then
hurled
it into the face of the
grrn nearest her. It snarled and snatched the spear from the air. Two chomps and it was only so much kindling.
The second grrn lunged for her, maw opened wide to snatch her like its partner had snatched the spear.
“No!” I shouted, and leapt from the tree, working-arms pinned to my sides and wing-hands angled to cut through the air.
I was too slow, but Rynn wasn’t. She leapt backward, straight off the side of the cliff, choosing to fall to her death rather than be ripped apart. The grrn swiped out with one massive paw, trying to snag her, but it missed, and Rynn fell.
Teeth gritted and lips peeled back, I adjusted the angle of my fall, trying to fall
faster
. I zipped past the pacing grrns, too quickly for them to react. Rynn kept her body spread wide, as if that could slow her. And it did. Just enough. I dropped below her and banked hard, bleeding off enough momentum I wouldn’t injure her as I caught her in my arms.
She hit me with a startled grunt. I pulled her in tight as I spread my wing-arms wide, muscles and tendons screaming from the strain of trying to slow our lethal plummet.
“Hold on,” I yelled.
She pulled her elbows and knees in close, making herself as small as possible just before we dropped through the canopy. Leaves ripped at the sensitive skin between my wing-fingers, already stretched to the point of tearing. Vines tried to tangle us. I twisted, turned, dipped, and dodged around branches that would smash our bones and pulverize our insides.
My feet slammed into a tunjun’s huge lower branch. I staggered along its length, wing-hands flapping to help bleed off the last of our momentum, tail whipping as I struggled to keep my balance with Rynn’s weight pulling me off kilter.
Finally, I staggered to a halt and sat hard, my whole body shaking as my breaths came in rapid, jagged pants. Rynn
groaned and pushed herself away from my chest. Not far. Just enough for her to blink up at me.
I offered her my most charming grin, though between my too-quick breaths, pinned ears, and slumped shoulders, it lost a lot of its impact. She blinked again, then let out a shaking sigh and closed her eyes, sinking against me. I held her tight, drinking in the living warmth of her, until both our trembling eased.
A shudder shook her small body, and then she started to pull herself away from me, legs shifting beneath her so she could stand. Only for her to cry out in pain and collapse back into my lap, gripping her leg.
Gentle despite the fresh surge of panic beating at my chest, I pulled her hands away long enough to see blood, rich red, and too much of it. Snarling curses, I got my arms around her and stood, striding back to the bole of our tree. I set her down with great care, then started digging through my pouches.
“I have some soak-moss,” I said. “It’ll help stop⁠—”
“Water first,” she said firmly, despite her expression being drawn tight with pain. “Clean it.”
I blinked. That was…not the way we did things, but if that was her people’s wisdom, I wasn’t going to argue against it.
Untying the leather cords holding one of several jinveep gourds to my belt, I wiggled the top free and handed it to her. She took a sip, nodded, then poured the water over the wound. I watched, the tip of my tail twitching with all the helpless frustration I tried not to feel.
“What’s soak-moss?” she asked through gritted teeth, the words barely discernible.
“It helps stop bleeding,” I said. “It’ll help fight off wound rot and blood fever, too.”
She grunted. Clutching the now empty gourd to her chest, she leaned back against the tunjun trunk and closed her eyes tight.
“Good. Can you⁠—”
“I’ll tend your injury to the best of my ability,” I promised her.
Nodding again, she blew out a shaking breath. I sat close, then carefully, oh-so-carefully, lifted the foot of her injured leg to my lap. She grunted, but didn’t otherwise show I’d hurt her.
I hated seeing the ragged, bleeding tears. The grrn must have managed to catch her delicate skin with a couple of its claws when it tried to snatch her from the air.
As soon as I finished, I looked around for something to use as bandages. Not seeing anything immediately useful, I reached for the length of cloth I wore to cover my cock pouch. It was one of the first pieces of cloth I’d woven as a kit. My dame had helped me prepared the plants, spin them into thread, dye the thread, then weave it into cloth. It was a memento, and I hated to ruin it, but Rynn needed it more than I did.
Before I could start the first tear, small but strong hands gripped mine, stopping me.
“No,” Rynn said firmly. She reached for the hem of her skirt. “Use mine.”
I hesitated. “Are you sure?”
She snorted. “It’s already
tourne
to
raags
. Yes, I’m sure.”
For once, I didn’t pester her about the unfamiliar words. We worked in silence as she tore strips from her skirt—shortening its already short length—and I wrapped them around her leg, binding the soak-moss to the wound.
When we finished, I moved to scoop her into my arms, but she tensed. I froze, except for the nervous lashing of my tail.
“May I carry you?” I asked. “I have an old den near here. We’ll be safe there.”
“Yes,” she said. “Sorry.”
She continued in a litany of lilting words. I picked out enough to gather she’d hadn’t tensed because she didn’t want me touching her, but rather bracing against the pain that’d come with any movement.
“Hold on, Rynn,” I said. “We don’t have to travel far.”
When I lifted her, she wrapped her own two thin arms around my shoulders, keeping herself pressed close as I used my wing-fingers, one working-hand, both feet, and occasionally my tail, to climb higher into the tree before leaping into the air and gliding away from the cliffs and the grrns’ new hunting grounds.

Chapter Twenty
ZYJII
I took
Rynn to one of my secret dens. I had any number of them scattered around the village’s hunting grounds. This one was right at the furthest edge, as far from the village as we could get without leaving the territory.
I might be an exile, but I wasn’t ready to give up the last tenuous threads I held to my sire and sister. Not yet.
I cradled Rynn close as I hopped off a branch and used my tail to swing us through the narrow opening in the tunjun trunk.
Another name for the tunjun was ‘den tree.’ This was why. Natural pockets grew into the trunk of the tree, their entrances tucked securely beneath the broad branches. The dens formed up high, but not so high a young Mrriiv kit couldn’t safely reach them on their own.
Not all tunjun grew dens, but some did.
I’d found this particular den tree during my aimless wanderings after my dame returned to the forest. I’d used it for a time. Even then, I’d thought to make a place for myself separate from the village, some part of me knowing without my dame to stand up for me, the others would cast me out sooner or later. But that had been many, many dances ago, and it showed.
The only signs of habitation were the moldering remains of the nest I’d made myself. I sniffed, but the air was clear of animal sign.
Satisfied, I set Rynn down near the entrance, where the air remained freshest. A quick check of the bandages assured me the soak-moss had done its job and stopped the bleeding.
Making the short hop to the nest, I picked up the blanket I’d left behind. The heavy must of decay wafted from it, and my ears pinned back as I curled my lip up in disgust. There’d be no saving it.
I glanced at Rynn, my ears sliding forward slightly in distress. She needed blankets. She needed to stay warm while she slept, and that was especially true with her wounded and weakened. Her body needed to reserve all its strength for mending.
I could make her another blanket. More blankets. All the blankets she wanted. But that would take time and tools I didn’t have here. For the short term, though…
“Wait here,” I said. She looked up at me, her usually bright eyes dulled with pain. “I’m going to fetch food and some other supplies.”
Her lips, once so pink and now nearly colorless, tipped up to one side.
“I’ll wait,” she agreed, followed by a muttered “Not like I can go anywhere at the moment.”
I hesitated, but…
Darting forward, I brushed my knuckles over her cheek in a gesture that was too familiar for whatever we were. A sky-fallen female and a Mrriiv exile. She didn’t protest, though. Didn’t hiss or slap at me. Instead, she tipped her head back and closed her eyes.
“I’ll be fast,” I assured her.
She smiled, trusting me.
I foraged enough food to last a couple days. Long enough to know if she’d get sick from her wounds. If she did…A shiver ran down my spine, puffing the fur on my tail. I had to hope for the best, but prepare for the worst.
Running through a mental list of all the things that might help her, I returned to the branch shielding our den and used my tail to swing inside, my arms and wing-hands full of useful things. I started to smile at Rynn, only to freeze.
The spot where I’d set her was empty. Rynn was gone.
Before I could spin around and throw myself back outside to begin looking for her, a quiet voice said, “Zyjii.”
I jumped hard enough to send the precarious stack of fruits and nuts balanced in my arms scattering. I twisted about to find my sky-fallen sitting toward the back of the den, her injured leg out, the other drawn up with her hands woven around it.
“Sorry. Didn’t mean to startle you,” Rynn said.
My tail twitched side to side in an erratic rhythm. Rynn watch it, mesmerized. She looked okay. That was all that really mattered.
I made quick work of emptying my arms and wing-hands of all the things I’d brought to help make the den more comfortable for her, then set to cleaning. As I did, I stretched my tail out to snag one of the jinveep gourds. I carefully freed the waxy stem from the top and used a claw to pierce the thick, tough skin so air could escape as the liquid inside was poured. Then I set it in her lap.
She accepted the offering. I watched to make sure she sipped and didn’t guzzle. Jinveep was good, and would help her
replenish all the blood she’d lost, but too much too fast could make her sick.
She took a few careful mouthfuls, then held out her hand for the waxy stem. I gave it to her, and she bopped it back into place. Scraping some of the waxy build-up from where I’d cut the stem free of its vine, she used it to seal the air hole I’d created. Just the way I’d showed her. The little plug would harden in a few hours, and then she’d be able to remove and replace it as needed.
She was so smart, my sky-fallen female. I hated seeing her like that, pale and injured and quietly suffering.
I refocused on my task. With her immediate needs met and her wound tended, it was time for me to do what I could to make her more comfortable. For now, I’d make her the warmest nest I could. And tomorrow, I would make the long trip back to my main den to gather all the blankets I’d made.

Chapter Twenty-One
LYNN
My own shivering
woke me sometime in the night.
Sleeping wrapped up in the emergency blanket, I hadn’t noticed before just how crisp the night air got. Not cold, exactly, but…It was a thing of relativity. I had no means of measuring the temperature fluctuations of the jungle, so I had no idea how big the change was. I just knew it got cool enough at night to make me glad to have a blanket, but not so cold I really worried about hypothermia.
Ha. This jungle would never get cold enough for hypothermia to become a concern. No, heatstroke was much more likely.
So while the shivering wasn’t normal, it also wasn’t cause for concern. My skin felt a little chilled to the touch, but that was as far as it went. There was none of that bone-deep ache that frequently accompanied my fevers.
Far more distracting than the shivering was the deep throb of pain in my injured leg.
Sucking in a breath, I reached down, moving as little as possible. I cupped my hand over the leafy bandages Zyjii applied earlier, but everything felt good. It was a little hot, but that was
to be expected. At least the touch didn’t send agony spiking up my leg.
I flopped back with a relieved sigh. No sign of infection meant I probably was just chill from the drop in the nighttime temps.
If I’d been on my own, I probably would have just sucked it up and dealt with the discomfort, resting as best I could. It wouldn’t be the first time. Unfortunately, I wasn’t alone, and my shivering made the leaves of my bed rustle.
One of Zyjii’s ear stuck straight up into the air and swiveled toward me. The tree-cave was dark enough that I wouldn’t have been able to see him at all if the jungle beyond the cave opening wasn’t lit with all kinds of glowing plant-life. That gentle glow created enough backlight for me to just make out the shadowed lump of him.
“Rynn?” he mumbled in a sleepy purr.
“It’s fine,” I whispered. “Go back to sleep.”
He did not, in fact, go back to sleep. Instead, he pushed himself up to his knees—he was a side sleeper and leaned more toward his stomach than his back, which made sense, what with the wings and all—and rubbed at his eyes with one hand, the other clutching his tail like a kid with a teddy bear.
His empty hand dropped into his lap and he blinked sleepily at me. His other ear twitched upward, turning in my direction. He frowned, then focused.
“You’re shivering,” he said.
“Very astute observation,” I grumbled, then winced, immediately feeling bad for snapping at him.
“Of course you’re shivering,” he muttered. “You don’t have any fur. Or wing-hands to wrap around yourself. And I threw out the blanket. I was going to get you more tomorrow, but I should have realized…I
did
realize, so why didn’t I do more?”
His ears slanted back in displeasure. He seemed to notice he still clutched his tail only when the tip started twitching back and forth. Glancing at me, his ears went flat out to the sides in what was basically a blush, and released his tail. When I didn’t say anything, he relaxed, then went back to frowning.
“It’s not really appropriate,” he said slowly, “but considering the situation…” His tail slapped the floor then stayed there while he drew his shoulders up. “I can hold you. Keep you warm.”
“Yes, please,” I said in his language, trying not to sound too needy.
Nodding, he crawled right up next to me, then settled down on his side, facing me. He curled an arm beneath his own head, and accidentally nudged my own with it.
“Sorry,” he murmured.
I grunted and lifted my head in silent invitation, which he took, shifting his arm into a more comfortable position that happened to provide me with a pretty comfy pillow. His other arm he rested across my stomach. All perfectly normal things for two people sleeping together for heat.
Then things got weird.
Instead of tangling our legs together, he draped his tail along the outside of my non-injured leg. It was fluffy and warm, and my muscles immediately went slack, drinking in the heat of him. The wing-hand—as he called it—came next. That was the really weird part. The ‘finger’ bones were long and looked delicate, but they were astonishingly strong. They had to be, to support his weight in the air, even in a glide. The skin stretched between them was furless, and thin enough to see the complex tracery of veins.
All those veins in the thin skin made the wing-hand a very warm blanket.
There was just one thing I worried about.
“Will you get…” I didn’t know how to say his word for cold, so I shivered intentionally.
“Cold?” he asked.
I nodded and mouthed the word, but it had one of those cheeping growling purring sounds I couldn’t replicate no matter how hard I tried.
So I said the English word. “Cold.”
And then realized it had an ‘L,’ one of the sounds he struggled with.
He grumbled a bit when he realized the same thing, but Zyjii wasn’t one to hold on to frustrations. He shrugged it off and snuggled closer to my side.
“I might get a little cool if I stretched my wing-hand across the ground,” he mumbled, sounding half-asleep already. “But wrapped around you, I’ll stay warm.”
That was good, because the heat he radiated felt absolutely sublime. And the fact he’d offered to hold me, just because I was shivering? I pressed a fist to my chest, over the heated spark that formed there.
His kind consideration threatened to undo me. He’d been like that right from the start. Helping me out. Giving me the tools and knowledge I needed to better survive, without making me feel lesser. Or stupid. Or frustrated because I knew perfectly well how to handle a spear and didn’t need some buff asshat mansplaining the basics of hunting to me.
Zyjii didn’t mansplain. He just gave me the damn spear and trusted me to figure out what to do with it.
I hated to say it, but he was a bit like my dad that way.
No, I did not have a Daddy complex. Actually, I was pretty sure I’d end up committing homicide if I ever married someone just like my dad. But it was thanks to him that I realized at a very young age that I’d never, ever stay with someone who felt the need to explain things I knew how to do better than him. Like
starting a campfire without using an entire fucking jug of lighter fluid.
Grumbling at the faded memory of my last—and really only—boyfriend, I snuggled closer to Zyjii’s solid warmth. My shivering eased, my eyelids grew heavy, and the throbbing pain in my leg faded to ignorable levels.
I had almost reached the land of dreams when Zyjii bolted upright into a sitting position.
I tensed, reaching for a weapon that wasn’t there. Never had I missed Mr McStaberson so badly since coming to this damn planet.
“Zyjii. What⁠—”
“Your things,” he said. “They’re strange and unusual, and if the village hunters find them, they’ll hunt for you.”
I frowned up at him in the darkness. I would have questioned whether that was really cause for concern, considering he’d been so open and accepting of me, despite my alienness, but the pained slant of his ears told me I’d maybe lucked out when Zyjii found me first.
“They won’t accept you,” he said, confirming my suspicions. He growled, half-frustrated, half-aggrieved. “They’re cautious, and will see you as a threat. I would try to explain but…” His ears drooped and he gave me a miserable expression. “I’ve caused them too much trouble for them to trust me when I say something is safe.”
It clearly tore him up, that his people might see me as a threat. I gripped the ‘thumb’ of his wing-hand that remained partially draped over me. He twitched in surprise, his tail lashing around him. It took him a bit of effort, but his ears lifted into a more relaxed pose, and he managed to smile at me.
“I need to go get your things,” he said. “Do what I can to hide your tracks.”
Meaning I’d be here in the tree-cave alone in the dark, without his warmth or a weapon.
I took a breath. I’d been in worse situations before.
“I’ll still be here,” I assured him.
The whip-like flick of his tail slowed into a more contented curl. Nodding, he got up and started shifting things around. Not that there was much in there with us.
“I’ll put these here,” he said, moving a leaf piled with fruits and nuts to the spot on the floor next to the head of my bed. “If your stomach starts to feel uneasy, drink the jinveep. It’s refreshing and nourishing, and helps sooth stomach upsets.”
Like ginger or mint, I thought. Good to know.
I was already mentally bracing myself to deal with the slight chill for the rest of the night when he set something on the mat next to my head. I glanced up at him, arrested by his unusually solemn expression.
“A knife,” he explained. “Just in case animals are attracted to the food scent. It’s not usually wise to keep food in the same den you sleep in, but…”
But I’d been injured and our current den was an emergency shelter. A thing of temporary necessity.
I reached up and grabbed the sheathed knife, then rested it on my stomach. I nodded at him.
“I’ll hurry,” he promised. “Fast as I can.”
“Be safe,” I murmured. But he was already gone.

Chapter Twenty-Two
ZYJII
I managed
to retrieve Rynn’s things—and disguise the most visible signs of her presence in the forest, without drawing the grrns’ attention—and make it to my main den unseen.
I pulled down one of the large baskets I’d used to carry my possessions when I first left the village, and carefully set all her things in the bottom. Despite the increasing worry about being away from her so long, I took a moment to consider what else I should take.
My tools and weapons were a must. My collection of shard rocks, used to create knives and spear heads, also needed to go with me. I could probably find more but that could take weeks of searching, and I wanted to make Rynn her own knife. Another spear, too.
I moved to the entrance, pulling back the woven cover so I could stare at the tunjun across the way. It had the dens where I kept my foodstuffs and the hrien I used for weaving. I’d take the dried meat, I decided, since Rynn wouldn’t be up for hunting until her leg healed. The hrien…
The half dozen bundles stowed neatly away represented weeks of work. If I left them, it would take weeks more to begin
weaving things for our den. I bobbed my ear, decided. I’d tie as many of them to the outside of the basket as I could carry without upsetting my balance.
With that settled, I let the cover drop and started packing. I used my favorite blanket to cushion and protect Rynn’s things, which had the added benefit of hiding them from casual perusal. A good thing, too. I’d barely finished tucking the blanket in when the scrape of a claw on tunjun bark announced a visitor, and Phex's scent proceeded her into my den.
“You didn’t request entry,” I said without looking at her as I weighed a pair of clay pots in my hands. Such was a privilege reserved for family and mates. Phex was neither.
“I didn’t actually expect to find you here,” she said.
I tensed. Anger had my wing-arms straightening, the long finger bones vibrating with fury. I whirled on her, ears pinned and fangs bared.
“You’ve been entering my den without permission? Invading my privacy when I’m not here?” I demanded.
She waved that away with a negligent flick of her tail. “This is the first time I’ve entered,” she said, as if that made all the difference. “I came to check on you. What with the grrn roaming about your usual hunting grounds.”
I pulled my wing-arms in tight and gave her my back to start throwing things into the basket with far less care than before. I shouldn’t have come. I could have remade all my tools. All the cloth and leather I’d stockpiled. All the clay pots holding the various dyes I’d concocted myself through trial and error. I could have made do.
But Rynn, my strange little sky-fallen, deserved better.
“As you can see, I’m fine,” I said tightly. “Leave.”
Phex did no such thing, instead choosing to ignore me—as she often did—to come poke at the contents of my basket.
“Going somewhere?” she asked.
“I’m moving dens,” I said. “Even I’m not stupid enough to wander where a mated pair of grrn are filling their larder. I’ve been studying ee vynla. I’ll just find a patch somewhere on the far side of the village. One too far for the village’s weavers to make use of.”
“You could do your…experiments in the village,” she said. “Then you wouldn’t have to live so far out, and⁠—”
She reached into the basket to grab something. I didn’t even care what. With a snarl, I used my tail to knock her hand away. When she glared at me, I pinned my ears and bared my teeth.
“You presume too much, Phex,” I said. “I have not welcomed you into my den. My things are
not
yours to handle as you please.
Leave
.”
She scowled, but let it shift into a sultry pout. “So welcome me, Zyjii. Tie your knot to mine and⁠—”
“Why,” I asked with all the bitter scorn I could muster, “would I want to tie myself to a female who does not respect our people’s ways?”
She hissed at me. “Clearly you don’t respect them either, or you never would have ended up an exile.”
I stared at her. “Is that truly what you think?”
She flicked an ear at me, a silent, resounding
yes
.
“Even if it were true, that doesn’t give
you
the right to barge in here uninvited,” I said. “I’ve told you to leave twice now, Phex. Please don’t make me ask a third time.”
She scoffed. “Or what?”
I met her gaze and let her see all my anger. I let her see the grim determination that had given me the strength and courage to leave the safety of the village. To not only survive on my own despite the dangers of the forest, but to
thrive
. If she did not leave, I would make her. It was as simple as that.
Her ears swiveled back at a nervous slant. She caught herself quickly, but I’d seen.
“Fine,” she said. “Find me once you’ve settled in your new den. We’ll speak of this again.”
I said nothing. Even if I hadn’t lied about my intentions, I wouldn’t seek her out, and she knew it. There was nothing more to say between us. I would not give her what she wanted just because she wanted it.
She curled her working hands into fists and stalked to the entrance, tail stiff and wing-hands spread enough for the fingers to shake.
She stopped, one hand on the wall, and looked back at me over her shoulder. “One day, Zyjii, you’ll see how wrong you are and come begging to me to accept you as my mate. They’d accept you back if you bound your heart-thread to someone the elders respect. Someone they think is strong enough to keep you in line.”
“If they think anyone is strong enough to force me to be other than what I am, then they are fools,” I said.
And so are you
.
I wasn’t quite brave enough to speak the words out loud, but from the snapping lash of her tail, I suspected she heard them anyway. With a hiss and a furious shake of her wing-fingers, she leapt from my den and vanished into the forest.
Vobe was well on his way to his daily rest by the time I returned to the den I now shared with my sky-fallen.
Phex had, predictably, attempted to follow me instead of leaving well enough alone, so I’d led her on a merry chase straight into a lyvchiss cluster. Also known as the sleeping flower. Hunters were taught to avoid them, because they sent anyone who breathed their alluring aroma into a deep sleep. And
kept them there as the denizens who lived amongst the tangled vines slowly ate them alive.
I’d worked out that it wasn’t the scent of the flowers that posed the danger, but breathing in the pollen. It was a knowledge I’d tried to share with the hunters, but they refused to listen. They trusted the learning tales, which had never gotten anyone in trouble. Quite unlike some of the things I’d discovered.
So I knew to hold my breath as I dove through the narrow tunnel the lyvchiss vines created, but Phex didn’t, and she stupidly decided to follow despite knowing the dangers.
I’d left her snoozing in an unused tunjun hollow, drugged into unconsciousness by the flower’s pollen. She’d wake in a day or two with a parched throat and aching head, but was otherwise unharmed.
I glided through the entrance of the den where I’d left Rynn, landing neatly right on the edge, only for something to come flying straight at my head. I jerked to the side with a startled cheep, the basket pulling me off balance. My tail and one wing-hand snapped out, bracing against the den’s wall to either side of the opening behind me, saving me from a backward tumble into thin air.
“Sorry!” Rynn cried. She sat on her nest, eyes wide and one hand pressed to her chest, as if her heart hurt. Her abashed look rapidly changed into a scowl. “You scared
the
shit out of me.”
I gave her a sheepish grin. “I’m sorry. I should have announced myself before swooping in like that. I was worried about being gone for so long. But look!”
I heaved the basket off my shoulder and bounded across the small space to her, tilting the basket so she could see⁠—
The blanket I’d used to keep things from toppling out while I made my way through the forest.
“Right, not that. Just a moment.” I quickly undid the knots holding the blanket in place, then whipped the fabric to the side. “There! See? I brought all the things we’ll need to make this a proper den. And you can’t see them yet, but your things are tucked in the bottom.”
I started pulling items out, carefully setting aside the pieces of my loom—inherited from my dame—and other more fragile tools. The cloth and other, less important things I tossed without caution or care, and soon the floor of the den was strewn with everything I’d thought worth bringing into exile. Deeper exile. Further exile? I flicked the thought away with one ear. It didn’t matter. I knew from experience that these were the things I—
we
—would need most.
Finally, I yanked out the last blanket, beaming as I tipped the basket to dump Rynn’s possessions into her lap. She blinked down at them, then looked up at me with wide eyes that glistened oddly for a moment before a tear trailed down her cheek.
My own eyes went wide. I’d made her cry? But I thought having her things would make her happy, not sad. I⁠—
“Thank you, Zyjii,” she said in a low rasp that made me worry she’d fallen ill while I was gone.
My wing-hands fluttered behind me as I started to reach for her, only to stop myself before I touched her, my tail quivering.
“Are you well?” I finally asked hesitantly.
Laughing, a sweetly musical sound, even if it warbled a little around the edges, she caught my hands and squeezed them.
“I’m fine. I know you said you were getting my things, so I don’t know why I’m…It’s just…” She quickly rubbed her hands over her eyes, stopping the tears that had gathered there from spilling over. “When you’ve lost everything, what remains becomes precious.”
My heart wrenched. I knew that lesson very well.
She caught my working-hands again and held them as she met my gaze.
“So,
thank you
, Zyjii. For bringing back my things.”
I wanted to lean in and nuzzle away the traces of sadness lingering in her eyes. Wanted to hold her and promise her I’d give her all the things she wanted. All she had to do was name them.
Instead, I returned the squeeze of her hands and smiled at her. “You’re welcome.”
Releasing me, she briskly wiped her cheeks, then straightened and looked about our den.
“So,” she said, and gestured at the mess. “What’s all this?”
