Chapter Twenty-Three
LYNN
After a quick meal
of fruits and nuts and some smoked river monster—taken from Zyjii’s stores because I’d been pretty close to out before the whole jungle shark-cat incident—he began sorting through the mess of items on the floor. Some were familiar.
I didn’t know a whole lot about weaving, but the lady on the property next to Dad’s raised a couple sheep and alpaca to supply raw wool for her little spinning business. Since Dad more or less trusted her, I’d spent a lot of time at her place as a kid. So I recognized what might be the primitive jungle version of carding combs, a number of spindles—both drop and supported—and a bunch of wood pieces that probably assembled into a weaving frame of some kind.
Zyjii talked as he worked, explaining what each item was and what it did. The slew of new vocabulary gave my translator a fit for a couple minutes until it did its magic and began to predict a word’s meaning before my mind made the association between the word and the object.
“My dame was a weaver,” he explained. “She always hoped I would take up her trade, since I liked it more than hunting—” he
glanced at me and flicked an ear in what I read as a dismissive gesture “—my sire is a hunter.”
I nodded. He had to choose between which part of the family business he’d follow. That was easy enough to understand.
“I liked weaving more than hunting,” he said, returning to his explanation. “Though hunting would have given me a better excuse to spend a lot of time in the forest, and I was good at it.” His gaze went distant in thought—or maybe lost in memory—before he gave his head a shake hard enough to make his ears flap. “Anyway, I decided to become a weaver, which suited my sister fine, since she was more interested in hunting with our sire. So, I got my dame’s things.”
He picked up a length of carefully carved wood and ran a reverent finger down its length.
“This is a part of her loom,” he said softly.
The wealth of old grief in those four words threatened to break my heart. I recognized loss when I heard it, no translation needed.
I braced myself against the pain in my bum leg and leaned over enough to settle a hand on his arm. His wings twitched and the tip of his tail curled, then began flicking back and forth. He glanced at me, smiled, and started assembling the loom.
“With this, I can make this a comfortable den for you,” he said. His tail froze mid-flick, and his ears drooped to what I could only read as a shy bent. An impression enhanced when his shoulders hunched up and he darted another glance at me. “For
us
, if you want.”
I had no idea what his people’s customs were around co-habitation. I also didn’t much care. I’d always struggled with loneliness. Ever since I was a little girl with a bowie knife as her only constant friend.
Here and now, Zyjii was my sole link to another sapient being. Without him…
I blew out a slow breath. I’d seen that Tom Hanks movie. The one where he ended up stuck on a remote island and goes a little crazy from the isolation. It was one of my dad’s favorite movies.
I did not want to end up with a pet rock as my only source of companionship.
More than that, though, I liked Zyjii. Liked how he viewed his hellscape jungle with excitement and wonder, despite growing up surrounded by it. Liked his easy chatter. Liked the way he made me feel seen. Understood. Accepted.
So I patted Zyjii’s arm, trying not to get distracted by the softness of his short fur.
“Yes,” I said, using his words so there’d be no confusion. “Our den.”
His smile was brighter than stepping into the sunlight after spending days spelunking without a headlamp.
I dozed a lot those first couple days. It never ceased to amaze me how exhausting healing from a major injury could be.
Zyjii came up with all kinds of things to help me. He brewed tea—somewhere outside, since lighting fire
inside
a tree was a downright stupid thing to do—that helped ease the pain. He put together all kinds of fruit salads and nut mashes for me to eat. He made sure I always had two of the drinking gourds. One with water, the other full of the gourd’s natural juice.
The real surprise was when he came in late one afternoon and hefted a third gourd, beaming with obvious pride.
“Yreki nectar,” he declared.
His fur looked a little more rumpled than usual, and there was a tooth-like thorn caught in the draping folds of his
loincloth, but he looked so pleased with himself I decided not to question him too hard about what a ‘yreki’ was.
I didn’t have to ask why he wanted the nectar. Zyjii, I’d come to learn, had boundless amounts of knowledge about his jungle home, and he was eager to share all of it with me.
“This nectar is special,” he said. “Not just to the yreki—though I’m pretty sure it’s what keeps them from eating each other most of the time—but because of what it can do.”
Eating each other…Huh. Wondered if that was the name for the ambulatory carnivorous plants that had tongues that looked like human spines and tended to rip each other to shreds when disturbed.
“And what’s that?” I asked.
He grinned, tail waving happily behind him. “It heals.”
I blinked. He hopped over to me and crouched down next to my wounded leg. He glanced at me, silently seeking permission, as he always did, and I nodded at him. He reverently set the gourd on the mat next to me, bracing it against my hip to keep it upright. When I picked it up so it wouldn’t accidentally tip over if I moved wrong, he smiled at me. Then he got to work removing the leafy bandages and wads of moss packed into the ragged tears across my shin.
Had to admit, the wounds looked better. Inflammation tended to peak within the first three days, and we were past that. My whole leg still ached, but usually only if I moved my foot, and it was more a sharp stab of pain quickly gone than the bone deep throb it had been.
“You were much smarter in your handling of the yreki than I was as a kit,” Zyjii chattered as he worked. “I didn’t seek safety in distance. I walked right up to them.” He paused long enough to flash me his disarming grin. “I was much younger then, and a lot more stupid.”
I kept my expression steely through sheer force of will. He didn’t seem to mind, his tail curling lazily in the air behind him.
“At least that time, my stupidity led to something good,” he said, and gently plucked the gourd from my hands. “I wanted to know how they moved. How to
make
them move. I eventually grabbed one of the tongues, and⁠—”
I sucked in a breath, because I’d seen. It was horrifically easy to imagine a smaller version of Zyjii with big, bright, curious eyes getting grabbed by one of those wicked carnivorous plants.
“Yes, you understand,” he said, bobbing one ear sagely. “I managed to fight my way free before the whole patch roused. During the fighting, some of the yreki’s nectar got smeared on my fur. Including over one of the cuts its teeth made.”
He brushed a finger over his forearm, eyes distant with memory.
“It healed far faster than all the other cuts,” he said. “I asked my sire about it, because he was a hunter and he knew
so much
about the forest, but he’d never heard of yreki having healing properties. He told me I must be mistaken. That if such a thing were true, wouldn’t the village elders know about it? Wouldn’t it be included in the learning tales?”
Zyjii fell silent, his trail drooping to lie limp on the floor behind him. Guilt, I’d learned during the year I spent working amongst aliens, was not a universal emotion. Vrigrix, for example. They understood the concept of guilt, but either lacked the emotional core necessary to experience it, or they simply never acted in a way that might burden them later.
Vrigrix weren’t real big on regret either, now that I thought about it.
I, however, was well acquainted with guilt, so I knew it when I saw it. Even in a being with additional bat hands, a ridiculously long tail, and adorably expressive long ears.
“I wanted him to believe me,” Zyjii said. That long tail all but coiled itself into a knot with the guilt in his voice. “I tried so hard. I went back, got more nectar, but it was like he’d shut his ears to me. I, uh…”
He fell silent, clearly conflicted about whatever memory he was reliving. Blowing out a long breath, he ran a hand over his head to the tips of his ears, which he clenched together in a fist.
“The hunter learning tales warn about yreki stampedes, but I’d never seen one. I didn’t know what caused them, or how to get the nectar without yanking on their tongues.”
The results of that equation—young boy desperate to make his father understand him, a secret he tried to share, and stampeding yreki—was glaringly, painfully obvious.
“Was anyone hurt?” I asked.
He shook his head. “When I realized they were following me—probably because I had their nectar smeared all over me, so I smelled like one of them—I veered away from the village. Another hunter spotted me, snatched me up, and carried me faster than I could move through the forest on my own. My sire was understandably furious, but it was my dame’s frantic touch as she checked me for hurts that I remember most.”
Straightening out of his hunch, he tipped back, bracing himself on his palms and staring up at the ceiling of our tree-cave.
“I wish I could say that was the last trouble I caused for the village, but…I was good, while my dame was there. I didn’t want to scare her like that again. After she returned to the forest, I became more reckless. More and more. I argued with the elders. Told the lead hunter to her face that she was stupid for not trying to better understand the forest’s mysteries. I caused enough trouble the elders deemed me a risk to the village and cast me out.”
“They never heard you,” I said.
“No,” he agreed.
He finally looked at me and smiled, a sweet, warm thing that hit me in the stomach like a field of butterflies taking flight.
“And good thing, too,” he said. “Now I am here with you. And because I was a reckless kit who grew into an only slightly less reckless adult, I have yreki nectar to make your wounds heal faster. The elders can tell me I’m wrong all they want. Words won’t change the shape of the trees.”
“Good thing,” I agreed softly, and meant it with all my heart.
OceanofPDF.com

Chapter Twenty-Four
ZYJII
It pleased
me to see how quickly Rynn healed with the help of the yreki nectar. Within another couple days, she was up and moving about the den. She walked with a pronounced limp, but she assured me the pain was mostly gone.
Not only did she insist on walking around the den several times a day, but she also contorted her body in strange ways. She mostly did it sitting, but there were some things she did while standing—like bending down to press her hands flat on the floor while keeping her legs straight.
“Stretching,” she explained.
It was meant to help with her healing. Help her regain strength in her injured leg.
I began to suspect it also helped her keep from going den mad. I’d seen it happen to many hunters during the wet season, when rain poured from the sky in such quantities it made traversing the forest even more dangerous than usual. Also pointless, because all the inhabitants took shelter, so hunting was poor, and foraging was a wasted effort because even the plants withdrew their leaves and stopped growing.
I recognized the signs, and so I kept a careful eye on Rynn. I did my best to keep her busy, giving her shard-stone to chip into replacement spearheads, and cured leather for her to fashion herself a belt to carry all the things a hunter needed to keep with them. It worked for a while, but not long enough for her leg to heal completely.
The day she remained lying listlessly on her nest, unable to work up the will to do her strengthening exercises, I knew it was time to intervene.
“There’s something I want to show you,” I told her. “It’s a ways away, so I’ll have to carry you, but⁠—”
She sat bolt upright, a fresh surge of life bringing color back to her pale cheeks.
“You’ll take me out?” she demanded.
I managed to keep a smile off my lips, but the lazy curling of my tail probably gave my amusement away.
“Yes,” I said.
She hopped up at once, limping as she headed to the entrance. She stopped upon reaching it, glancing at me over her shoulder.
“Well?” she demanded. “Are you coming or not?”
Since I needed my working hands free for climbing, and wing-hands free to balance and catch the air if we fell, Rynn ended up clinging to my front like a little vriiryn kit. The commonalities between their names was not lost on me, and I decided I needed to find a colony of the small but fierce predators so I could show them to her. From a safe distance, of course.
I kept one hand braced around her whenever I could, but when I couldn’t, her legs tightened their grip on me, pressing her
into me until it became a struggle to keep my cock in its pouch where it belonged. The last thing I wanted to do was extrude without her express request. Just the thought of it made me want to die of mortification.
The press of her against me, the squeeze of her legs around my hips, the feel of her fingers digging into the thicker mane that ran down the nape of my neck and along my spine all the way to my tail, would have been easier to ignore if it weren’t all accompanied by a hint of spicy musk. I’d never scented a sky-fallen’s arousal before, but there was no mistaking it.
She kept her attention turned outward, intent on ignoring the heated tension brewing between us, so I did my best to ignore it, too. I kept our minds busy by pointing out the things I thought might interest her. Pretty flowers, edible fruits, insects that were masters at hiding in plain sight.
“Oh, look,” I said.
I dropped to the branch below us and strode toward the wide bole of the tree. Rynn twisted as much as she could without releasing me to see what had caught my interest. After a quick look, she decided the branch was wide enough for her to stand on safely and dropped her legs. I released her reluctantly.
To distract myself from the strange feeling of missing her, even though she stood right in front of me, I stepped close to the bole of the tree and ran the pad of one finger over a patch of lacy moss, pale green and spotted at the edges with tiny purple flowers.
“This is soak-moss,” I told her. “It’s what I used to treat your wounds. Let me show you the proper way to harvest it.”
She stepped close to me, bracing one hand on my shoulder as she leaned in to get a better look.
“Show me,” she said.
I crouched—carefully, so as not to disrupt her balance—and began harvesting the moss, avoiding the darker green patches
spotted with flowers. I used the claw of one finger to carefully scrape only the palest green of the lacy plant away from the trunk and tucked each chunk away in one of the pouches hanging from my wide belt.
“You usually only see it in the higher branches,” I explained. “Where the rains linger in the air, so thick it covers the world like fine fabric.”
“Sounds like
myst
or
fahg
,” she said. She glanced at my ears—standing straight up with eager interest—and huffed a soft laugh. “
Myst
is the water in the air that comes from rivers falling.”
“Mist?” I suggested.
She grunted her agreement, leaning more of her weight against me. “
Fahg
is…mist but thicker.”
“Fog.”
“How about
klawds?
White things high up in the sky.”
“You mean clouds?”
She glanced at me, amusement in the tilt of her eyebrows. “If you had words for it, why didn’t you use them?”
My tail lashed a moment before I gave up and let it curl around her uninjured leg.
“Well, they’re not very poetic, are they?” I teased.
She snorted at me, but pointed at the soak-moss, returning my attention to the learning tale at hand.
“So that grows in areas where there’s lots of water in the air.”
Smiling, I settled into sharing my learning tale with her, content now that the excited light had returned to her eyes.

Chapter Twenty-Five
LYNN
I’d needed a day out.
I didn’t do well being cooped up inside all day with little to do. I’d survived working on spaceships because the ships used by the ISSC’s ambassadors tended to be absolutely massive, with several gardens, swimming pools, gyms, shooting ranges, and a whole assortment of other recreational offerings to keep people from going bug nuts with cabin fever.
Zyjii had done his best to give me things to do, but I’d needed out of the tree-cave something fierce, and he’d provided before I broke down and begged.
Now, he kept one arm around me as he carried me through the trees. Possibly because I was tired enough I had a hard time keeping a solid grip on him. My limbs kept going all loose and limp as I fought against the pull of sleep. Entranced by his excitement and as eager to learn about his jungle home as he was to share his knowledge about it, I’d pushed myself too hard. Only the hot throb of my leg kept me conscious.
Yet he didn’t take me back to the den. Instead, he seemed to be searching for something, cheeping happily when he finally found it. He climbed to a spot where the branches of several
trees intersected at nearly the same height. The tangle of smaller limbs and heavy waxy leaves created a platform of sorts that housed, I realized as I took in the delicate looking plants, a small field.
“Here’s what I wanted to show you,” he said.
I started to unwrap my legs from around him, but he made a sharp sound, freezing me in place just long enough for him to sit. With me still wrapped around him, clinging like a limpet. It left me in a rather, uh,
compromising
position. Not that he seemed to notice or care.
“No more using that leg,” he said firmly. “Since we have to wait for full dark, we will rest here.”
Disentangling my arms from around his neck, I shifted myself off him and around so I sat beside him. He immediately picked up my bum leg and checked the bandages, swooping down to do a sniff test. Satisfied, he began absently kneading my calf muscle.
I bit my lip to stifle a moan. God, that felt absolutely
divine
.
“Those,” he said, using one skeletal wing-finger to point at the floating field of flowers, “are ee vynla.”
I obediently shifted my focus to the flowers. Anything to get my mind off his long, strong fingers and what they were doing to me.
“We use them for dye,” he explained. “Mostly, the village weavers only gather them when Vyneera dances alone in the sky.”
It took me a second to translate that. I found it weird that Zyjii used names for the sun and moons and talked about them as if they were actual people and not celestial bodies. Yet he didn’t act like they were gods to be revered, so it made it a little disorienting whenever he brought them up so casually.
Vyneera, I recalled, was the smallest of the three moons. The ‘dance’ was basically the moons’ phases. So ‘when Vyneera
dances alone’ meant when the smallest moon was the only one visible at night.
“Some weavers will harvest during Meerya’s or Pheena’s solo dances,” Zyjii continued, oblivious to my momentary brain blip. He flicked an ear toward me without taking his eyes off the field of flowers. “For the different colors the Sisters coax from the flowers.”
I nodded. A majority of the glowing flora seemed to have their bioluminescence tied to the phases of the moons, rather than simply day or night like the plants on Earth.
“Once my dame took me under her wing-hand and began teaching me the weavers’ learning tales, I figured out pretty quickly that the ee vynla bloom every night, no matter where the Sisters were in their dance. But the specific step in the dance produces different colors.”
He leaned back, gaze pensive as he stared at the flower patch without really seeing it. “I found it interesting, and wanted to see how many colors the flowers could produce, so I began to collect and craft dyes from each step in the Sisters’ dance. It took a long time.”
With three overlapping moon phases? I just bet.
He fell silent, his gaze shifting to something far away. Whatever visions of the past he saw made his ears droop and his tail tuck in close to his body.
I hated that. Hated watching him shrink into himself, as if he needed to make himself smaller to fit the world around him. He didn’t. He was big, his emotions loud, his exuberance a massive thing that should never be contained.
Before I realized what I intended, I reached out and stroked his arm. A comforting caress that brought him back to me.

Still, I knew how to conceal my trail until even my dad struggled to find me, and I planned to put all my skills and knowledge to use. There was no way I wanted to spend the rest of my life hunted by scared Mrriivs led by a resentful Phex.
“There are lands beyond the jungle,” Zyjii told me as we walked along the first of many massive branches.
He stayed behind me, ready to catch me if my balance wavered. Our journey started in the trees, but it wouldn’t stay there.
“Like the mountains,” I said, remembering.
“Yes, but even beyond those. The mountain city was filled with people from all over, and I begged to hear every traveling tale from everyone we came across.”
I smiled, because of course he had. It was all too easy to imagine a young Zyjii unleashed upon a city filled with new and interesting things, with people from places he never could have dreamed existed. Free from the smothering disapproval of his village for the first time in his young life, he’d have attempted to learn everything he could all at once, drowning himself in every new experience he came across.
“There are places called deserts,” he said. “They have few trees, and those trees are small. Stunted. Barely bigger than you.”
“The place I come from has deserts, too,” I said.
He crept up closer behind me. I glanced over my shoulder to see his ears straight up and quivering with barely restrained eagerness. I smothered a laugh. I knew what he was going to say before he even opened his mouth.
“Have you seen them? What are they like?”
So as we traveled along branches as big around as giant sequoias in a jungle on a planet far, far from Earth, I told him stories about the Grand Canyon, Arches National Park, and all the other incredible desert wonders in the mountain west my father had taken me to see. Zyjii’s open awe made me wish, for one aching moment, that I could take him away from his planet and back to mine. So he could all the strange and beautiful places for himself.
Being surrounded by glowing flora sure made night hiking easier. It also introduced a whole new slew of dangers for us to avoid—there were a lot of plants that were safe to touch during the day, but became hazardous once they started emitting their
own light—so Zyjii and I decided to bed down and continue traveling in the morning.
We found shelter in the hollow of a fallen tree. There were signs of animal habitation, but they were old. I didn’t think we’d have to worry about fighting off any residents upset at finding intruders in their home, and Zyjii concurred.
After a quick meal of dried meat and fist-sized berries from a nearby plant that partially sheltered our hollow, I laid out the emergency blanket I’d taken from the pod so long ago, and we bedded down together.
Sleep refused to come, though. Mostly because Zyjii was clearly still keyed up and twitchy.
“Do you want to take turns keeping watch?” I asked.
“No,” he said. “I just…I’m sorry, Lynn.”
I frowned into his furry chest. “What? Why? You haven’t done anything wrong.”
“I lied to Phex.”
Ah. So he
did
know how to lie. I’d wondered. Then what he said really registered. I propped myself up on one elbow to look down at him. His ears hung at an unhappy angle, and he refused to meet my gaze. Whatever his lie was, it clearly upset him.
I replayed the whole incident with the bitch female in my mind, but couldn’t remember him saying anything that struck me as unusual or wrong.
“Do you want to tell me what you lied about?” I asked.
“I told her I tied my heart-thread to yours.” He curled in on himself, even his tail coiling up tight. Trying to make himself look smaller. Trying to make himself a smaller target as if he expected me to lash out in a rage which…okay, frankly that hurt a bit. “I haven’t yet, though.”
I blinked.
“Oh.” I laid back down beside him, quiet as I turned that over in my head. “Do you not want to be mates with me?”
That
provoked a reaction.
He shot up to a seated position, twisting around to stare down at me with a fierce expression.
“We
are
mates,” he growled. “You’re mine. And I’m yours.”
The hard knot of hurt that’d lodged in my chest eased. At least enough for me to breathe again.
“Then why not tie our heart-threads together?” I asked.
The hurt leaked into the words despite my best efforts, and Zyjii heard it. With a little pained whine, he curled around me, holding me.
“Because I didn’t want you bound here,” he whispered. “For just this reason.”
“Phex and your village, you mean.”
He nodded, his grip on me tightening. “You deserve better than to spend your life being hunted. I thought…maybe it would be best to leave our mating untied, leave you untethered, so you could return to the sky-lights if we were discovered. I’d rather you be safe and far out of my reach, than beside me and in danger.”
I sucked in a deep breath in an attempt to still my churning emotions. It didn’t work. Worse, it shook. Eyes shut tight against the imminent threat of
feelings
, I slid my arms around his head and held him hard.
“I can’t go back,” I admitted. “Not on my own. The people I traveled with, we were attacked. I don’t think anyone else survived.”
“Lynn,” he rasped, his sorrow rich and deep.
“Even if others survived, they won’t know where to look for me.” My arms tightened of their own accord, fiercely protective. And possessive. “I’ve already decided that if they come for me, I won’t go back. My life is with you, Zyjii. I don’t care where we go, or if we spend our whole lives running, as long as we’re together.”
I took a breath and said three words I’d never really had cause to say to anyone. Not since I was a little girl, when I could freely express my emotions because I was too young to notice my poor dad’s extreme embarrassment.
“I love you, Zyjii.”
He shuddered once, hard enough to rattle my bones, before he began to relax by degrees. The rough pad of his thumb stroked over my cheek in a gentle caress.
“And I you,” he whispered, voice low and husky and sexy as all fuck.
“Will you tie our heart-threads together?” I asked once he’d gone loose limbed and heavy above me. “I’d do it, if I knew how.”
He was silent a moment. I thought I felt a tug somewhere in the vicinity of my heart, but maybe that was wishful thinking.
“It’s done,” he murmured. He tilted his head to press his smile against my cheek. “We’re tied together forever, now.”
The last knots of tension making my chest ache crumbled. Finally, I felt like I could sleep.
The same could not be said for my mate.
“Lynn?”
“Hmm.”
My response was more wordless protest than actual answer. Zyjii hesitated, and I let out a long mental sigh.
“What is it?” I asked with all the gentleness I could muster.
Even to me, I sounded grumpy.
“If your people don’t tie heart-threads, how do you mate?”
“We trade words,” I said. “Words, and rings.” Then, before he could ask, I explained, “A ring is a band of metal worn on the finger. Decoration, like your necklace.”
His fingers flexed against my back. “Sounds dangerous. What if it snagged on a crag of bark? You could break a bone.”
I huffed a quiet laugh. “We don’t live in trees. We’re ground dwellers. And people who work with their hands will thread their
ring on a necklace, so they can still wear it without risk of injury. Or losing it.”
“Will you give me words and a ring someday?” he asked.
His tone might have been teasing, but the subtle tension in the arms holding me told me he was serious.
I chose to answer the unspoken need, not the lighthearted tone.
“If that’s what you want, then yes. Of course I will,” I said.
He was quiet a moment. Long enough for me to snuggle in and let myself start drifting toward sleep again.
“I want that,” he finally said, a soft admission whispered to the dark as if it were too fragile for even the gentle glow of the jungle flora outside our hidden shelter. “I want us to be bound in the way of your people, too.”
“Mkay,” I agreed.
I actually liked the idea of putting a ring on him, and I feel asleep planning how to make it happen.

Chapter Thirty-Five
ZYJII
A familiar,
rhythmic clacking woke me. Lynn laid tucked beneath me, her warm breath stirring the fur on my shoulder. I frowned, confused for a moment. Had I dreamed⁠—
The clacking came again. I tensed, listening intently.
Lynn woke in reaction to my sudden stillness.
“What—” she started, but I cut her off with a soft hiss of warning.
No sign at cliffs. Continuing search.
Five breaths of silence followed by:
Found den. Signs of quick departure.
Two breaths, and the message continued.
No tracks. Will keep searching
.
Other messages came in, my ears swiveling to catch the distant sounds, but the tree sheltering us muffled most of them.
Lynn waited until I blew out a sharp breath and rose to begin clearing away all evidence we’d stayed there.
“What’d they say?” she asked.
“They found our den, but not our tracks.” Largely thanks to a combination of me keeping us in the air as long as possible and her skill at concealing any tracks we did leave. “They’re looking for us.”
She blew out a breath, then got up and quickly gathered her strange blanket. A blanket that remained dry despite the dampness of the ground beneath my toes. She folded it into a square the size of her palm—small indeed for a blanket big enough for us both to stretch out on—and stuffed it into her pack.
“We should get going,” she said, sounding surprisingly calm despite the situation.
I was not calm. My wings shivered with my frantic need to scoop her up and carry her far away from any hint of danger.
I managed to resist only because I knew she wouldn’t appreciate being protected like that. Not my Lynn. She was more fierce than any battle scarred old hunter.
“Yes,” I agreed.
She caught my hands, gently pulling them away from their restless fidgeting with the straps tying my spear to the basket filled with our most precious belongings.
I met her gaze, the not-quite-blue steady, her calm unshakeable.
“We expected this,” she said. “The risks remain the same, our plan unchanged. We’ll be fine.”
I nodded. She spared a moment to brush her hand along my cheek in a soothing caress, and then she got to work.
It didn’t take long to hide all signs that someone—that two someones—had spent the night in the hollow. When we set off, we remained on the ground, with Lynn leading the way. She was more familiar with hiding her tracks in dirt and rotting leaves, and so I did as she commanded. I stepped where she stepped,
and moved as my father taught me, shifting with the forest rather than against it.
Lynn moved amongst the thick fronds like a night whisper, a hauntingly beautiful creature said to be made of wisps of mist and glow-light from flowers like the ee vynla. She was all silent grace, a vision from beyond Vobe’s searing light.
Until she glanced back at me, her reassuring smile more like a grimace. It lightened some of the heaviness weighing down my wing-arms.
I fought against the urge to grab her hand, but eventually gave in, letting my tail snake forward to twine around her wrist. She tossed me another look, this one amused. And tempered by a gentling around the eyes that made the expression as sweet as it was chiding.
We continued on like that through the morning, not even stopping to eat. Lynn pulled chunks of dried meat from her pack as we went, along with a mix of nuts and dried fruit she’d put together days before. All easily consumed while we walked. Nothing that left traces as we moved.
“Can you
sweem
?” she asked me.
One ear turned toward her, while the other kept swiveling, trying to catch any further hunt-speak.
“
Sweem?
” I asked.
“Move through water without falling to the bottom.”
I snorted, amused by the description.
“Yes, I know how to swim,” I said. “Why?”
“Because we’re nearing a river, and nothing conceals tracks like water does.”
That pulled my attention firmly away from the forest and the still-distant hunters.
“Rivers are dangerous,” I said. Considered it a moment, and corrected myself. “
All
ground waters are dangerous. They’re
used by the predators who can’t fly or climb. And there are plenty of predators
in
the water.”
Lynn was silent a moment, likely remembering her first encounter with a mlyrk.
“Right,” she finally agreed. “But what if we gathered bunches of the river plant? The one that turns away predators and other animals?”
“Zeeving grass?”
“That’s the stuff. We could strap it to our arms and legs. To our packs. That’d provide protection to swim, yes?”
“Yes,” I said slowly, my hesitation drawing the word out into a long hiss. “But the waters themselves can be dangerous. The surface might look slow, but the current can move so fast beneath that even the strongest hunters in the village can’t swim against it.”
“You don’t swim against the current,” she said, as if this was a much repeated learning tale. “You swim
with
it. And a fast current would carry us further from the hunters faster than our feet can.”
There was that. Still, I hesitated. It was too dangerous. Even if we covered ourselves in zeeving grass, there were still things that could catch us. Trap us. Drown us. And my worry about the strength of the water itself remained.
I chuffed. Traveling by river had merit. We just needed to be smart about how we did it. Smarter than the hunters following us.
“If you want to travel by water, I might have a way,” I said.

Chapter Thirty-Six
LYNN
I stared at the…creature—Beast?
Animal, maybe—in a combination of fascination and confusion. My lips parted, but nothing came out. It wasn’t like I needed to ask
what the hell
is
that?
because Zyjii, always eager to share his knowledge, gestured at it and exclaimed, “A

!”
He said it with such glee. I was more dubious. The creature stretched its long neck out, its mouth nomming the empty air as it attempted to catch Zyjii’s tail. Only the hulking beast moved far too slow for his whip-quick movements.
Its round, pudgy face looked like an axolotl’s, complete with feathered sticky-out bits. It definitely had a shell. A shell that apparently had hollow bits filled with sand because it issued a hiss like a rain stick every time it moved.
The feet, tail, and teeth were all crocodile.
Oh, and it was giant. Like, its shell was the size of an elephant.
“That’s a lot of teeth,” I finally said.
Zyjii blinked at me—clearly, that was not the reaction he’d expected—and twisted around to studied the krisvreep. It slowly opened its mouth, giving us a great view of the rows upon rows
of said teeth, then jerked its head forward and snapped its jaws shut, missing the tip of Zyjii’s tail by a scant inch.
“They look fierce,” he admitted, sounding thoroughly unconvinced, “but they’re harmless.”
I stifled a sigh. No animal that size was ever ‘harmless.’
“Well,” he continued, looking a little sheepish, “except for their feathers. You have to watch out for those. They sting.”
Wonderful, I thought dryly.
“And the fact it seems intent on eating your tail?” I asked.
“Oh, that?” He glanced over his shoulder, watching his tail snaked back and forth as the krisvreep's head followed it. Slowly, but without wavering or deviating from its intended target. “
That
is because of
this
. Catch.”
He flicked his tail. My left hand snapped up on pure reflex—you try growing up with a dad who thinks the only appropriate way to pass the salt is to say “Think fast” and chuck the salt shaker at your head, and see what it does for
your
catch reflexes—and something big, round, and meaty smacked into my palm.
I twisted my hand around to stare at the fruit.
The krisvreep's big head shifted toward me, its neck stretching. Frowning, I tossed the fruit toward it, and it snapped the fruit out of the air.
“Biin berries,” Zyjii informed me, chest puffed a bit. “Krisvreep love them, but have a hard time getting them, since they grow on vines high above the river. Other animals love them too, so by the time they’re ripe enough to drop from the vine into the water where the krisvreep can get them, there aren’t many left.”
“So it’s a rare treat,” I said. “What does that have to do with traveling the...river…”
My voice trailed off, because a horrible, awful suspicion snuck up on me, just waiting to pounce and ruin my day.
My cheerfully clueless mate beamed at me. “It’s simple. We attach a biin berry to a length of twine, and attach that to a long reed. The krisvreep will chase it, while we sit safely on its shell, out of reach of the water predators.”
I gaped at him. Oh. My. God. He planned to carrot-on-a-stick a giant axolotl turtle creature to turn it into a ferry for us. He was crazy. Absolutely bug nuts insane. There was zero chance this plan would work the way he expected it to.
And I was worse than crazy, because I was going to go along with it.
“I can’t believe this is working,” I muttered an hour later.
An hour sitting on the uncomfortably poky turtle shell while Zyjii used his berry-on-a-stick to steer our…conveyance.
“I told you,” Zyjii said. His tone remained cheerfully unconcerned, but there was a definite smugness to his flicking tail. “I used to do this as a kit, before I learned how to swim.”
“Your poor mother,” I muttered under my breath.
Seriously, from the stories he told me, he’d been a downright mischief goblin as a kid. One of those little boys with way too much curiosity fueled by boundless energy that got him into—and out of—all kinds of trouble.
I imagined chasing after a tiny version of Zyjii, with ears too big for his head, huge eyes, and a wildly expressive tail, trying to keep the miscreant from getting himself eaten by a yreki patch. Somewhat to my shock, my lips kicked up in a smile.
Huh. I sure as shit had never wanted kids before. Still wasn’t sure I wanted them now, but…I didn’t hate the idea as much as I thought I would. Actually, I kind of like the idea of little
Zyjiis running amok, getting into their father’s weaving and ‘borrowing’ my darts and atlatl to go hunting.
Smile softening, I lifted the knot of wood I’d taken from the hollow where we’d spent the night, and blew away some shavings.
“What’s that smile for?” Zyjii asked.
I glanced up from my work to find him with a sweetly puzzled smile as he watched me.
I hesitated, then shrugged. “I was thinking about children.” His ears twitched straight up, and I bit back a laugh. “Kits,” I explained before he could ask. “Mostly, I was thinking that any kits of yours would be little troublemakers.”
He chuffed a laugh. “I
was
trouble. My poor dame was forever scolding me for scaring all the fur from her tail. She liked to tease me that if I wasn’t careful, I’d be responsible for making her tail completely bald, like a kicluck’s.” He gave me a teasing glance. “A rozard’s.”
He’d told me once he thought my name for the rodent lizards was adorable. I suspected it was more that he thought
I
was adorable, but whatever.
“I, of course, found the idea of my dame with a bald tail hilarious,” he continued, “and used it as an excuse to cause even more mischief, just to see if I could make her threat come true.”
I grinned. Of course he had.
The tail that had been flicking idly against my thigh stilled. I glanced up from the awkward task of using a sharp rock to chisel away at a hunk of wood to find him with his head tipped back, staring upward, to the occasional breaks in the jungle canopy that revealed a deeply, impossibly blue sky.
“I’ve never really thought of having kits before,” he said softly. “Do you want kits?”
“I don’t know,” I admitted honestly. “Definitely not right now.” I hesitated, then gave him another truth, one he might not
understand. “We couldn’t have kits together anyway. We’re too different. It’d be like…”
“Vriiryn and trigiz,” he said.
Vriiryn were like wolf-sized foxes, only with the retractible claws and the boneless grace of a cat. Trigiz looked a lot like porcupines. Bigger, because everything in this god forsaken jungle was bigger, with reptilian scales instead of fur, but their overall shape, complete with quills, was similar.
“Yeah,” I agreed softly. “Like vriiryn and trigiz. They might den together, hunt together, and even love together, but they can’t make kits.”

“It’s probably just as well. Can you imagine a vriiryn with quills?” He shuddered dramatically, the finger bones in his wings rattling. “They’re quite prickly enough as it is. They don’t need any extra bite.”
Chuckling, I petted his tail, then bent back to work whittling down the wood knot to the size I wanted.

Chapter Thirty-Seven
ZYJII
Vobe
strode high through the sky, his light piercing through the leaves that thinned as the river grew fatter and slower. Our krisvreep maintained a steady pace, occasionally snapping its teeth in an attempt to catch the biin berry I dangled in front of it, forever just out of reach.
I tried not to feel too bad about that.
“I promise, you’ll get the berry,” I said. “And more besides.”
I’d stuffed my largest sack full of them.
Lynn snorted softly.
She sat with her back braced against mine. I held my wing-hands above her, the first long fingers linked together at the claw to create a canopy to shade her. Vobe didn’t like my sky-fallen female. “It’s not that bad.”

burned

areboriial

Arboreal

A pose I recognized because I’d hunted alongside her. A pose a hunter from the village wouldn’t understand the danger of because we didn’t throw our spears. We dropped with them, using our weight and the speed of the fall to aid in our kill.

I wasn’t sure how I felt when a shuttle’s exhaust winds blasted the foliage around us into a swaying, agitated frenzy. Relief? Worry? Guilt? Maybe a combination of all that and more.
Zyjii brought a wing around to shield us from the worst of the buffeting. When the winds cut off abruptly, he let out a startled cheep. Shifting his wing enough to peer around it, he studied the apparently empty space, his ears working overtime as he took in every minute change in the forest.
I wondered if he could hear the shuttle’s super quiet engines. I would have asked if it wouldn’t have required a lengthy, probably impossible explanation of what an engine
was
.
One thing was clear to me, though. The shuttle had landed, and no one was coming out. I narrowed my eyes at the seemingly empty space. I could just make out the marks on the mossy rocks
that showed where the landing feet had settled. So, yeah. They were definitely waiting for something.
Zyjii’s wildly lashing tail brushed against me, and I had a sudden spark of realization. The ISSC had pretty strict policies about interacting with people on underdeveloped and primitive planets. Whoever was in the shuttle respected the ISSC’s laws enough to wait for Zyjii to leave before they approached me.
Which wasn’t going to happen.
Standing, I brushed dust and broken leaf bits from my bare legs. Zyjii instantly leapt to his feet, his tail draping itself over my shoulder in a way that managed to be protective, possessive, and concerned, all at once. I stroked it, as much to reassure myself as to sooth him.
Then I stepped in front of him, planted my hands on my hips, and glared at the shuttle.
“My mate’s not going to leave me, and I’m not going to ask him to, so you might as well come out.”
Three breaths later, the soft hiss of an airlock seal breaking disrupted the eerie silence the jungle had descended into. A hatch opened, unseen except for the slice of the shuttle’s interior it revealed. The tip of Zyjii’s tail started flicking madly against my stomach, tickling me badly enough I gripped it and shifted it to wrap around my arm instead.
I risked a quick glance at him, honestly a little worried about his reaction. Seeing a camouflaged shuttle open up was disturbing to me, and I’d seen it before. To him, it must have been strange and possibly terrifying and⁠—
His ears stood up straight with familiar quivering intensity. His tail, despite remaining anchored around my arm, writhed behind him. In eagerness, not fear.
I pursed my lips and stifled the mad urge to laugh like a hyena. I should have known better. Present a world shattering
mystery to my Mrriiv mate and of
course
he’d react with extreme curiosity.
That curiosity only ramped up when an alien stepped into the doorway. What I felt, however, was extreme shock. Because it wasn’t possible. My eyes had to be playing tricks on me.
“Hirza?” I whispered. Behind me, all of Zyjii went still at whatever he heard in my voice. “You…you survived? But⁠—”
But I’d watched. I’d scanned the blackness of space with my eyes and the computer, searching for other escape pods. There’d been none. To all apparent evidence, I’d been the only one to escape the attack on the
Rsp’rin
.
Hirza frowned at me, her ears swiveling back and her three tails writhing behind her in a rare show of distress. But when she spoke, her voice remained crisp and even, full of that badass girl boss energy I’d so admired over a year ago, during the interview that saw me hired by aliens.
“All ambassador class ships are equipped with escape pods capable of voidstalker level camouflage.” Her expression soften, more a relaxing of her ears than a shift in her facial expression. Ah. That explained why I’d had such an easy time reading Zyjii right from the start. I hadn’t realized just how similar his body language was to that of a Rehiki. “I’m sorry you had to believe the worst, and for so long.”
I sucked in a deep breath that shook. “Then the others? T’rlkiax?”
“Only those caught in the initial blast were lost. T’rlkiax made it out. He’s spent the last two months working tirelessly to get the approval needed for a surface search and rescue.”
I blinked at that. Why would they⁠—
Correctly reading my confusion, Hirza smiled wryly.
“Usually, when a pod crash lands on a primitive planet, it’s a huge source of tech that we can easily track from outer orbit. But…well, you were there. Your pod crashed because of massive
system failures caused by the shockwave from the jump engine imploding. That meant no tech to track. Except a single piece shielded by living substance.”
She pointedly tapped her head, next to her ear. My translation chip, she meant.
“We should have still been able to track you from orbit, but…this jungle.” Hirza’s lip curled in a displeased snarl. “Between the constant, heavy layer of water vapor that hovers over this place, the super dense canopy, and whatever weirdness the plants here seem to produce, we simply couldn’t find you. We tracked the crash site. Got imaging that showed the door had been opened, but we didn’t know if it was because you’d left the pod yourself, or wild animals had ripped it off to get at your remains.
Behind me, Zyjii growled softly. Despite the fact Hirza and I were both speaking English, a lot of conversation involved vocabulary he simply lacked the context for me to even begin to explain. But he got enough of that last part to understand the implications.
Unsure what else to do, I reached back for him. He immediately took my hand and stood beside me. Hirza glanced between us, fox-like ears perked.
“Hirza,” I said. “Allow me to introduce my bonded mate, Zyjii. Zyjii.” I glanced up at him, but he kept his eyes focused on Hirza. “This is my friend, Hirza.”
“The one you thought you lost,” he said softly.
“Yeah.”
He studied the Rehiki and the slice of an entirely alien world he could see behind her.
“She’s here to rescue you. And she’s someone you trust.”
My hand clenched convulsively around his. “Yes. To both.”
He finally tore his gaze away from the tiny sliver of the shuttle he could see, his wonder and curiosity overshadowed
by some far darker, stronger emotion as he stared at me. His shoulders started to heave as if he was gearing up to dive to the bottom of a deep lake. The grip of his tail around my arm tightened before it fell away.
“Then,” he started. Stopping, he dropped his gaze to our clasped hands and closed his eyes. “Then you have to go with her. You have to go.”
Confusion tipped with razor sharp hurt stabbed through me. “Zyjii?”
Releasing my hand, he ripped himself away from me hard enough he staggered two steps. Wings rattling with distress, tail coiled around his own leg, he faced Hirza.
“This place isn’t safe for her,” he said, voice impassioned while the rest of him screamed in silent agony. “My people. They don’t accept those that are different. They will hunt her. They
have
hunted her. If they stumble across us, they’ll hunt her again. So she must go.”
I stared at him, my heart threatening to tear itself into bloody tatters. Until I clenched a metaphorical fist around it and squeezed, wringing out the heartbreak. This wasn’t a rejection. This was his attempt to protect me in the only way he knew how.
“Zyjii,” I growled at him.
He gave me a panicked look, but quickly shifted it back to Hirza. He clamped his wings tight to his back and fisted his hands, drawing himself up and setting his jaw with determination, even as his ears betrayed his misery.
“Please,” he pleaded softly. “I want her to be happy.”
“Then I’m staying with you,” I snapped.
He whined softly in distress, and I softened. A little. Enough that I carefully took his fist into my hands instead of slugging his shoulder. Gently, I pried his fingers loose,
tsk
-ing over the bleeding wounds his claws had dug into his palms. I tugged open
one of the pouches at my waist—a pouch he’d made me—and took out a pinch of soak-moss to press into the wounds.
“Idiot male,” I whispered. Cradling his hand in one of mine, I cupped his cheek with the other. “
You
make me happy, Zyjii. I’m not leaving you. End of discussion.”
“But—” he started.
I glared him into silence. “No. I’m staying with you.”
Ears trembling, he turned a pleading gaze to Hirza, who watched us with unfeigned consideration.
“We’ll see her safe,” she said. When I snarled at her, she smirked. “Don’t worry, Lynn. You know we don’t separate bonded mates.” Her golden eyes soften when she looked at Zyjii. “We’re not going to take her from you.”
His tail crept around my leg, coiling tight. Hope made his moss green eyes shine. “Does that mean I can go with her? With you?”
Hirza dipped her nose in a yes, then flicked an ear in a no. “I’m not authorized to remove a local from a primitive classed planet. Let me make some calls and see what I can do.”
It wasn’t a yes. Zyjii understood from the determined slant of her ears, even if he didn’t understand everything she’d said. That didn’t stop him from snatching me up in a desperately tight hug, his whole body shaking with relief.
He’d been willing to give me up in order to see me safe. I held him back, just as fierce, just as shaken by the thought of being forced to leave him. He really was an idiot male. A sweet idiot, and he was mine, but definitely an idiot.
My hand slid through his thick mane of hair to cup the back of his head and pull him in closer. Geeze, how I loved him.

Chapter Forty-Three
ZYJII
Lynn let
me cling to her without complaint. Better, she held me back even more fiercely.
“I’m sorry,” I murmured into the skin of her shoulder.
She sighed and stroked the length of one ear.
“I forgive you, but only because you obviously acted from a place of concern and care.”
“Not care,” I muttered. “I love you, Lynn.”
Huffing softly, she leaned into me a bit harder. “Then don’t try to get rid of me again.”
I jerked away as much as her grip allowed, spluttering in offense. She narrowed her eyes at me, daring me protest, but…she…wasn’t wrong. From her perspective, it must have felt like that was exactly what I was trying to do.
Accepting defeat, I sighed, then dropped my forehead back to her shoulder.
“I am sorry,” I mumbled. “That wasn’t my intent.”
“I know,” she said. And left it at that.
Slowly, the sounds of the forest—animal calls and insects singing and the occasional rustle of a plant relocating itself—returned. As they did, my heartbeat slowed and steadied.
At least until the hiss of Hirza’s sky-den announced her return.
Lynn straightened away from me, and I let go. Mostly. My tail stubbornly insisted on remaining wrapped tight around her waist. Not that she seemed to notice. Or maybe she did, because she ruffled her fingers lightly through my fur, coaxing it into lying flat when I hadn’t even noticed it puff with tension.
“I managed to contact T’rlkiax,” Hirza announced. I assumed the strange word with the click in it was the name of a sky-dweller. Both she and Lynn treated it as one, at least. The sky-dweller who was so like a Mrriiv and yet so different settled her sharp yellow eyes on me. “He’d like to speak to you.”
Lynn tensed. “Inside the shuttle
?
”
Shuttle. Sky-den.

not
like her looking at my Lynn like that.
“Is that a problem?” Hirza asked.
Lynn’s lip twitched like it wanted to curl in a snarl, but she refrained from responding. Instead, she glanced at me and shrugged. Leaving it to me to decide.
“I must speak to this sky-dweller before I can leave here with Lynn in your sky-den?” I asked.
Hirza gave my mate an exasperated look. “Lynn,” she chided.
“What?” Lynn demanded, defensive. “He saw my pod crash, and saw me emerge from it. He’s called me ‘sky-fallen’ since the first time he approached me.”
My wing-hands shifted restlessly, the soft clatter of my finger bones drawing their attention back to me.
“So?” I said. “I must talk to this sky-dweller, yes?”
“Yes.” Hirza folded her hands in front of her. “If you’ll come this way?”
I stepped forward, Lynn right at my side. Possibly because my tail still refused to budge from around her waist.
“Zyjii—” she started in a low voice.
But Hirza’s sharply pointed ears weren’t for show.
“Lynn,” she warned sharply.
Lynn bared her teeth in frustration, but said nothing else.
It was clear she wanted to warn me about something, so I entered the sky-den with care, senses alert for anything out of the ordinary. Except that
everything
was extraordinary.
The ground beneath my long-toed paws felt strange. Smoother than the smoothest river-worn stone, but more solid than the smoothest leaves. Light, white and pure and as bright as Vobe’s glare, came from…everywhere. Long stretches of the ceiling, odd spaces on the walls that might have been patches of moss except the edges were too even, too uniform. The light even came from the corners where walls and floor met.
I bent over for a better look, but Hirza cleared her voice.
“This way,” she said.
I rose and followed her through a second opening into a space easily twice the size of the den I’d shared with Lynn. Toward the back of the space stood a monster straight from the murky depths of the floodlands. My fur puffed and my wings immediately rattled a warning. The monster studied me silently with eyes as black as the sky when all the Sisters rested from their constant dance.
When the monster remained still, I relaxed a fraction, studying it warily. My tail flicked when I noticed something strange.
I tilted my head.
“Is that a spirit?” I asked.
Behind me, Lynn snorted. “No. Think of it as a form of hunt-speak.”
I chuffed in disbelief. “Hunt-speak that…what? Throws images instead of sounds?”
“Yes,” the monster—who was vaguely transparent, like the veil of a shallow waterfall—said.
I cheeped in surprise, wing-hands bristling, then I crept forward, wanting a better look at this not-spirit monster.
I circled the figure. It turned with me. A stranger creature I’d never seen. Its head was skull-like, topped by a crown of many small horns. Those black eyes were deep set and—despite first appearances—not at all lifeless. Its teeth remained bared in a perpetual threatening grin, and it took me a close study to realize it was because it lacked lips of any kind. Only exposed black tendons connecting jaw to skull.
Behind it waved four vine-like tendrils that started off thick at the base and tapered to narrow ends. As I stared, spines lifted along the length of them, and I stiffened, my legs quivering with the need to leap away. I recognized a warning sign from a predator when I saw one.
I eyed the spirit-like monster, then darted one hand out, quick as a blink. Instead of hitting the solid breadth of the creature’s chest—a clothed chest, I realized—my fingers met only air and suffered a strange tingling akin to grabbing a krisvreep by one of its feathers.
Hissing in surprise, I hopped back, cradling my hand to my chest. My heart pounded hard, but the tingle faded instead of spreading. Not poison, then.
“Well, that’s encouraging,” the monster said.
Not a monster, I decided, eyeing its—his—complicated, finely woven clothing. This was another sky-dweller. The T’rlkiax I was supposed to talk to.
“What?” Lynn asked. “Were you expecting him to run screaming?”
“I wasn’t expecting him to make it through the
aerlahk
without panicking,” the hunt-speak spirit of T’rlkiax said. “And running screaming is the reaction most species have when encountering a Vrigrix for the first time. Hello, Lynn. It’s good to see you alive and well.”
“Same,
bahs
,” she said, her voice a rough rasp full of emotion she didn’t let reach her face.
I tilted my head first one way, then another, my ears swiveling. Because while his jaw moved, it didn’t do so in time with the English words he spoke, and the sound originated from somewhere else.
“So,” the hunt-speak spirit continued, watching me as I circled it and then began hunting down the source of its voice. “You went and bonded with one of the locals.”
Lynn shrugged. “I love him. And since I wasn’t expecting rescue, I didn’t see the harm in making a life for myself here.”
“Understandable,” the spirit murmured. Then he cleared his throat. “Zyjii, was it? What are you doing?”
“Trying to find where your voice is coming from,” I said. “Because it’s not from there.”
I waved a wing-hand in his general direction as I braced my working-hands on the floor and peered beneath the strange table that spanned most of the wall behind the hunt-speak spirit.
“You’re not afraid?”
My ear swiveled toward the sound of his voice—which seemed to be coming from one of those strange glowing not-moss shapes on the wall—but I looked at the image. I blinked and gave his terrifying figure another long look.
“You look like the dark dweller who hunts the floodlands,” I said. “It is a creature of many terrifying tales. But you wear clothing. The dark dweller only cares about eating.” I grimaced. “It’s worse than grrns, so it’s a good thing it’s confined to the floodlands.”
“
Lahjikal
,” the hunt-speak spirit murmured. I didn’t recognize the word—it wasn’t one Lynn had taught me yet—but his approval was clear. “You’re observant, and don’t react to the unexpected with fear. That’s a good quality for anyone to have, but especially someone who intends to step from their familiar world into something much bigger and more confusing than they could possibly imagine.”
“Actually, sir,” Lynn said wryly. “Zyjii’s curiosity is more likely to get him into trouble than anything. He doesn’t have much of a survival instinct when something catches his interest.”
Scoffing, I flicked my tail at her in dismissal of her description of me.
“Pitcher plant,” she said, her tone flat.
I growled. That was different. How was I supposed to know the plant would try to eat me? It’d looked like a giant jinveep, and those weren’t just harmless, but good to eat.
Leaning in close, I sniffed at the glowing part of the wall where T’rlikiax’s voice came from. It smelled like everything else in Hirza’s sky-den. Oddly lifeless, as if it hadn’t come from a tree, or rock, or anything else I was familiar with.
I considered licking the glowing spot, but that was generally a stupid thing to do in the forest. I settled for tapping it with the tip of a claw. It sounded solid, but also hollow. Head tilted and ears pricked I tapped it again.
“I think you may be right about that, Lynn,” the hunt-speak spirit of T’rlkiax said wryly.

everything
. I wanted to figure out how the hunt-speak spirit worked. I wanted to understand where the light came from if it wasn’t from Vobe or plants. Maybe they’d somehow managed to capture many tiny white flames?

had
said that. “I trusted you, because I was a stranger in your world.” She pressed our clasped hands over her heartbeat, then pet the back of my fingers. “Now I need you to trust me for the same reason.”
I grumbled a bit, but I also nuzzled my nose into her mess of pale hair and stroked the tip of my tail down the back of her leg so she knew I didn’t really mean it.
“I think,” the hunt-speak spirit of T’rlkiax said, “that this one will do just fine.”
I lifted my head in time to see him nod once.
“Hirza, bring them both home. I’ll get the
paeperwerk
started.”

Chapter Forty-Four
LYNN
Rescued.
Even standing in our assigned room aboard the ISSC patrol ship the
Slipstream
, freshly showered and fed, I could hardly believe it.
Zyjii—now equipped with a custom programed translation chip designed to fill in the gaps for words his language simply didn’t have—prowled around the room, his fur still damp enough his tail left behind the occasional drip of water. He poked his nose into everything, though he did wait until I reassured him there was nothing dangerous in our room. Except the hot water tap, which could produce boiling hot water on demand if you weren’t careful.
Once he finished inspecting every corner, and poking into every drawer—which mystified and fascinated and pleased him, in that order—he came to stand behind me. Pressing in against my back, he wrapped me up in his arms and tail, his embrace familiar and comforting, even if was still just a wee bit damp.
“There’s so much I need to learn,” he said. For the first time, he sounded daunted by the prospect, rather than ecstatic.
I stroked my fingers over his arm. “We’ll take things one step at a time. If you begin to feel overwhelmed, let me know. We’ll
come back here and rest. T’rlkiax put in the paperwork so you can keep your loom and other tools. Working with the familiar will help make everything else seem more manageable.”
“Is that what you did?”
“Well, no,” I admitted. “But I left my childhood home to get away from the familiar, and humanity is more advanced than your people so my adjustment was easier.”
He let out a trembling breath and squeezed me harder. Not for the first time, I wondered if I’d made the right choice, taking him from his planet.
“Do you regret it?” I asked. “Leaving?”
He was quiet long enough for tension to start tying knots along my spine.
“I asked the healer questions,” he finally said. “Every question I could think of. Do you know what they did?”
“What?” I asked, though I already knew.
“Answered them. Even if I didn’t understand everything they said, they answered my questions to the best of their ability. They didn’t click their tongue at me, or tell me I didn’t need to understand what their tools did because it wasn’t my chosen path. They didn’t tell me that knowing things I shouldn’t would bring trouble. They even let me try using some of the tools myself.”
He rested his cheek on top of my head with a deep, satisfied sigh. “If I discover something new, they’ll listen and offer advice on how to discover more, even if it’s not new to them. I…I feel less like an outsider here, on this sky-de—this
ship
than I ever did in the village, surrounded by my own people.
“Do I regret leaving?” Again he was quiet a moment, really thinking it over. “I regret I won’t be able to discover why the ee vynla change the color of their glow depending on which Sister is dancing across the sky, and I still would have really liked to see the desert, but…those are small things.”
He squeezed me, then dropped his head to caress my shoulder with his lips.
“I do not regret choosing a life where we will not be hunted for being different,” he said firmly. “I do not regret being thrown into a world so much larger and stranger than anything I could have imagined. I will
never
regret being allowed to stay with you, no matter where you go.”
My eyes felt suspiciously hot. I blinked away the dry, gritty feeling that always preceded tears, and gave his arm an awkward pat.
“Good,” I said. I cleared my throat, trying to get rid of the telling rasp. “I love you, too. And I wouldn’t have regretted staying on your planet. Even if we did spend the rest of our lives traveling. As long as we did it together.”
He squeezed me and kissed my neck. “Together. Always, Lynn. My sky-fallen mate.”
Hirza and the rest of the crew gave us plenty of space for a few days. Apparently they thought I needed time to recover from the trauma of roughing it in a jungle for a couple months. Clearly, they did not understand that was pretty much my idea of a relaxing vacation.
All right, I could have done without the ambulatory carnivorous plants and giant ass shark-cats, but everything else was just a day in the life.
Zyjii ended up infected with cabin fever long before me. Hirza had provided us a tablet loaded with all kinds of teaching apps, and while Zyjii found the learning guides useful, he was more interested in figuring out how the tablet worked. And by
figuring out how it worked
, I really meant
take it apart to see what made it tick
.
Since I didn’t expect him to be able to put it back together in a way that would leave us with a still-functioning tablet—something he needed—I took him to the break room the engineers and mechanics tended to hang around in. Every ISSC ship of modest size had one, and they all invariably ended up littered with bits of tech or mechanical projects in various states of completion.
You’d think we’d walked into a candy shop, the way Zyjii lit up when we stepped into the cluttered room.
“Don’t touch without permission,” I warned him.
He threw me his most charming grin, the impact of it only heightened by the excited wiggling of his tail.
“But I
can
look,” he said.
I pursed my lips to keep from smiling. “I’m certainly not going to stop you.”
He lit up and bounded away toward the table shoved against the nearest wall. One covered in assorted bits that looked like engine parts in need of replacement or repair. He kept his wings tucked politely in and his hands clasped at his back. His tail, though, flicked back and forth, occasionally freezing whenever he spotted something particularly interesting.
One of the people on break obviously recognized me as someone who didn’t belong and approached me. The Ogirryn female stood way taller than me, and nearly as broad through the shoulders as Zyjii, but her curves were markedly, definitely feminine. So was the delicate chain connecting a silver stud in her pointed ear to the hoop in her broad, upturned nose.
Ogirryn would never not remind me of orcs from some kind of fantasy game. Only with skin tones that ranged from grey to brown to yellow instead of green.
“You need something?” she asked gruffly.
“Yeah, actually. You got any broken tablets and the tools necessary to take them apart?”
She eyed me. “You interested in tech work?”
My hands shot up in instant surrender. “Not me.” I tilted my head toward my mate, who’d tilted over until he was practically upside down in order to get a better look at something clinging to the bottom of the junk table. “For him.”
The Ogirryn followed my gaze and grunted.
“Word’s gone around about the new guy,” she said. “We were told to be patient with him and answer any questions he has. Like he was raised on some backwater planet and knows nothing about anything.”
“Close enough,” I muttered.
She watched him with eagle-eyed intensity for another couple minutes—probably to make sure he didn’t touch anything he wasn’t supposed to, which he didn’t, though he did nearly get his nose zapped when he stuck it too close to a misfiring piece of tech someone had stupidly left out—before she grunted again.
“We ain’t just gonna hand over delicate tools to someone who doesn’t know how to use them,” she said.
I scowled but didn’t protest.
“Hey, you,” she called.
“Zyjii,” I supplied.
Of course, his ears immediately swiveled in my direction, even though I hadn’t raised my voice. So he was already lifting his head when the Ogirryn called him over.
“Zyjii.” She waved one massive hand.
He came over, properly wary of her size and the bulge of her clear cut muscles, but not scared.
She crossed her massive arms over her chest and gave him a once over. “Your mate tells me you want to learn how to take things apart.”
“No,” he said. She tensed, but he continued. “I want to figure out how they work.”

“Well, now, then,” she said, lowering her arms. “That’s something I don’t mind helping with. Name’s Sierva.”
She slammed a fist to the center of her chest in the traditional Ogirryn greeting, the meaty thump of it loud enough to make me wince.
“Zyjii.” He bobbed his ears at her. Then after a moment of uncertain tail writhing, he copied her gesture, hitting a fist to the center of his chest.
She grinned. “You’re cute. I like you.”
I cleared my throat.
She grunted at me. “Keep your panties unbunched, human. I’d be more likely to try to seduce you than your male.”
Fluster blazed across my face and I tried not to sputter. Zyjii just tilted his head, completely unconcerned with the idea of the giant Ogirryn female flirting with me.
If I had even half of his confidence…
“And me liking him is good news,” Sierva continued, smirking at my embarrassment, “because it means I’m willing to teach him what he wants to know.”
Zyjii perked. “You will?”
“Yeah.” She clapped a hand to his shoulder and steered him toward the back of the room. “Come on. We’ll start with something simple. You any good with your hands?”
“I’m a weaver.”
Sierva stopped mid-step to stare at him in blank incomprehension.
“Huh.” Finally she grunted and resumed striding across the room. “Maybe I better make it something
really
simple.”
Bemused, I made myself comfortable on one of the well-worn but definitely comfy sofas. I pulled out the small chunk of wood I’d talked Hirza into letting me keep—after sending it through a brisk decontamination process—as well as the small penknife I’d asked for and been issued our first day on board. Then I let my hands be busy while my mind chewed over a problem I needed to work out before the
Slipstream
reached port.
