Chapter Forty-Five
LYNN
Zyjii’s
happy chatter accompanied us all the way to the mess hall where we grabbed dinner. He drew more than a few curious looks, for several reasons. One, because he was a never-seen-before species. Two, his cheer radiated from him like heat from a supernova, warming everyone around him. Then there was his tail, which kept alternating between intent swishing flicks and ecstatic wriggling. Considering there was considerably more tail than male, it garnered some attention.
Finally, there was the fact he still only wore his woven loin cloth and corset-like leather belt, though he’d left all his pouches and stone knives in our room.
To be honest, my presence probably didn’t help matters. Because I was human, and while humans were a known quantity in the ISSC, we were few and far between. There was a good chance I was the first Earthling most of these aliens had ever seen up close in person.
If that wasn’t all attention grabbing enough, I’d taken to wearing soft sports bras and the intragalactic version of cozy yoga pants, but over those, I wore the top and long skirt Zyjii had made me. I did it mostly so he wouldn’t feel pressured into
giving up the clothing he felt comfortable in. He faced enough change as it was.
“Lynn?”
I blinked down at my bowl of questionable soup—delicious, but it was better not to ask specifics about what kinds of meat or vegetables they put in things—and realized I’d been staring at it without eating for long enough to derail Zyjii’s happy ramblings.
I glanced up at him to find him studying his own tray intently.
“Yeah?” I prompted.
“Sierva asked if I planned to go into mechanics, electronics, or engineering.”
He pronounced the last three words with care, likely because they weren’t in his native tongue. The med techs had done a full work up on his physiology, brain waves, language centers, and a whole host of other tests I didn’t know the proper names for. Turned out his people’s language base—grammar and syntax as well as aural vocalizations—was similar to the Rehiki. Similar enough that they went ahead and programmed his translation chip using it as filler to provide all the necessary vocabulary living in the Coalition required.
So the words were unfamiliar to him in more ways than one, but I had no doubt he’d pick up on this new amalgamation of languages quick as a jiffy.
“What’d you tell her?” I asked, genuinely curious.
He flicked an ear at me. “That I didn’t understand what any of those things were.”
I snorted, nearly inhaling my soup.
He rescued my spoon before I dropped it, leaving me free to laugh. I could just imagine the Ogirryn’s absolutely befuddled expression.
“And?” I asked when I finally caught my breath.
“She explained that the…tinkering—” another careful tasting his way around the unfamiliar word “—we were doing was mechanics. And doing the same with things like the tablet was electronics, and engineering was making sure all the things worked the way they were supposed to.”
I nodded. Not a bad explanation, actually.
Instead of continuing, he fiddled with his silverware. Something he wasn’t used to using, but did his best to mimic me regardless. I made a point of eating with my hands whenever I could. Again, there was no need to push him over every little thing when he already had so much he needed to stuff in his head.
Having gone to the big city as a country bumpkin myself, I understood where he was coming from. It made supporting him in whatever small way I could extremely easy.
Studying his ears—which often told me more about his mood than anything else—I saw uncertainty in the way he held them. Uncertainty, and that intensity he had whenever he was presented with a problem he didn’t have a ready answer for.
“You don’t have to decide anything right away, you know,” I said. “We have time.”
He ear-bobbed in easy agreement, then shook his head like a dog coming out of water.
“I like tinkering,” he said. “I like taking apart the things Sierva gave me and trying to put them back together. It’s interesting, and there’s plenty of new things for me to learn, but…”
“It’s not the right fit?” I suggested.
He gave me a relieved look. “Yes, that’s it. I like the…metal things. The gears and electronics.” His eyes took on a distant misty look. “But I think I’d rather find a thing to do that involves plants.” The far-away look faded as he focused on me with a grin. “I still have mysteries about my forest to solve. And what if
what I already know could be used to help solve the mysteries of plants on other planets?”
The idea clearly excited him.
“You’re talking about xenobotany,” I said. The way his ears pricked to quivering attention made me smile. “That’s the study of plants from alien planets. And since we’re in the ISSC, all plants are technically from alien planets.”
He made his thoughtful croon. Then he noticed I wasn’t eating and pointedly nudged my spoon with one knuckle. I lifted an eyebrow at him—he’d stopped eating, too—and he grinned at me before tearing off a piece of bread and holding it up to my lips. I accepted the morsel, because I liked bread, and he didn’t.
He could eat fruits, nuts, and any kind of mystery meat in endless quantities without issue, but grains and dairy did not agree with him at all.
“I like the idea of that,” he declared. “I want to study plants. I want to figure out if they can be used to help others, like the yreki nectar. Mostly, I want to figure out why different moonlight makes the ee vynla change their glow colors.”
He uttered the last as an annoyed growl. It made me smile, which from the smug way his tail curled around my waist had probably been his aim. Smile settling deep in my heart, I leaned my shoulder against his arm.
“If that’s what you want to do, then we’ll make it happen,” I assured him.
Happy to have a solid path through the twisting confusion of his new life, Zyjii took a second to nuzzle me, then dug into his meal.
The Coalition had long ago worked out the psychological benefits of providing green spaces for long-term space crews, and that didn’t even take into consideration the many alien species who
required
a daily dose of sunlight.
Zyjii didn’t
need
green things and sunlight the way some other species did, but he was a being from a rich and verdant jungle, and I knew the sometimes cold sterility of the spaceship wore on him.
Most ships in the ISSC’s official fleet had hydroponic gardens, even if the garden wasn’t much bigger than a bedroom. The
Slipstream
’s garden was a lot bigger than that. More like a small city park. It was a gorgeous blend of strange flowers and edible plants, complete with a pond, brook, and several decorative bridges. Functional beauty at it’s finest.
Watching Zyjii’s wings finally relax as he stepped into the garden and took a deep breath full of the scent of growing things and damp earth confirmed all my suspicions. And my worries.
No matter where we ended up, it needed to be a planet with natural spaces. He might be able to survive on a ship, but he wouldn’t ever be fully happy or comfortable on one.
Leaving him in the capable hands of the Handavari in charge of the gardens—think elf, but less ethereal, with a splash of marsupial weirdness—I wandered around until I found a bench near the pond. Not long after I settled and pulled out my on-going busy-hands project, a familiar alien settled next to me.
“You know,” Hirza said, “I expected you to hunt me down for answers a lot sooner than this.”
Yeah, I just bet.
“Weirdly enough, my priorities have shifted,” I muttered.
Her eyes tracked to where the Handavari was showing Zyjii how to harvest a fruit vaguely shaped like a pumpkin, only it was pink with white and blue spots and grew on a tree.
“Yes,” Hirza murmured. “That’s understandable.”
I blew some shavings from the small chunk of wood I’d painstakingly pared down from the huge chunk it’d been when I’d first found it, way back in that hollow tree where Zyjii tied his heart-thread to mine.
“So?” I asked. “Why was the
Rsp’rin
attacked? What happened to the rest of you? I imagine the blast that fried my pod’s systems knocked yours offline, too.”
“It did.” Hirza leaned back, weight braced on her palms. Her tails twined together in a writhing display of old anxiety. “It was touch and go for a while. We likely all would have either frozen to death or suffocated in our individual pods if a friendly junker hadn’t been passing through.”
Junkers were rigged with all kinds of delicate equipment designed to search for signs of tech failure. Gathering up wrecks and other space junk was their job, after all.
“Lucky,” I said.
“Yes,” she agreed.
We were quiet for a second. I waited for her to expand on their rescue by the junker, but she didn’t. I huffed. I’d gotten to know Hirza pretty well over the year I’d worked for T’rlkiax, even though she worked as something as a fixer and hadn’t spent much time aboard the
Rsp’rin
—she took on the job of visiting distant planets like Earth to find suitable employees for the upper echelons of the ISSC. Because of that she tended to be very, very tight lipped about certain things.
That was fine. Let her keep her secrets.
“And the attack?”
Her tails writhed faster before she forced them still.
“The official story is that we came out at a previously unused jump point and got caught up in the test run of a new Fala’ahn weapon.”
I grunted. “And the real story?”
She was quiet for long enough I suspected she wasn’t going to tell me, so when she spoke, I jumped enough I nearly stabbed myself with my penknife.
“I was entrusted to bring T’rlkiax information regarding a Fala’ahn commander who recently went missing under suspicious circumstances. We were en route to deliver it to the Fala’ahn High Council.”
I remembered the data chip T’rlkiax had pulled from his console and tossed to Hirza, right before she picked me up and hauled ass to stuff
my
ass into an escape pod.
“Let me guess,” I murmured. “Someone didn’t want that information to reach its destination.”
She nodded. The gesture looked a little weird on her, since her face was more like a fox’s than a human’s, but I’d long since gotten used to it. Hirza was very good at adopting the body language of the various species she interacted with. She told me once it was because it helped put them at ease and made them less likely to eat her.
I thought it was more that it made her easier to trust, but whatever.
“In the end, they failed. We safely made it off the ship and we survived our pods’ computer systems going out. The junker managed to track your pod to its crash site, though it lacked the tech necessary to find you.” She gave me a wry smile. “Junkers can find a tablet floating in the vast expanse between solar systems, but finding a single human on a fertile planet?”
My lips kicked up because, yeah. I could see the junker’s crew being a little out of their depth when it came to a rescue mission.
“It all worked out in the end,” she said.
“Lynn!” Zyjii exclaimed, bounding over to us. “Try this.”
He lifted a slice of the blue and white spotted pink pumpkin thing to my lips. I obediently open my mouth and took a bite. Flavor exploded across my tongue, filling my mouth with
the undeniable sweetness of strawberry shortcake with whipped cream frosting.
I may have moaned. Just a little.
“I think that may be one of my most favorite things ever,” I said. With great, honest feeling.
Grinning, he offered me the last of the slice, and I ate it straight from his fingers, ignoring Hirza’s blatantly amused stare. He fed me the rest of the fruit before he kissed me—swift but deep enough to catch the taste of the strawberry shortcake fruit lingering on my tongue—then bounded back to rejoin the Handavari.
Yeah. It had all worked out, hadn’t it? Now I needed to make sure things continued to work out for us going forward.

Chapter Forty-Six
ZYJII
After months
of traveling with Hirza and the crew of the
Slipstream
—months of loving Lynn and tinkering with Sierva and tending the garden with its overseer, Leeanlyn, and studying, so much studying—Lynn and I finally stepped into the sunlight of a new planet.
Hirza escorted us, because she was the one who’d made the arrangements.
“Welcome to Va’outh,” she said, gesturing. “This entire planet and its moons are dedicated to learning. Technically, the planet itself is supposed to be a single academy, but functionally it’s more like a number of interconnected universities,” she explained as I took in the huge buildings in front of us. Bigger than anything in the mountain city of my home planet. Bigger even than a full grown tunjun tree. Maybe.
This wasn’t the first time Hirza had explained how this planet worked, but it was the first time I truly understood the scale of what she meant.
From space, the planet’s surfaced had gleamed like a polished gem on the sun-side, while the night side glowed like the bioluminescent moss that speckled the bark of tunjun trees.
From my studies, I knew the planet had several land masses, but the entire surface was covered with manmade structures. Even the oceans.
“Each moon has a unique biome,” Hirza continued, “without any significant natural dangers, which make them well-suited to serve as training grounds for new researchers and explorers.”
They would also be the classrooms Lynn used for her teaching.
Originally, Lynn had planned to find some other job. Despite being a planetary system devoted to learning and teaching, there was no shortage of other occupations. As Lynn had explained, students needed to eat, and professors needed places to live, to buy research materials, to relax after a tough day. She insisted she could find a position waitressing, at the very least, though she hadn’t sounded thrilled about it.
Instead, Hirza found her a position as a professor’s assistant.
Lynn’s agreement to fill the position was the only reason I’d even been allowed to take the entrance exam. Apparently Va'outh was not only the biggest academy in the ISSC, but one of the most prestigious. Thanks to my studies aboard the
Slipstream
, I’d managed to pass the lowest entry level test to get into Va'outh’s accelerated learning program.
I was going to have to work hard to keep my place as a student, but I was eager for the challenge.
Lynn, though…
“So,” she said sourly, planting her hands on her hips. “Who’s this professor I’m supposed to work with?”
“He should be here shortly,” Hirza said. “Let me go see if they’ve unloaded our luggage.”
As soon as she waltzed off, her many tails swaying, I inched over to Lynn and curled my tail around her ankle in a nervous gesture.
“Are you sure about this?” I asked.
“Yes,” she said flatly.
My ears pressed back, because she didn’t sound happy about it. She hadn’t since Hirza first told her of the teaching position.
Lynn glanced up at me, noted the position of my ears, and sighed, her body softening. Sliding her hand into mine, she laced our fingers together and squeezed, a silent reassurance.
“I don’t think I’m cut out for teaching,” she said. “I don’t have the patience for it. And if I lose my position, will they kick you out?”
“You won’t be the teacher, though,” I reminded her gently. “You’ll just be helping him. And, Lynn.” I bumped her shoulder with mine. “I can honestly think of no one better to teach a class on how to survive unexpected situations on uninhabited or primitive planets.”
She grunted her disbelief, but she leaned into me.
“Besides,” I said lightly. “You taught me your English, with all it’s tricky sounds and words with many, often complex, meanings. If you can do that, teaching others how to start a fire should be easy.”
She barked a laugh, much to my delight. It made me feel good, knowing I could tease her into a better mood.
“I suppose you have me there. But you’re smart, Zyjii.”
I tilted my head at her. “Implying the students in your class will be stupid?”
“Not stupid,” she said, and I knew she meant that. Lynn rarely said something she didn’t mean unless she was being sarcastic at the expense of someone treating her like she was a pet—or worse—just because she was human. “Just…clueless. I imagine this class is going to see a lot of people who’ve never spent a full day outdoors, let alone slept on the ground or cooked over a campfire.”
Which was very much the reason why Va'outh put this class together in the first place, I suspected.
“Maybe I can come help on days when I don’t have class,” I said. I used our clasped hands to tug her into me, curling my tail around the backs of her thighs as I bent my head to nuzzle at her. “I like the idea of sharing the learning tales from my home planet with others.”
Smile going soft and sweet, she placed a hand on my chest. If I’d been dressed as I usually was, she’d have petted my fur. But today I wore more ‘decent’ clothes. They’d been a gift from one of the Fala’ahn who helped care for the
Slipstream
’s gardens.
Like me, Fala-ahn had wings, though theirs were feathered and made for actual flight. They also had six of them. Unlike me, they did not have tails. The piece he’d gifted me, he explained, had been specially designed with me in mind.
I liked the Fala-ahn style of dress, and already had plans to weave myself more. The clothing was mostly just long strips of lightweight fabric draped and arranged around wings—and other excess limbs, because the Fala-ahn also had four arms—and then belted into place. It was not dissimilar to what my people wore. There was just more of it.
Apparently there were enough alien species concerned with ‘modesty’ that the rest of the ISSC made concessions.
At least the loose clothing didn’t ruffle and irritate my fur the way the bodysuit Sierva gave me did. I was supposed to wear it while tinkering to protect against cuts and burns, but I hated it.
Now, Lynn’s hands smoothed over the fabric dyed a blue that was
almost
a match for her ee vynla dyed skirt.
“You’ll probably need all your free time for studying,” she said. “But we’ll take things one day at a time.”
That seemed to be her ruling principle. One day at a time.
Smiling, I finished nuzzling her and straightened up, intending to check on Hirza, when a heavy step scuffed to a halt behind us, catching my attention. One ear swiveled toward the source of the sound just as the person spoke.
“Lynn?”
The voice was masculine and rusty, as if it wasn’t used very often. In my arms, Lynn went stiff.
“Lynn-bear? That you?”
“Oh, holy fuck,” Lynn breathed.
She pulled away from me and I turned, surprised to see what must be a human male. I even recognized him, because Lynn had described him to me in bits and pieces through the stories about her childhood.
His skin glowed a healthy reddish brown, though his face looked like old leather, cracked and worn. His hair, a darker yellow than hers and threaded through with more silver than she’d described, was pulled back in a long tail. His appearance reminded me a bit of the village elders, but his bright blue eyes remained sharp with a hunter’s focus.
Lynn confirmed my suspicions with her stunned, “Dad?”
Tears shimmered in the other human’s eyes, but he didn’t let them fall. Instead, he held his arms open.
“Hey, little bear,” he said. “Finally found you.”
Moving as if in a dream, Lynn went to her sire. When his arms—lean and corded with muscle, visible even through the short sleeves of his shirt—closed around her, she made a small sound and burrowed into him.
My heart ached for her. She always spoke of her sire with a mingling of affection and exasperation. From her stories, I knew he had not been what humans considered a good sire, but she always insisted he’d done his best.
She’d also told me how she’d come to leave her planet and join the ISSC. Like my people, the humans of Earth did not know about the vast galaxy-spanning Coalition. She’d applied for a job, not knowing being accepted into the position meant being taken from her planet and everything she’d known in order to join the widespread collection of advanced alien species.
Her sire was supposed to believe her dead, so it was no wonder his presence there stunned her.
She recovered quickly though, my Lynn. Grabbing her sire by the shoulders, she shoved back from him, glaring.
“How the
hell
are you even here?” she demanded.
He smirked. “You think a bunch of aliens could keep me from my daughter after stealing her away from me?”
“He went to Chicago,” Hirza said as she rejoined us, followed by a floating platform laden with our luggage. Mostly her luggage, since Lynn and I didn’t have much. “To see for himself that you’d died. He didn’t accept the story we concocted about a fatal crash and your body being cremated by mistake.”
“I wasn’t going to believe you were dead ’til I saw the corpse myself,” her sire growled.
My fur bristled. He had a good growl.
“He managed to track down our hiring agency, and then proceeded to hunt down each and every shell corporation we have set up across the United States and Canada,” Hirza continued. Her tone was very dry, but the snap of her many tails gave away her displeasure with the whole situation. “He hounded us ceaselessly until we agreed to bring him to you. Of course, by then, you and I were already aboard the
Rsp’rin
, and⁠—”
“Boom,” Lynn murmured, shaking her head in wonder. “So the teaching position…”
“It was
not
made just to give him something to do to keep him out of our fur, no matter what he claims,” Hirza said. “It is, however, a position that needed filling, and he has the required skillset and experience to fill it. As you know, all humans who wish to be a part of the ISSC must remain productive citizens. He was permitted to leave Earth in order to find you, but if he wishes to stay, he must be employed.”
“Teaching sounded like a better job than some of the others they offered me,” her sire said, rubbing the back of his head. Then he dropped his hand, his expression intent as he studied her. “You’re looking good, Lynn. I heard you had a rough time of it recently. ”
“Not so rough,” she said. “Except for the jungle shark-cats. You would not believe the size of those things.”
“Grrns.” I nodded. They were a threat everything in the forest was wary of.
My speaking drew her sire’s sharp-eyed stare to me. The fur along my tail puffed, but I managed to keep my hackles smooth and my ears relaxed. He narrowed his eyes. I flicked an ear and bared my—I mean, I
smiled
at him.
“Dad,” Lynn said, exasperated. She came to me, tucking herself against my side, where I liked her best outside of our bed. “This is my mate, Zyjii. Zyjii, this is my dad, Paul Davis.”
I made an aggrieved noise before I could think better of it. “What is it with your family and that awful sound.”
Her sire tensed. “Awful⁠—”
Lynn piped up quickly, stopping her father’s anger in its track. “He means the ‘L’ sound. It’s the only part of English he hasn’t mastered yet.”
“I’ve mastered it,” I countered. “I just can’t say it.”
Not all the time, at least. There were certain words I made sure I could say correctly. Like ‘Lynn’ and ‘love.’
…I’d have to practice saying ‘Paul.’
Lynn gave my chest a consoling pat. “Honey, unless you can say it all the time, you can’t say you’ve mastered it.”
My ears relaxed into a lazy, happy slant even as I growled at her. She’d never used an endearment like that before. I liked it.
“If I am your
hun-ee
,” I said, exaggerating the word because it would make her snort—which it did— “does that make you my ee vynla?”
She tilted her head back to look up at me. Whatever she read from my ears and the lazy coil of my tail made her soften. Until she quirked an eyebrow at me, mischief a sparkling light in her not-quite-blue eyes.
“You mean I’m a mystery you’ve yet to solve?” she said tartly.
I barked a laugh. “Is that not true of every male’s mate?”
“Hmm.” She eyed me, clearly unconvinced.
Her sire, though, had relaxed, wearing an odd kind of smile. It took me too long to recognize it as pride and affection. Two things my sire had not showed me in…a very long time. Lynn caught sight of it and flushed.
“Mate, huh?” he said. She cleared her throat, clearly flustered, and his expression softened. “He makes you happy?”
“Yeah, he does.”
He nodded, then stuck a hand out to me. “Then it’s nice to meet you, Zyjii.”
My tail snapped in surprised, but I clapped my hand into his in the gesture Lynn told me was called a
handshake
, and served as a greeting between humans, especially human males.
“It’s nice to meet you, too, sir,” I said.
“That’s wonderful. You’ve all connected,” Hirza deadpanned. She clapped her hands together. “We need to get Lynn and Zyjii settled in their apartment, and then I can leave you lot to your own devices.”
She then started herding us away from the…I forgot the name. Shuttle something. The place where many shuttles landed and let off their passengers before returning to their home ships. Lynn smirked at her vriiryn eared friend before she let us be herded, her sire falling into step on her other side so they could talk and catch up.
I envied her reunion with her sire, but I was happy for her, too.
I let my mind travel back through the stars to my distant home, imagining my sire and Jeejee sharing tales about me as they cleaned their latest hunt. A pang of longing went through me, until a hand, small and with too many fingers, slid into mine and squeezed, reminding me that I had family here, too. The ache in my chest eased, and I tugged Lynn into my side, holding her love for me close.
OceanofPDF.com

Epilogue
LYNN
I checked,
then double checked, to make sure my gift sat neatly in its box. It was something I’d planned to give to Zyjii before we settled on Va'outh, but the
Slipstream
hadn’t had anything to seal wood—weirdly enough, wood wasn’t a common building material used on active spaceships—so I’d had to wait until I was planetside, with an address where I could have an order delivered.
Closing the box, I wrapped it up in a pretty piece of silk I’d picked up during a weekend exploration to one of the many market cities tucked between the massive universities and academies spanning the whole planet. With that done, I went to find my mate.
Zyjii never ceased to amaze me. He settled into life in a city full of advanced technology that blew
my
mind as if he’d grown up there. I think attending his first set of classes helped with that. It astonished him that he could learn any subject he wanted, at any time, and the teachers would answer
all
his questions to the best of their ability.
And if they didn’t have answers for him, then they directed him to study materials or another person that could.
He loved it. The uninhibited learning.
On his days off, like today, he liked to spend his time outside. Hirza had found us a place in a residential quarter known as the The Grove. It catered to the needs of those who didn’t do well separated from nature.
Which basically meant all the buildings were relatively small, discrete, and surrounded by greenery. That included giant trees Hirza assured Zyjii could be climbed as desired—as long as no irreparable harm was done to them.
Claw marks apparently didn’t count, and good thing, because Zyjii wasn’t the only alien there with a penchant for tree climbing.
I didn’t bother trying to hunt him down. I simply scaled the trunk of one of the large trees and tucked myself away on my favorite branch. I think, like Zyjii, I needed time outdoors, surrounded by green things and the sound of wildlife, even if the The Grove itself was groomed and orderly. Usually, I brought a piece of wood to whittle. Something that kept me busy, but didn’t feel like
work
.
Today, I leaned back against the trunk and closed my eyes to wait for my mate to join me. It didn’t take long. But then, it never did.
Something furred slid around my ankle, creeping up my leg, and I smiled.
“Hello, mate,” I said.
“Were you sleeping?” he asked.
I shook my head, but didn’t open my eyes. I lifted the flat, silk wrapped box from my lap.
“For you.”
“This?”
He took it from me, and I cracked my eyes open to watch as he unwrapped the silk. He ran it carefully through his fingers, lifted it to his nose for a sniff, then rubbed it against his cheek.
“Silk,” he said. “Finely woven, but the threads themselves are so fine I can’t imagine how they were spun.” He squinted at me, ears swiveling as he attempted to unravel the puzzle I’d presented him. “This was handmade?”
“Yep. All natural processes and dying methods. No machines or artificial pigments.”
He made an appreciative sound and nuzzled the large kerchief sized square of fabric again. “Must have been expensive.”
Because anything in the ISSC that was genuinely handmade sold at a premium. The galaxy ran on machine created, 3D printed—or the hyper-advanced tech equivalent—or mechanically produced everything. Those with the wealth to spend on it appreciated the imperfect perfection of handcrafted things.
“That was a sample, so it wasn’t so bad,” I said. “That’s not the real gift, anyway.”
He carefully folded the square of silk and tucked it away into one of the pouches on his belt. The pouches no longer held herbs, spice mixes, soak-moss, or any other number of useful things to keep on hand while living in a jungle. Now, they carried his Personal Communication Matrix—so, a cellphone, only fancier—his university assigned tablet, credit chip, and other odd bits and bobs he liked to carry around with him.
Like the necklace he used to always wear. The one strung with teeth, painted wooden beads, and one big claw. He’d stopped wearing it after it got caught in something he’d been tinkering on with Sierva, back on the
Slipstream
, and nearly broke it.
He didn’t want to risk ruining it by wearing it all the time, but it was precious enough he wanted to keep it on him. And so the pouch.
Hopefully what I’d made him would work better.
“What is it?” he asked.
Taking up the box, he gave it an experimental shake. I pressed my lips together hard to keep from snorting. I’d known he’d do that, so I’d padded the thing enough it didn’t make a sound.
“Why not open it and see,” I suggested.
He plonked himself down next to me, his tail doing an excited wiggle. Ears up and intent, he carefully worked the lid off the box, removed the padding, then sat there and stared.
“Do…you like it?” I asked. I nearly bit my lip when I sounded like an uncertain ninny. “It’s a wedding band.” There, that was better. “You climb, and work a lot with your hands, so you can’t really wear it on your finger. It’s, uh, carved from tunjun wood. So you could always wear a little piece of home.”
Oh-so-carefully, he traced a fingertip along the smooth, curved edge of the wooden ring. I didn’t know if it would even fit on his finger. I certainly hadn’t tried to size it, always intending for it to be worn on a cord around his neck.
“It might get caught on something,” he said, looking sad and wistful.
“No, see.” I reached to lift the necklace from the box, but stopped myself, glancing up at him. “May I?”
He nodded.
“Here.” Lifting the necklace out of the box, I unraveled the cord, showing him the two knots. “I made it long enough to fit over your ears—” I hoped “—but you just work these two knots apart and…”
I demonstrated. He cheeped, that deep bass sound he did when truly startled.
Taking the necklace from me, he experimented with the knots, tugging the cord so it stretched to its fullest extent, then sliding the knots, decreasing the length of the loop until they met
at the ring, which I’d fixed in the center with a simple lark’s head knot.
“H-how,” he demanded.
“It’s called a sliding knot.”
And, because I knew my mate, I reached into a pocket and pulled out a length of cord, and demonstrated.
“Two loops around the cord, then through,” I said. “Flip it, and repeat with the other tail.”
I unworked the knots, then handed him the cord. He accepted it, and whipped up the first knot with ease. He was more careful with the second knot, even though the process was the same, and was mighty pleased with himself when it worked as designed. He tucked the cord away in one of his pouches—also not unexpected—before he returned his attention to the ring.
Picking it up by the cord, he set the ring in his palm, then carefully curled his fingers around it.
“This is a human mating mark?” he asked.
I cleared my throat. “You said you wanted one.”
Smiling that open, joyous smile I so adored, he tipped forward, pressing his forehead to mine.
“My Lynn,” he murmured. “Thank you.”
I cradled the side of his face in my hand, closing my eyes as I basked in his delight. When he straightened, I let him go, mostly so I could help him get the necklace on and settled where he wanted it. I stood and moved behind him. He passed me the necklace, and I looped it over his head, taking particular care with his long, expressive ears.
“How tight?” I asked.
“There,” he said.
I tipped forward to check. The ring sat right beneath the hollow at the base of his throat.
“Looks good,” I said, my voice a little raspy. Seeing him wear the ring I’d carved for him hit my possessive cavewoman right in the feels.
Reaching back he grabbed me and hauled me over his shoulder and into his lap, helped along by a little boost from his wings.
I might have let out a cheep of surprise.
He folded himself around me, holding me close. Touched but also a little confused, I stroked his head.
“I love you,” he said simply. “I’m so glad I got to come to this place with you.”
He lifted his head, staring around the leafy shade of The Grove. Not too far in the distance, ground vehicles zipped about along carefully routed roads. Overhead, giant wings beat the air as flight capable aliens carried themselves to class or the market or one of the many, many restaurants littered about.
Zyjii’s awe remained as fresh and vast as when he first stepped into Hirza’s rescue shuttle and confronted a holo-image of my old boss, T’rlkiax. He’d gotten better at keeping it hidden so strangers wouldn’t judge him for it, but he’d never let that awe fade.
And then he turned that wonder on me.
“My sky-fallen female,” he said, and smiled. “You continue to be the most incredible thing I’ve ever encountered in the forest. Or away from it.”
Huffing to hide my embarrassment, I buried my face against his neck. He laughed and tucked me closer.
“And you’re so incredible I had to cross the galaxy to find you,” I mumbled.
He purred his happiness at me.
We remained there for a while, simply holding each other. Eventually, we climbed down the tree and headed home to share our wonder of each other in private.
OceanofPDF.com

About the Book
Hello! Welcome to the end of the book, to the place where I tend to ramble about…the book. Clearly.
Ahem.
This book holds a special place in my heart. I’m a pretty voracious reader, and a lot of the sci-fi romance books I’ve picked up involve survival elements when the cast of characters end up stranded on an uninhabited or primitive planet.
It’s just…the FL never seems to know what to do. She always,
always
ends up relying on the ML to take care of her. And frankly, that bothers the piss out of me.
You have to understand, I live in a mountainous region where even the locals end up lost with no supplies and no choice but to sit tight and survive the best they can until rescue can find them. Learning the basics of surviving the wilds (how to find water, make shelter, stay warm) feels like it was such a basic and ingrained part of my childhood that it honestly stuns me when people
don’t know
.
Like, what do you mean you don’t know to dig yourself a little den in the snow during a blizzard? I thought that was common knowledge!
(Friends, this is
not
common knowledge in places where they, you know, don’t
have
snow. I’m aware of this, even as it continues to boggle my little neuro-spicy brain.)
*Coughs* Anyway.
I wanted to read a story about a woman with the knowledge and skills necessary to survive stranded on a strange planet on her own. Since I couldn’t find one, I decided to write one myself.
Ah, the song of authors everywhere.
And that’s the story of how this book came to be. There will probably be more like it in the future, though I doubt any of the future FLs will have quite the same depth of knowledge as Lynn. Lynn’s upbringing was rather, uh,
unique
, and was mostly just an excuse for me to show off my own magpie collection of survival skills.
If this book wasn’t your cup of tea, then no worries! The next couple of books are going to lean much harder on the sci-fi part of the sci-fi romance genre. We’ll get to know more about the ISSC and the various alien species within it. I’m looking forward to exploring the Milky Way Galaxy and all the potential weirdness contained within the ISSC.
If you haven’t yet, be sure to rate and review the book! It’ll help me a bunch, and also ensure there’ll be plenty more books like this in the future.
OceanofPDF.com

A Note on Language
First off, you need to know that I’m something of a language nerd. English is (tragically) the only language I speak, but that doesn’t stop me from nerding out over the interesting linguistic quirks of other languages. Mostly Japanese, since I have an unhealthy obsession with anime, and that’s where I got some of Zyjii’s issues when it came to learning English.
Lynn mentions that Zyjii’s language doesn’t have an L sound, and yet it still appears in a few words here and there. Like:
Zvillyn
Kicluck
Ee vynla
Mlyrk
Lyvchiss
This wasn’t a mistake on my part, promise. Mrriiv
do
have an L sound. Kind of. It’s more of a soft purr, as opposed to the rolling growl of their R (like in
grrn
). There’s a distinct difference (to
Zyjii’s ear) the same way there’s a difference between R and L for native English speakers.
Since this is a work of fiction, let’s say the reason
kicluck
and
zvillyn
are spelled with the dread L is because Zyjii’s people don’t have a written language yet, and so all spellings of Mrriiv words are rough, phonetic approximations Lynn made up to help flesh out the ISSC’s linguistics database. She probably poked at Zyjii to get him to say the words over and over, until he got fed up with her and told her “spell it however you wish.
I
know the proper way to say it,” and then continued to tease her by growling all the words she had trouble pronouncing when they were first trying to figure out how to communicate with each other.
So, there you have it. The use of L when making up words for the Mrriiv’s language wasn’t a mistake at all, but rather a carefully curated issue to serve as a stumbling-block-turned-inside-joke for our couple.
Other Alien Languages in this Book
The Vrigrix are…an interesting combination of deep sea creature, ram, and insect. More or less. Their language is even more impossible for us pitiful humans to replicate, since it consists of a series of rasps, clicks, and the kind of chirping crickets make.
I’ve done my best to capture the essence of that in a phonetic form for English readers. That said, the apostrophes in Vrigrixic words and names aren’t there just to make things “look more alien.” They serve a linguistic purpose. (Because I’m a nerd, remember.) In this case, they’re a hard click.
So T’rlikiax would be Teh-(click)-ril-kee-ax
(Or, you can soften it to Tehk-ril-kee-ax, which is what I do, since it’s a lot easier on my poor monolingual tongue.
)
This is not to be confused with the apostrophes in Fala’ahn words. Theirs is a much softer language, so the apostrophes are used to indicate a soft glottal stop to separate vowel sounds.
If you have any questions about pronunciation or anything else, please feel free to use the
contact form
on my website. If I get enough requests, maybe I’ll cobble together a glossary or something.

Chapter One
LYNN
One more.
Just one more, and I’d be done for the day.
I snorted. God, I sucked at lying. Even to myself.
Because while I had only one more job interview, I definitely would
not
be done for the day. Living in a big city like Chicago was expensive. More expensive than I had anticipated.
No surprise there, considering. Growing up in the backwoods of Montana did not prepare one for any kind of city life, yet I was determined to make it work. Somehow. Before my time ran up and I had to return to my dad’s little cabin in the middle of nowhere.
I strode into the lobby of the sleekly modern building hosting my last interview for the day as if I belonged, completely ignoring the fact my entire off-the-rack suit dress probably cost less than the secretary’s gleaming tie. Was that silk? It was probably silk.
I was most of the way to his desk when one of my spiked heels slipped on the gleaming white marble floor. My ankle wrenched, I tripped, flailed, and let out a very loud, very unladylike curse before I managed to catch my balance.
When I straightened, the solid metal barrel of my functional accessory—aka a fire starter disguised as a pendant—thumped against my chest. I quickly tucked it down my shirt again, glancing up to find the secretary out of his chair, leaning over the desk as if he’d tried to catch me.
Sweet, but unnecessary. Also not feasible, what with the massive slab of gleaming granite countertop between us.
“Are you all right?” he asked.
I jerked my head in a nod.
More than once—
especially
since arriving in Illinois—I’d been told I should smile more. One of the other waitresses at my part-time job had actually pulled me to the side mid-shift during my first week to ask me if everything was all right.
“Yes, of course,” I’d assured her. “Why?”
“You just look so angry.”
I’d frowned. “This is the way I always look.”
She’d hemmed and hawed, and finally gave me that dreaded, hateful line.
Resting bitch face was a real thing, and I had it in spades. Fair, since it matched my absolutely sparkling personality.
Still, I was there for an interview, and my allotted six months to land a full-time salaried job were nearly up. So I ignored the ding to my pride and plastered on a smile.
“I’m fine, thank you,” I said.
The poor secretary paled and hurriedly reclaimed his seat. “Good. That’s…good.”
I sighed and dropped the smile. Smiling was stupid anyway. It was something people did when they were happy, but who’d be happy after spending seven hours trekking around this godforsaken city in five inch spike-heels, carting around a folder full of carefully crafted resumés that no one ever bothered to read, unable to find a position working in a cubicle maze because I didn’t ‘look friendly enough’?
That was a real criticism I’d gotten after one of my first interviews. At least they’d had the decency to say it to my face before kicking me out.
“I’m Lynn Davis?” I said now, when the secretary continued to sit there and stare at me like I was a kodiak bear and not someone way, way out of her element. I felt one eyebrow creep upward and tried to stop it. “I have an interview with a Ms Hirza.”
He blinked once, then visibly shifted into work-mode. “Right. Yes. Of course. You’re a little early, but she’s ready to see you now, if you want head back to the interview room.”
He gave me directions. I thanked him politely—no smile, since I honestly didn’t want to traumatize the poor man any further—then strode away, walking with a lot more care to avoid a repeat of the slipped heel incident.
The woman waiting for me in the cozy, minimalist interview room was everything I aspired to be. She exuded serene elegance, from her flawlessly coiffed fox red hair to her long, french manicured nails. Her commanding presence elevated the room from bland and spartan to something more suitable for stupidly rich CEOs to conduct top-secret business meetings in.
I’d admit it. I was intimidated.
My nerves did not ease up at all at her friendly smile. Instead, the small hairs at the back of my neck rose, like hackles, as a familiar tingle ran down my spine. I recognized a predator when I saw one. Even one dressed in a billion dollar bespoke dress suit.
“Ms Jones,” she said, holding out her hand for me to shake. “I’m Hirza.”
I accepted it, pleased by the firmness of her grip. You’d be surprised at how many women had no idea how to do a proper handshake.
She gestured behind her. “Please, have a seat.”
There were only two chairs in the smallish room, a low coffee table centered between them. I was clueless when it came to identifying wood through grain or whatever, but I could tell it was real, solid wood, not cheap veneer over plywood.
If I hadn’t been impressed before, I definitely was now. My chances of being hired by an obviously top tier company were approximately nil.
That didn’t stop me from plopping into the chair Hirza indicated and mentally reviewing questions and answers. I nodded firmly. I was ready. I could do this.
Except I couldn’t shake the odd feeling that Hirza—I wondered if that was her first name, surname, or only name, like Madonna—was a lot more dangerous than the grizzly my father made me hunt when I was fourteen.
“I got the job!”
It was the first thing I said when I burst into work—my part-time waitressing work, not my new chic work in HR. Jessica, the closest thing I had to a friend, squealed. She started to go in for a hug, but hesitated, because I’d made it pretty clear I didn’t like being touched.
I was just glad I hadn’t actually broken Bill’s arm when he grabbed me unexpectedly from behind. He had, however, managed to convince the other men working the kitchen that I was vicious and had attacked him unprovoked, so now they all avoided me like the plague.
Which was fine. Really. I knew when I started working at the little mom and pop diner that my employment there would be temporary. One way or another.
At that moment, I was so full of happy-feel-goods, my usual reserve went out the window. I grabbed Jessica’s hands and bounced like a little jumping mouse. I didn’t squeal, but it was a near thing.
“Congratulations!” she cried, bouncing with me. She gave me a sly look. “I suppose this is your two week notice?”
I nodded. Hirza had mentioned I might start as early as tomorrow, but if that happened, I’d simply rearrange my schedule with the diner’s manager to take shifts after I got off work. It’d make for a rough two weeks, but I’d survived worse.
A month alone in the backwoods of Montana, in late autumn, armed with nothing but my favorite bowie knife, Mr McStaberson, to help me survive definitely counted as
worse
.
Scheduling issues were a worry for later, though. Right now, I had a four hour waitressing shift to get through.
I swear to god and all that was holy, if
one more person
commented on how much nicer I looked when I was genuinely happy, I was going to stab someone with a fork.
Fortunately for my coworkers and all the customers, my shifted ended before I was driven to violence.
My earlier good mood completely gone, I wadded up my apron and threw it in the laundry hamper, swapped my low-heeled pumps for my well-worn hiking shoes, grabbed my purse from the locker—leaving behind the folder of resumés because I didn’t need it anymore and didn’t want to deal with carrying it home tonight—and slammed out the backdoor.
My scowl was fierce enough that the group of rough looking men who hung around the parking lot and liked to heckle the waitresses scattered before me like pigeons fleeing a stalking cat.
I’d asked Jessica once if she wanted me to do something about them. Jessica had given me a blank look that had quickly morphed into one of horror tinged worry. It’d amused me to learn the worry had been for the men.
Apparently, they acted as a kind of buffer. They were crude and annoying, but they weren’t mean, and they never touched any of the waitresses. Except once, when they’d arrived to find one of the girl’s being beaten on by her abusive ex. While three of the parking lot loiters beat the ever living shit out of the ex, the fourth escorted her with one of her hands in his and his other hand at her back. He’d stayed with her in the diner until her friend came to pick her up.
And they’d made a point of hanging around ever since. Since they protected the other waitresses in their own rough way, no one protested them being there, and no one called in to report their more legally questionable activities.
For the other waitresses, a bit of friendly heckling and cat calling seemed a small enough price to pay for a bit of security.
The assholes never heckled
me
. They didn’t dare. I was, and I quote, ‘One scary ass bitch.’ Which was hilarious when you considered the fact I was a petite blonde girl with the curves of a two-by-four.
It was fine. It wasn’t like I wanted complete strangers whistling and jeering at me.
But, damnit, it still stung a bit. And after four hours of hearing ‘Oh, wow, you’re
so much prettier
when you smile,’ I really could have used the confidence boost.
“Chickenshit assholes,” I muttered as I stalked down the sidewalk.
Stupid tears welled in my stupid eyes, and I swiped them away. More than anything, I hated the fact I always got teary when I was exhausted. I’d been on my feet for nearly ten hours, and I was fucking
done
.
I muttered all the way to the bus stop. I checked the schedule and cursed.
Of course, I’d managed to miss the last bus. It was only four miles to my apartment—an easy hour’s walk—but damnit, even in my comfy hiking shoes, my feet throbbed in time with my heartbeat, a deep ache that spread up my calves and threatened to start stabbing my kneecaps. Another hour on my feet might just result in the need for emergency amputation.
I opened my purse and stared at the contents. There was some cash in there, but not much, since most people paid, and tipped, electronically. I had my tip card—how the diner paid out electronic tips—but I didn’t trust the thing. I could thank my father for that particular bit of paranoia.
Actually, I could thank Dear Old Dad for all the bits of paranoia that made me ‘weird’ and ‘quirky.’
Sighing, I pulled out my knife—just a rinky-dink little pocket knife, but I kept the blade razor sharp, and it’d be enough to pierce a neck or stab out the eye of an attacker—then closed my purse and shouldered it, as grim as any soldier preparing for battle. It was only four miles. Less than an hour if I kept my pace brisk.
The streetlight above me flickered and went out. My hand tightened around the pocket knife, and I thumbed the blade open, just in case.
If I ran it, I could half the time it’d take to reach my apartment. I experimentally shifted my weight from one foot to the other, and winced. Yeah. No. I
could
run, but it wouldn’t be pleasant. Best save that for if someone decided to chase me.
Nodding once to myself, I adjusted my grip on my knife and set off. I’d barely gone three steps when some asshole’s high-beams hit me right in the eyes. I snarled and lifted my arm to block the light out, except I couldn’t move. No way I was suffering from deer-in-the-headlights syndrome.
Wait. I stood on the sidewalk. There shouldn’t be headlights directly in my eyes.
For a moment, I swear I went weightless, my feet lifting off the pavement, even though nothing had touched me. There was no pain, no crunch of glass. Just the light, the sensation of floating, then nothing.
OceanofPDF.com

MY PRIMITIVE ALIEN EXILE
Hired by Aliens
Book 1
LINDSAY FOX
OceanofPDF.com

Chapter Two
LYNN
Sunlight made
the inside of my eyelids glow reddish-yellow. Groaning, I tried to roll over and bury my head under my pillow so I could sleep longer, except I couldn’t move. Something banded over my chest and arms held me in place.
Trapped.
Adrenaline dumped through my veins and I threw myself to the side as hard as I could, wondering what fresh hell my dad had planned for me now.
“Release the straps,” a vaguely familiar voice snapped. “Before she hurts herself.”
The resistance holding me back vanished, and I tumbled to the floor.
“She’s going to hurt herself regardless,” a sweet, weirdly musical voice muttered.
Scrambling away from the surface I’d been trapped on, I shoved myself to my feet, determined to arm myself, only to gasp as bright daggers of agony speared through my head. Caught completely off guard by the pain—Dad had
never
hurt me before—my legs went noodle-y and my knees hit the ground.
Movement in my blurry vision snared my attention. I bared my teeth and growled. The hand reaching for me froze, but didn’t retreat.
“You’re safe, Ms Davis,” that vaguely familiar voice said.
“If I’m safe, then why does it feel like you jammed an ice-pick in my skull?” I demanded.
A fluting trill sounded from somewhere on the other side of the—I squinted suspiciously—floating slab I’d fallen off of. The high pitched sound should have made my ears throb, but instead, it eased my headache enough it no longer felt like my eyes were going to burst in their sockets.
“Fee?” the voice close to me said.
The flute made a distressed sound. “Installation shouldn’t cause pain. Perhaps it’s the integration? This is a new model, and hasn’t been tested on a human yet.”
Oh, great. So I got to be someone’s guinea pig.
I stifled a snort, squeezing my eyes shut as I focused on breathing past the pain.
I froze as realization pinged through me.
Installation?
What had they done to me? Maybe more importantly, who was
they?
Correctly reading the stiffness in my shoulders as rising panic, the vaguely familiar one said, “We gave you a translation chip. And you
did
sign the papers giving us permission to install it.”
“No, I didn’t,” I growled.
“You did,” she—definitely a she—insisted.
I risked having the light try to murder me through my eyeballs to glare at the speaker. Who knelt on the floor near me.
As soon as the hazy blur across my vision cleared, I recognized her. Hirza no last name. From my new job. Except the put-together business woman had been replaced by something from one of those Japanese anime. The human aspects were all there; head, neck, shoulders. Two arms, two
legs, five-fingered hands. Two eyes and ears, a nose, and a mouth.
Just…the ears stood up straight from the top of her head and were distinctly fox-like. Her face was shaped oddly, turning her nose—a small dark triangle, again, not dissimilar to a fox’s—and mouth into a brief muzzle. And her eyes. The pupils were a brilliant, gem blue in a pale grey iris. They were also slitted like a cat’s.
But what really got me were the tails. Yes, tail
s
, plural. Three of them moved behind her, twisting together in what my achy brain translated as akin to nervous hand-wringing.
Taking a slow, deep breath, I touched the tip of each finger to my thumb, counting as I went. Five fingers. Excruciating pain in my head rapidly subsiding to a dull throb. Same clothes I’d worn home from my shift at the diner, including my old, reliable hiking shoes—never boots, except in winter. Wearing boots could lead to weakened ankles, and⁠—
Not the point.
For my final reality check, I fumbled at my neck, finding the sturdy chain and the hefty metal ‘pendant’ attached to it. For a second I considered pulling it off, unscrewing the cap, and testing the strike stick to see if it’d actually produce sparks, but considering we were indoors, I decided against it.
All proof said I wasn’t dreaming. Which left me in something of a shitty situation.
“Okay,” I said with enough calm Hirza’s tails stilled and…quivered. “What are you? Where am I? And why did you take me and jam a translator in my head?”
An hour later, I sat alone in the room Hirza led me to. It was bigger than my tiny apartment, but not by much. I sat in the single chair, staring at the solitary window. Porthole. It was small, round, and set in the wall of a fucking
spaceship
.
So, porthole.
And I was on said spaceship, thousands of lightyears away from home, somewhere on the other side of the Milky Way Galaxy. Because I’d apparently failed to read the fine print on the massive stack of paperwork Hirza had gone through with me at the end of my interview where it said I’d been hired by aliens.
Actual
aliens
.
What did aliens need with someone who wanted to work in human resources, you might ask? Because I sure as hell did. My first assumption had been the kind of resources you packed on a backpacking trip. Food. Water. The basics.
Hirza had been genuinely horrified by the idea, which made me feel vastly better about the whole situation.
I’d been hired, she told me, by the branch of the Intragalactic Star-System Coalition—the ISSC for short—that covered things like conservation of primitive worlds and the protection of rapidly developing alien societies.
Lucky me, humanity was on the severe watch list because of those tech-bros who couldn’t resist fucking over our planet in order to progress AI. Something that was, apparently, a huge no-no as far as the ISSC was concerned.
My job, Hirza explained, was to work with Ambassador T’rlkiax and act as a kind of representative of humanity.
(I spent two hours practicing that name because there was a weird throat-click right at the beginning that wasn’t used in English at all, and I wasn’t about to offend the alien in charge of fighting to keep my home planet from being invaded in a semi-hostile takeover by saying his name wrong.)
It was my job to answer every question the ambassador had about humanity and Earth, our development of AI, our space programs, and…pretty much anything and everything he could think of.
Me. The girl from the backwoods of Montana who barely managed to scrape up a GED and the necessary college degree to get into HR. I knew enough about computers and modern tech to be able to use my smartphone to make calls, send texts, and pull up directions on the map thingy when I didn’t have a paper copy and compass handy, but that was it. The only thing I knew about AI was that it was a massive resource drain, and just
one
of the damn things generated more carbon emissions in a month than over two-hundred fifty flights from New York to London.
I stared out the porthole at a night sky I’d never seen before. Stars. So many stars, and they were so bright. The ship apparently didn’t produce much light pollution, if any, and because there was no planetary atmosphere wherever the hell we were…
No obstructions. Nothing to diffuse the brilliant star light. As the ship moved, the cloudy ribbon of our galaxy filled my porthole. I’d seen the Milky Way back home, but not like this. Here, in space, I felt like I could reach out and sweep my hand through the multi-hued stardust and collect it on my fingertips.
I blew out a shaking breath. I had to wonder if any of the distant little specks were planets, and how far we were from the nearest one. Because, frankly, the idea of being in dark space, probably billions of miles from the nearest source of solid ground and breathable air scared me spitless.
I’d never suffered agoraphobia before, but I think I understood it now.
Shuddering, I pointedly turned my back on the porthole, drew my feet up onto the chair, and buried my face against my knees.
I wasn’t, by nature, a pessimistic person. I definitely wasn’t a
look on the bright side
kind of person, either. No, I was more of an
assess the situation, determine the risks, and plot a course of action
kind of gal.
The first was pretty easy. I was somewhere in the Milky Way Galaxy, but nowhere even close to home, with no way to get there without help from the aliens who’d abducted me. I was alone, without resources beyond the clothes on my back and my little fire starter pendant. They’d confiscated my phone and credit cards as contraband. I imagine they would have done the same with my necklace if they’d realized it was anything more than a hefty piece of jewelry.
Determining the risks was more challenging, mostly because my life wasn’t the only one I had to factor into things. If I messed up and made humanity seem like any kind of threat to the wider galaxy, it could mean bad,
bad
things for Earth.
No pressure or anything.
Setting the staggering weight of the risks inherent with my current situation—both to me personally and to humanity as a whole—I worked on plotting a course of action. That was a bit easier, since my choices were pretty limited.
I could refuse to work with the ISSC and Hirza, but that wouldn’t change anything. Now that their super advanced translation chip had integrated with my brain, there was no way to remove it without killing me, and they couldn’t return me to Earth and risk having it discovered.
Since I had zero desire to end up as a government lab rat, I agreed that going home was off the table.
That left me with exactly one choice. Make the best of it, put my best foot forward, and do the job I’d been hired to do.
“I should probably warn you,” Hirza said as she led me through the weirdly pristine halls of the spaceship, on the way to meet my new boss. “T’rlkiax is a Vrigrix. I’ve watched most of your science fiction movies in an attempt to prepare myself for debunking any false assumptions your kind have developed about us.”
That seemed like something of a non sequitur. Until I thought about what kind of sci-fi movies she might have seen, and why she might bring them up in relation to my boss.
“He’s one of those little grey aliens with the big heads and touchy about it,” I guessed.
She gave me an amused glance. I appreciated the fact her face was still humanoid enough to have facial expressions I could read. And if I found the constant waving of her tails distracting, that one was on me.
“No. There are no such beings that we’ve come across. There is no evolutionary path in which it makes sense for a being to develop a head that so vastly outweighs their body it’d likely crush their vertebrae.”
I grunted. Fair. And scientifically logical, which my pragmatic soul appreciated.
“No, the Vrigrix bear an uncanny resemblance to the aliens your people named Xenomorphs.”
I stared at her so hard I nearly tripped over my own feet. “Seriously?”
“More or less,” she said. Then her tails all twined together before spreading apart in a nervous wave. “Definitely less. They have a black exoskeletal-like structure and are facultative bipedal, but that’s where the similarities end.”
“I have no idea what ‘facultative’ means,” I admitted.
She smiled. The expression itself was surprisingly gentle, but the sharp teeth made it look more than a little threatening.
“It means that while he’s largely bipedal, he’s also capable of quadrupedal locomotion.” Her smile vanished, replaced by something somber. “Be wary of the Vrigrix, Ms Davis. And if you
ever
see one drop to all fours, I suggest you do everything in your power to get out of their way and pray to whatever deities you believe in that you’re not the target of their ire.”
So I was employed by a pseudo-Xenomorph who could trample me flat without effort. Noted.
I took a deep breath and reassessed the risks of my current situation. Things were not looking good. But I could make it work. I
would
make it work. My dad didn’t raise a timid mouse. He gave me all the tools I needed to survive, no matter the environment. More than that, he taught me the importance of not letting fear rule me.
I nodded once. It was time to get to it.

Chapter Three
LYNN
1 year
later
“Sir, we’re approaching the jump point,” I said. I checked the display on my holo watch and continued. “You have five minutes.”
My boss, Ambassador T’rlkiax, waved a tentacle in my direction to let me know he’d heard.
The motion no longer creeped me out. Despite Hirza’s long ago warning, meeting my boss had been something of a shock. Vrigrix were predators on a galactic scale, and it showed. Skin as hard as plate armor, joints designed for the kind of piston force that made mantis shrimp famous, tentacles, horns. They had it all.
Oh, and don’t forget the venomous, retractable spines. Or their lipless faces that looked like demonic, grinning skulls.
Vrigrix were hands down the most terrifying looking aliens I’d run into over the course of the last year—Earth time, more like 2.5 years standard ISSC time. The only thing that saved them from being villains of the worst sort was a core value
founded upon a deeply held belief in honor, and there was no honor in destroying the weak.
Unsurprisingly, the Vrigrix made up the largest percentage of the ISSC’s fighting force. My boss was considered an anomaly. His horns were smaller than average, but there were more of them, and that meant something to the Vrigriz that equated to charismatic strength rather than physical, or something.
I did my best to learn everything I could, but there were so many different alien cultures and customs that they still tended to blur together, the details either lost on me completely or getting constantly swapped around in my head.
At least I, as a human, rated as
weak, needs protection
on the Vrigrix scale of predator vs prey. T’rlkiax had been surprisingly patient with me, his corrections firm but gentle. He never yelled, even when I screwed up big time, and more than once, he’d defended me from the backlash of others.
It made it easy for me to give everything I had into doing my part to make his job easier.
And that included shooing him off an intragalactic phone call that threatened to drag past the point of safety.
“Shall I disconnect you, sir?” I asked.
He slapped the tips of his top two tentacles together in what I’d come to learn was the Vrigrix equivalent of baring their teeth in a threatening-yet-still-playful smile.
The end of a third tentacle pressed the section of his desk that muted the call.
“Don’t you dare,” he hissed. “This is important.”
“Jump in two minutes, sir,” I reminded him.
Vrigrix didn’t sigh, but the wilting of his tentacles conveyed the same general meaning.
“I must go,” my boss told the holographic display of a Kritallin.
Interesting beings, Kritallin. I was sure there was a scientific explanation for why they had crystals growing out of their bodies, but I didn’t know it. Probably wouldn’t understand it, even if someone explained it to me using very small words.
“Fine,” the Kritallin said. “Be safe, my friend.”
“We shall see,” T’rlkiax said in a dark rasping hiss that immediately put my hackles up.
My boss was not one for dramatics. That kind of statement, delivered in that tone of voice, raised every single one of my red flags and started waving them in a complicated, eye-catching color guard dance routine.
While my boss wrapped up his interstellar face-time call, I finished prepping his office to make the jump into folded space. The jump was supposed to be a seamless experience, but sometimes physics got a little wonky right at the transition point and made a mess of anything not nailed down. If there was one thing the ambassador wouldn’t tolerate, it was mess of any kind.
T’rlkiax shut down the view screen and strapped himself into his seat with exactly thirty seconds left to jump. I went to the wall and slid my fingers along a section that looked indistinguishable from the rest of the pure white expanse, and a jump chair emerged. I settled myself in, touching my watch, then my ear.
The first time my boss had caught me doing it, he’d gently reassured me that my translator would be fine. All ISSC tech was designed to survive jumping and the scientific shenanigans that went on in folded space. The chip being integrated into a living nervous system served as additional protection.
It’d been the reassurance I needed, but even though I no longer worried about my translator exploding my brain, the touch had become habit.
“We should brace ourselves for emergence from the fold,” T’rlkiax said suddenly. “Things might get a little rough.”
That flag-waving color guard team added flashing neon lights to their performance.
My eyes went wide with alarm, but before I could ask what he meant by ‘rough’ the ship jumped.
I didn’t like folding. Imagine being crumpled like a piece of overworked origami paper, crunched into a wad and tossed into the garbage,
and
feeling like a piece of fresh taffy, stretched well beyond the breaking point, all at once.
Now imagine it sucking about a hundred times worse than that.
It took me three months to stop being sick after re-emerging into real space.
This time was no different. Every atom of my body simultaneously compressed down into a single point of agony and spread apart until I worried my soul would rip free and be lost forever in the spaces stretched between stars. I counted the sluggish, painful beats of my heart to keep my body’s instinctive panic at bay. One, two, three.
After nine, there was a bright
snap
and reality righted itself. I sucked in a gasping, shuddering breath, hands clenched tight around the edges of my seat. T’rlkiax chittered his stress, his tentacles stretched to cling to the ceiling, floor, and his desk, before he wrangled them under control.
“I hate that,” I said.
“I, as well.” Shuddering hard enough to make his tentacles rub together with a sound like cricket song, he started to undo his chair’s jump straps. “And they say it doesn’t matter how much you travel. You never get used to it.”
“Peachy,” I muttered.
I managed to undo my own straps and stood, stretching out the kinks from my muscles. A sharp alarm blared, startling me. It was immediately followed by the entire room jolting hard enough to throw me to the floor.
“What—” I started to yell, but the office door sprang open and Hirza flowed through with a grace and speed that was utterly inhuman.
She met my boss’s gaze for a brief second, then hurried to me.
“Up,” she said. She grabbed my arm and hauled me to my feet with the same effort I might use picking up a discarded stuffed toy. “Come on. Up, Lynn. We have to go.”
I looked back toward T’rlkiax. “But⁠—”
“I am right behind you,” he said, even as he started using all his tentacles to eject memory chips from his desk.
He whipped one toward Hirza with one of those startlingly fast, liquid motions that still tended to make my lizard brain gibber in panic. Her fingers closed around the chip, and then we were running. Or she was running, dragging me along behind her with an iron-hard grip around my wrist.
The ship shuddered and heaved beneath us. I stumbled, slamming a shoulder into the wall. Somehow, Hirza kept her feet. Muttering a curse, she popped the chip into her mouth, then spun around and snatched me up. Then she
ran
.
The hallway blurred, the flashing red emergency lights becoming a single long tunnel, like the throat of some giant beast swallowing us straight to hell. She skidded to a stop with an abruptness that nearly gave me whiplash, then tossed me through a surprisingly small doorway.
“Strap yourself in and hit the blue button,” she snapped.
“What about⁠—”
“Don’t argue, Lynn,” she said. “The pod only seats one. I’ll be in the one right next to you.”
Swallowing back the tight knot of fear, I nodded and did as ordered. As soon as she stepped away from the door, it slid silently shut. I sat, my hands steady as they made quick work of the safety straps. The last buckle clicked into place, and I slammed my open palm down on the large blue button.
“Escape pod ejection protocol initiated,” a computerized voice said in Vrigrixic. “Launch in three. Two. One.”
I braced myself for the propulsion to smash me back into the seat, but nothing happened. Panicked, I checked the controls, but I’d left my text-translating glasses…somewhere. Damn it! Hirza had talked me into getting the contacts version, but I couldn’t wear them for more than three minutes before suffering some kind of serious allergic reaction the med techs had never seen before. Now, the one time I really needed their help, I didn’t have them.
“Computer, turn on view port!” I commanded, my voice high.
Panic is a good way to get dead, Lynn. Control your fear. Don’t let it control you
.
My dad’s voice was so strong, so immediate, I looked over my shoulder to see if his glowing ghost stood there, Jedi style.
But no. I was alone in an escape pod thousands of lightyears away from home, and my dad thought I died in a bus accident.
The blank expanse of wall in front of me suddenly cleared to show a view of a huge blue-green planet taking up most of the screen. My pod had definitely cleared the ship, then. I took a deep breath, forcing my lungs to expand just beyond the point of comfort. Hold for thirty. Then I blew all the air out in one rushing exhale.
Better. At least my hands had stopped shaking
“Computer, can you angle the pod so we can see the ship?”
“Affirmative. Positional correction initiated.”
I didn’t feel the pod move, but the planet shifted out of view, replaced by a distant purple moon, and then the ship itself. For
all their insectile-tentacled weirdness, the Vrigrix loved sleek, gracefully curved lines, and it showed in their engineering. The ambassadorial cruise ship
Rsp’rin
looked like the love child between one of the boats from the
Lord of the Rings
movies and a ship from
Star Trek
. The simple, organic lines of it glittered against the black backdrop of space.
It was also broken into pieces. I stared, heart pounding, as another chunk of it just disintegrated, scattering debris like sparks from a firework.
“Computer, can you see what’s attacking the
Rsp’rin
?” I asked.
“Negative. Scans suggest if the mothership is under attack, it is being done by a vessel under a complex cloaking shield, using cloaked ammunition.”
I bit my lip, watching and waiting for more pods to eject.
And waiting.
Something tore through the center of the ship, scattering more debris. Sound didn’t travel through space, but I swear the universe held its breath in the brief instant between impact and explosion.
The
Rsp’rin
died a beautiful death. She lit up like the heart of a star, surrounded by a halo of blue-white light. The corona swept over my pod, setting the emergency alarms blaring, the whole pod shaking like a row bow at sea in a hurricane, but my eyes remained locked on the view-port as the blazing ball of light that had once been the height of Vrigrix technology and design sucked into itself and winked out of existence.
I jerked out of my dazed fascination with the destruction of the
Rsp’rin
—trying not to think about all the people who hadn’t escaped—to focus on more immediate problems.
Like my survival.
“Warning,” the computer said in a voice so calm it caused a whiplash of cognitive dissonance. “High velocity electrical
impulse detected. Shielding has dropped below recommended percentage levels.”
“What’s it at?” I demanded, yelling to hear myself over the continuing blare of sirens.
“Fifteen percent.”
I sucked in a sharp breath. That was bad, right? I tried to remember the safety courses and emergency drills Ambassador T’rlkiax insisted his staff run once every three months, but shock made my thoughts sluggish.
Recommended shield levels bottomed out at twenty-five percent, because that was the bare minimum required to survive anything from a photon blaster to a strike from a pod-sized piece of space debris. It was also the minimum shield level recommended for attempting atmospheric re-entry.
Not that I needed to worry about that. The pod’s computer would work to keep us in orbit around that big green-blue planet to make pick-up easier. We⁠—
A sound I remembered from Earth. A horrifically loud crunch as metal crumpled and glass shattered and tires squealed. The whole pod jerked, snapping my head to the side to smack into the head support hard enough to leave my ears ringing and my vision fuzzy.
“Warning,” the computer said in that same, ridiculously calm voice, “impact with unknown space debris has caused catastrophic damage to the hull of EP-127. Recommended action: Emergency landing on planet CTX-897615.”
“Is that the big planet right outside the view-port?” I asked on a gasp.
“Affirmative. Preliminary scans of planet CTX-897615 show signs of compatibility to support all oxygen breathing lifeforms currently listed on Mothership
Rsp’rin
’s manifest.”
I bit my lip, eyes shut against the strobing red lights and blaring sirens. It hurt to think.
“What are the chances of successful re-entry?” I asked.
“Calculating…error, can not calculate.”
I stared at the screen which showed nothing but an expanse of black.
“Wha—why can’t you calculate it?” I demanded.
“I seem to be experiencing multiple, unexpected, undefined system failures. Directional thrusters are off line. The previous impact has knocked EP-127 off the predetermined course. Shall I attempt to recalculate a potential flight path?”
“No.”
With its thrusters offline and its systems already running errors, what was the point?
“Oh, fuck,” I whispered. “I’m going to die.”
