Chapter Four
ZYJII
The sky’s
anger startled me out of a sound sleep.
The echoing roar reminded me of the constant storms that heralded the wet season, but that was a step in the Sisters’ dance still a long way out. Wild storms weren’t uncommon, but no rain pounded the lush forest beyond the shelter of my den, so that wasn’t what made the angry sound.
Leaving the warmth of my nest, I swung out of my den and up onto the branch sheltering the entrance. I climbed quickly, taking the familiar path to the best branch to catch a glimpse of the sky-lights, but tonight they were hiding from the brilliant glow that seared through the darkness like a good knife through too-ripe fruit.
My breath caught as I watched the falling sky-light—for what else could it be, even if I’d never seen one that size before—streak toward the forest. I didn’t see its impact, but I felt it. The whole forest felt it, trees shaking, vines swaying, all the forest’s denizens falling deathly quiet.
Slowly, hardly daring to breathe and disrupt the absolute silence, I crouched. My tail wrapped around the branch I perched on, squeezing tight as if worried I might fall. The fingers
of my working hands flexed, claws unsheathing to dig into the hard, thick bark of the tunjun tree.
I shouldn’t go. I could already hear the elders’ scolding, see my sire’s disapproving glower.
I couldn’t bring more trouble to the village.
Couldn’t
. Not again. But…wouldn’t it be best for everyone if I worked to keep trouble
away
from the village? Wasn’t that what I’d decided when I moved into my current den, far from everyone else?
My weight shifted forward, the fingers of my wing-hands flexing as they readied to stretch out and carry me through the trees.
Curiosity—my greatest failing and the cause of so much ill, according to my sire—burned in my chest. I wanted to see the fallen sky-light. I wanted to know what it was. Why had it fallen? Would more fall with it? Maybe I could figure out how to put it back in the sky where it belonged.
A foolish thought. No matter how high we climbed, no one could reach the sky.
I should know. I’d tried once, as a young kit.
I’d also broken the thin branch at the top of the tree I’d perched in, and I’d been too young and panicked to think to use my wing-hands to slow my fall and steer me safely through the tangled maze of tunjun branches.
If that hunter hadn’t seen me tumbling and caught me…
Well, she had, and I’d survived completely unharmed. Not that my poor dame had taken much comfort in that. That was the first time she truly scolded me, her fury fueled by her fear.
Then she’d pulled me into her arms, cocooned me in her wing-hands, and rocked me until my trembling eased.
I shook the past away hard enough to make my ears slap. The past was past. My dame was gone, and no hunter would help me unless they caught me hauling back food to share with
the village. And that was mostly in an effort to keep me and the trouble I brought far away.
I shifted my weight, the claws on my toes digging into the branch, my tail clenching once before it released its grip to whip free behind me. An eager breath, a flex of muscles, and I leapt, spreading my wing-arms wide to glide through the forest, away from my den and toward the fallen sky-light.
Once, when I was still a kit, my sire and dame took my sister and me to the city grown deep in the mountains beyond the forest. There, they had a temple dedicated to the skies, and their greatest idol was a once fallen sky-light. To my young eyes, it had looked like nothing more than a strange rock. Shiny in some places, smooth in others, with ripples like disturbed pond water frozen forever into stone.
Now, I dangled by my tail from a low tree branch—wanting but unwilling to get closer—and stared at my fallen sky-light. This looked nothing like the stone on the temple’s altar. This was many times larger than that. Nearly as large as my smallest storage den. And it wasn’t like any stone I’d ever seen. Parts of it had broken off like bark from a rotting log, leaving a cluttered trail through the trees.
What was it? Was it truly a fallen sky-light?
Fires flickered within the downed trees, but the weight of damp vegetation soon smothered them. In the darkness, the songs of the forest dwellers returned in fits and starts until their full chorus once more filled the night. Under the cover of the noise, I dropped from branch to branch, getting as close as I dared.
I was in the middle of weighing up the merits of dropping to the ground and approaching to get a better look at the not-stone when it hissed at me. Startled, I jumped hard enough to dislodge myself from my perch. I let out an embarrassing cheep as I snapped my wing-arms out, banking in a sharp curve to hide behind the broad trunk of the tunjun. I crouched amongst the roots, wing-arms clamped to my back, tail coiled tight around my ankle, and ears pressed flat against my skull as I fought to slow my panicked breaths.
The fallen sky-light hissed again, more slowly, and a there was an odd hollow sound followed by…something. A voice, I thought, my ears pricked forward. Light and feminine, despite being a sharp bark of sound.
Cautiously—the elders would be shocked to realize I even knew that word let alone put it into practice—I crept from my hiding spot. I kept low to the ground, weaving my way through the tangled tunjun roots, until I had a good view of the fallen sky-light. And the creature now standing next to it.
It—no,
she
, I decided, spotting the same mammaries on her chest my people’s females had—placed her hands on her hips and surveyed her fallen sky-light. I stared, agog. Why, she only had two arms! Where were her wing-arms? Or her tail? She was angry enough—I could smell the peppery burn of it from where I hid—that her ears must be pressed tight to her skull, the darkness concealing them. Something about the shape of her legs bothered me, too. Like she didn’t have enough joints. Were her feet broken?
She made more noises, this time the sounds lilting and lyrical and completely incomprehensible. She lashed out with her leg, kicking the fallen sky-light. The impact made that strange hollow sound again, and she barked, jumping back and hopping about.
I squinted as if that would help my eyes pierce the distance. Her feet must not be broken for her to be hopping about like that. They were just tiny things, and remained flat on the ground. More like a kicluck’s oddly jointed paw than a proper foot with strong toes and sharp claws for leaping from branch to branch.
I looked from the strange female up through the broken branches left by the fallen sky-light to the empty sky above. Had she come from among the sky-lights? Why?
How?
And why had she chosen to fall here?
Had
she chosen? Watching a large chunk of the fallen sky-light’s smooth skin slough off and hit the ground, I thought she might not have had much say in the matter.
I touched a finger-pad to my food pouch to reassure myself it was well stocked. Someone needed to keep an eye on the strange female. To make sure she posed no threat to the village. Since I was the one who found her, it was my duty to keep her sa—I mean, keep the village safe from her.
And that’s exactly what I would tell any of the hunters if they found her.

Chapter Five
LYNN
After taking
my frustration out on the pod—which honestly hurt me more than the useless hunk of space junk—I took in my surroundings to get a lay of the land. I didn’t expect much, what with having fallen on the night side of the planet, but there was actually quite a bit for me to see.
Because the whole jungle glowed.
Awe stole the breath from my lungs and soothed the last lingering shakes of shock and terror of crash landing on an alien planet. And boy, was it
alien
.
Looking at the bole of the nearest tree, I decided it was a miracle I’d survived, pure and simple. The trunk was easily twice the size of anything in California’s famous redwood forests. These trees went beyond massive. It was more like standing at the base of an organic skyscraper than a tree.
It was pretty easy to judge the size of the thing, thanks to the colorful glowing mosses that clung to the rough, rock-like bark, limning its dimensions in a soft radiance.
The tree had a massive aerial root system. Between that and a hard line where the moss stopped growing, I suspected
the jungle suffered frequent flooding. Flooding that obviously provided the bioluminescent moss crucial nutrients or whatever.
Tangled among the roots of the giant glowing tree were giant glowing flowers. And small glowing flowers. I decided to retreat back into my pod for shelter when I saw a mushroom move in a very not-mushroom-like manner.
I tried to query the computer about what kind of lifeforms I could expect to find out there in the wild dark, but it’d gone completely dead. An hour of poking at things while I waited for my adrenaline to fade enough for me to maybe get some rest, and I discovered that anything remotely electronic was dead. Including my watch. The one with the fancy sensors that told me if the alien foods served in the mess hall were safe for human consumption or not.
So, that sucked. Trapped on an alien planet with unknown glowing flora that may or may not kill me if I breathed on it too hard.
Oh, and the emergency beacon was the final casualty. It was now little more than a fancy black brick the size of a hockey puck.
Things were looking just peachy.
I knew myself well enough to know that exhaustion fueled my more pessimistic traits, and if I kept going, I’d only drive myself into a state of apathetic depression, so I touched the fire starter necklace that had stayed with me since I was taken from Earth, and counted it as a positive. Then I dug up an emergency blanket, sniffled back the tears that always hit me when I was tired and frustrated, curled up in the solitary chair, and got some sleep.
My situation had not improved come morning, but I was in a better headspace to deal with it.
First off, I assessed my situation, which wasn’t as dire as it could have been.
The pod was trashed. That much had been evident from the look I’d gotten at it last night. The harsh light of day made everything more real and more shocking. The burned hull cracked and flaked in places, while entire panels fell off at the slightest bump. I’d survived pretty much unscathed.
Definitely a miracle. I had no idea if anything out there in the greater universe was looking after me, but I sent up a silent
thank you
, just in case.
Like me, the pod’s interior had survived, which meant there were all kinds of emergency goodies for me to pilfer, including nutrient bars, drip filters for water—I tried hard not to think about what kind of liquid they expected someone to filter while trapped in a tiny escape pod floating in space—another emergency blanket, and a small med-kit.
If, by another miracle, someone came to rescue me, I was going to use T’rlikiax’s power to submit a proposal that all escape pods also have things you’d find in a standard survival pack. Knife, some kind of fire starter—I was deeply grateful for the necklace my father had given me, but most people didn’t carry around flint and steel as a fashion accessory—rope, needle and thread, a better stocked med-kit, and maybe something non-tech that could be calibrated to the user to test food for toxins and other compatibility issues.
I snorted. My dad would have lightly slapped me upside the back of my head for suggesting that last one.
I taught you how to test for poison in plants, Lynn-bear. Getting complacent is a good way to get dead.
My lips twitched, and I thought
yes, Daaaaad
, in the exasperated tone of teens everywhere. No, really. I’d overheard T’rlikiax’s son hiss in the exact same tone whenever he thought T’rlkiax was being a worrywart.
Grief came up out of nowhere to body slam me.
Oh, god. T’rlkiax. All the other aliens I’d spent the last year working with and getting to know. And Hirza. Hirza wasn’t even serving on the
Rsp’rin
. She’d dropped in during her vacation to check in on me and make sure I was doing okay.
Now she was gone. They were all gone.
I wrapped my arms around my stomach and curled in on myself, screaming out my rage at the unfairness of it all. And if the scream became sobs and tears fell from my eyes like waterfalls, well, no one else was there to see, so it was okay.
Five minutes, I decided. That was all the time I could afford to let myself break.
Once I’d let the worst of the grief spill out, I straightened, slapped my cheeks, and got back to the business of assessing my tools for survival.
“Why don’t you have a weapon?” I growled at the dead pod.
It didn’t answer me, of course. It was dead. That didn’t change the fact it was also useless.
I’d tried breaking off a chunk of its metal hull to maybe use as a spearhead or knife or something, but the stuff was either too strong for me to work with my bare hands, or it was too brittle from being superheated during reentry.
That left me with a pack—made from one of the two emergency blankets—filled with food, the means to filter water, and nothing useful to defend myself.
I turned my back on the useless pod to consider the jungle. I could trace the path the pod took during its crash by means of the sunlight trickling through the dense canopy. It was a good thing those massive giant trees didn’t have many limbs, or I’d have crashed into one of those and likely exploded on impact. Like an insect smacking into the windshield of a truck going eighty on the interstate.
The super massive trees weren’t the only ones in the jungle. I’d find a broken branch lying around sooner or later. In the meantime…
It took some work, but I managed to tear a long strip from one of the emergency blankets. I scrounged up a pebble roughly the size of a peach pit, and gave my makeshift sling a whirl.
It worked. More or less. Not well, and not comfortably, but it’d do in a pinch until I made something better.
Good enough. It was time to take on the jungle.
What’s the point of modesty out in the wilds, Lynn? No one’s going to see you, and modesty won’t help you stay alive. Take off your damn shirt and put it to better use
.
Grumbling under my breath, I stripped out of my fancy button-up blouse. I’d already converted my makeshift sling into a hair-tie, using it to keep my short hair off my neck, but that barely helped in keeping cool. It was too damn humid.
“Humans aren’t designed for ultra humid climates,” I griped.
At least this human wasn’t. Montana was considered semi-arid, meaning we got some humidity, but nothing like this.
One of my dad’s few buddies had told me stories about his time living in New Orleans. About how, in the summer, they experienced something called supersaturated air, where the relative humidity went
over
one hundred percent.
“What is the point of sweat when there’s so much water in the air evaporation can’t happen?” I snapped at the nearest plant.
It, too, was sweating. Great big drops of neon yellow goo I very carefully did not touch.
My sweat sure wasn’t doing anything to help me. I was drenched in it. Which was bad. Not because losing water was dangerous—though it was—but because of the loss of electrolytes. Without those, you could drink until your organs drowned and still die dehydrated.
At least the canopy was dense enough I didn’t have to worry about sunburn on top of everything else.
I finished tying the sleeves of my blouse around my waist and huffed. As comfortable as I was going to get, I continued trekking through the sometimes dense undergrowth. Ten minutes later, I kicked off my low pumps after nearly twisting an ankle for the third time. I glared at the useless things and poked at the blisters forming on my heels. And on the tips of my toes, for some weird reason.
The thick loam of the jungle floor would be more gentle on my feet than those damn pumps, so I may as well get started building calluses now.
Shortly after I finished wrapping my blistered toes with tape from the pod’s med-kit, I found a good stick. No, it was the
perfect
stick. Long and straight, without too much taper on either end. The bark chewed the skin of my hands like serrated teeth, so I used one of the ginormous rock-like jungle trees to rub it off, exposing the smooth wood beneath.
“Oh, yes,” I crooned, stroking my fingertips up its length, plucking and peeling away the last lingering shreds of bark. “You’re gorgeous, aren’t you?”
In my mind, the image of my father shook his head at me. He’d never approved of my habit of talking to my tools. I blamed it on the fact a kid needed friends. Even if that friend happened to be a full-sized bowie knife named Mr. McStaberson.
I was more than willing to blame all my weirdness on the way Dad raised me. Seriously. He should have just given me the teddy bear I’d asked for.
New friend in hand, I continued on, taking in the jungle around me. The size of the plants worried me. The smallest flower I could find was bigger than my hand with my fingers spread out. The largest would have dwarfed the biggest flower on Earth. They were called corpse flowers, thanks to the stink they put out to attract pollinators. These flowers did not stink, but they did look suspiciously like they were ready to uproot and attack the first thing that touched them.
I did not touch them. I’d read
Day of the Triffids
. During my time in space, I’d seen at least two alien species that strongly resembled sentient, mobile plants. I wasn’t going to risk them
actually
uprooting and eating my face off.
But I did kind of want to find out what would happen if something disturbed them. I hadn’t forgotten seeing that mushroom skulking about last night.
So first, I found some good sized pebbles. Then, after tucking my staff through the loop of my pack to free my hands, I scaled one of the rock-bark trees, perched myself on its lowest branch—which still hung a good twenty feet off the ground—and chucked pebbles at the majorly sus flowers.
It took a few hits before I found their trigger. A pebble struck one of the tongue-like protuberances that dangled from the center of the ‘mouth,’ I figured you’d call it. The tongue
looked disturbingly like a human spine, and the instant a pebble touched it, the whole plant whipped into violent motion. Vine-y tendrils lashed out and up, sweeping through the air like feelers seeking prey, while the whole flower flipped upside down to reveal a circular mouth with rows upon rows of spade-shaped teeth.
I watched in fascinated horror as the plant, unable to find viable prey, turned on the flower next to it. The two locked together, leaves rattling and vines tangling while their freaky tongues wrapped together and…I tilted my head, unsure if they were eating each other, or eating each other out.
Whatever the case, their thrashing set off a chain reaction, and soon the entire patch of evil plant monsters were going at each other. Shuddering, I scooted back to the trunk of the tree, carefully climbed around to the side furthest from the vicious plants, then made my way back down to the ground. As soon as my feet touched solid earth, I bolted.
At least now I knew one thing to keep a leery eye out for. I’d call that a win.
OceanofPDF.com

Chapter Six
ZYJII
Whatever she was,
she was a clever female, I’d give her that.
My heart nearly burst from my chest when I saw her start throwing stones at the yreki. Nasty, wicked things. They always grew in big colonies, and they’d eat
anything
. Including each other. At least the sky-fallen female had the foresight to throw her stones at them from up a tunjun tree.
Good thing yreki couldn’t climb.
I followed her at a distance for most of the day. To make sure she didn’t head for the village, of course. I absolutely wasn’t worried about her using that long stick she stripped of bark to poke at something that would retaliate with violence and end up hurt. Definitely not. It didn’t take long for me to realize that, strange though she may be, and strange my world must be to her, she seemed to know what she was doing.
Even when she found the river, she approached it with appropriate caution, using her stick to jab the mud. Smart. A lot of nasty flesh-eaters lurked in shallow water, and if she stepped on one, she’d end up stripped to the bone before I could glide down to save her.
She didn’t need saving, though. After stabbing the mud, she eventually retreated and continued following the river downstream until she found a rocky span of shoreline. Again, she didn’t immediately go for the water, instead hurling small pebbles, further and further out with each one, and when that didn’t provoke a response from any predators, she found a bigger rock and lobbed it in. When she finally, cautiously approached, she kept her stick close to hand.
I could have told her this was a good spot to go swimming. Maybe she didn’t see the purple-teal zeeving grass rippling beneath the river’s surface. Where zeeving grew, predators steered clear. They didn’t like the scent the plant secreted when its fragile leaves were bruised.
Thinking about it, she probably didn’t even know what zeeving was. The sky she came from didn’t contain much. Just Vobe, the giant, searing eye that watched the forest from high above, and his three daughters. Shy and sweet, they only ventured across the sky once Vobe closed his eye for the night.
And then, of course, there were the sky-lights. Perhaps each light was like the one the female had fallen in. But then, how did one explain the fallen sky-light the temple displayed on its great alter?
My tail flicked in the air where it dangled beneath my perch. Mystery upon mystery, and no way to uncover answers. Frustrating.
Satisfied the water bore no dangers, the female removed the sling she’d fashioned from a piece of shiny fabric, stripped off her clothes, and waded in.
I stared. I couldn’t help myself. She really had no tail! I’d begun to think that maybe she simply kept it wrapped around her waist. That was the fashion in the city, where the crowds were thick enough wandering tails were in danger of being stepped on. Or maybe her tail was short enough she kept it
tucked beneath her clothes all the time. But no. The graceful line of her back flowed straight into the round globes of her rump, without so much as even a scar to indicate she’d once had a tail.
My own tail flicked beneath me, the tip curling back and forth. I waited as she swam further out into the river, armed with her long stick. I remained perfectly still—except for my tail, which I struggled to control at the best of times—as she paddled around a while longer she took a big breath.
The instant she dove beneath the water, I dove from the tree. I stretched my wing-arms out at the last possible moment, landing lightly on my feet. Then I bounded over to her abandoned sling and began poking through it.
I’d watched her enter and leave her fallen sky-light multiple times that morning, and each time, she’d set something on the ground. Oh, how I’d longed to get a closer look, but I wasn’t foolish enough to risk her spotting me just to sate my curiosity. No matter what the elders or the other villagers thought of me, I wasn’t stupid.
Usually.
The knots holding the length of fabric together came apart with a few experimental tugs. And then I was left free to go through the strangest assortment of…things. Some of them crackled when they moved. It startled me at first, and I darted a quick look at the river, but the female hadn’t surfaced. She’d been under for a while. Perhaps she was a dual-breather? Able to breathe both air and water, like the lumbering krisvreep?
Abandoning the thought for later consideration, I went back to studying the contents of her sling, but I had to admit defeat. Not a single thing made sense to me, beyond the fabric of the sling itself, and a couple of empty water-bags. The bags were a substance I’d never felt before. They smelled so odd, I decided maybe they weren’t meant to carry water at all, but something else entirely. And the fabric! I rubbed it between my fingers,
worried it might snag on the thick callouses left from a lifetime of scaling rough-barked tunjun trees, but it was much sturdier than it appeared, yet it was so soft. So pliable. I doubted even the best weavers in the city could replicate it.
I became so engrossed in my discoveries that I entirely forgot about the sky-fallen female. At least until she made a sharp sound from where she lurked in the river, sending me leaping to my feet.
Maybe, sometimes, the elders weren’t wrong about the dangers of letting my curiosity get the best of me.
OceanofPDF.com

Chapter Seven
LYNN
I explored
underwater as long as I could. No wonder this section of the river was a funny color. The grasses here grew teal and purple, not green. I spent a good long while looking for fish—to eat or to avoid, I wasn’t picky—but all I found were some tiny little frog-like things with lacy tails and a collection of brightly colored snails I very carefully didn’t touch.
I didn’t know how things worked on this planet, but I’d come to learn that, in the wider galaxy, bright colors usually meant “Danger!” Some of the most beautifully colored of the sapient alien species also happened to be the most deadly.
Better safe than sorry,
my father’s memory said with a shrug.
When I finally emerged from the river, I did so as silently as I could. I’d been under long enough some curious critter might have come to sniff around the clothes I’d left on the bank. I spent half a second being annoyed at my own sense of paranoia. Until it proved well founded.
I gaped at the creature pawing through my things. He—definitely physically male—lifted one of the nutrient bars to his face and sniffed at it, his huge ears swiveling back and down
before springing upright again. A long tail snaked behind him, the tip curling like a cat’s as it flicked back and forth.
He looked…almost human. A lot like a giant lemur, with those huge ears and prehensile tail. But his facial features, those were human. Ish. Had one of the planet’s natives found me already?
I was content to study him, silently treading water, until he tried to bite the still-wrapped nutrient bar.
“Hey!” I shouted.
He startled like a cat, dropping the bar like it’d burned him as he leapt a solid three feet straight up. He landed in a crouch, all the fur on his body standing on end, tail bushed out, and—and, holy shit, were those
wings?
They looked like freaky long-fingered hands with thin skin stretched between them. Like bat wings. But more hand-shaped. He spread those hand-wing-things and shook them as he hissed.
Refusing to be intimidated—who would be frightened off by winged jazz hands?—I surged up and slapped the river’s surface, sending a gout of water in his direction. With a startled, bass-y
cheep!
he bolted to the nearest tree and darted up it, vanishing into the shadowed branches.
Painfully aware of my nudity, I rushed from the water and snatched up my staff, holding it in front of me and settling into the fighting stance I’d taken a thousand times before. Because no way would my crazy father let his only daughter go out into the world unable to defend herself.
Why a staff, you might ask? Because in a fight, reach matters, and staves were quickly made from easy-to-find materials. I was better with a sling, but, stupid me, I’d used the strip of fabric meant to be a sling to tie my hair up when the heat and humidity first started threatening to melt me.
I waited, heart pounding, hardly daring to breathe, but there was no sign of the lemur alien. I strained my hearing, but the
only sounds remained the strange calls of the jungle’s residents and the gentle burble of the river behind me.
Only when I was utterly convinced I was alone did I plant the butt of my staff on the ground and slump against it. Shit. I’d meant to startle him so he wouldn’t bite through the wrapper. Eating plastic—even the biodegradable stuff the ISSC used—wasn’t healthy. But then he’d acted aggressively, and I’d responded exactly the way my father taught me.
Now he was gone, and I still didn’t know if he was just a staggeringly humanoid animal, or a sapient being who might be willing to help me.
“Good job, Lynn,” I whispered.
Sighing, I gathered up my discarded clothes and carried them into the river to clean. As soon as that was done, I needed to find shelter before dark fell.
I found shelter amongst the trees, unsurprisingly.
The ground beneath two of the behemoth trees had collapsed, causing them to crash one atop the other in a giant T. Their boles were so thick around that the leveled one would make a decent floor, while the one propped against it provided a pretty solid roof. I figured with a bit of work to close in the sides, it’d serve as a good place to stay.
At least for now. Until I figured out what I was going to do. If I was stuck on this planet indefinitely, then building an actual house for myself might be a good idea. Considering the water lines on all the trees—a line that extended several feet above my head—I’d probably want to build it up in the trees to avoid what was likely seasonal flooding, and that…wouldn’t be easy.
No. Best to focus on my most immediate needs. With shelter secured, I just needed fire and food. And clothing. Not for modesty’s sake, but because human skin was fragile and needed protection from things like thorns, poisonous or irritating substances, and the sun. Most especially the sun. What little there was of it.
I always burned more easily than my darker skinned father, but a couple good peels, and that’d be it. Here…I studied my bare arms. Because of the jungle’s overabundance of deep shadows, I hadn’t spent more than twenty minutes total exposed to direct sunlight, and yet my skin was still pink and tender to the touch. Not quite a burn, but I’d definitely want to avoid prolonged time in the sun.
Maybe I could find an aloe-like plant to help with my burn if it got worse, though the thought of experimenting on my already abused skin made my stomach twist. Or maybe that was the lack of food. Or possibly the water I’d drunk at the river, even though I’d filtered it. Or it could be heat stroke.
Sitting in the deep shadows of my shelter, I wrapped my arms around my knees and gave myself permission to spend a whole three minutes feeling sorry for myself. Then it was time to get up and gather the materials for a fire.
Days on this planet were longer than what I was used to, either on Earth, or aboard the ISSC ships I’d traveled on. In some ways, that was good. It meant I had more daylight to do things like find and carry rocks up to my shelter to make a fire-pit. And then haul up several storage bags worth of clay-like mud to help buffer the hard bark of the trees from said fire-pit, because the last thing I wanted was to set my shelter on fire.
It also meant I had enough light to venture out on my first real forage for food. I found several good options, picked the one that seemed most promising—the riper fruit closer to ground level had been heavily munched on by other critters, so the chances of it being poisonous to me were significantly lower—and took one piece of fruit back to my shelter.
Then came the agonizingly slow process of deciding if the fruit was edible. A task I started after I spent thirty seconds mourning my watch, which would have made things so much easier.
I started by cutting the fruit open and rubbing the flesh—neon pink with streaks of blood red and ember orange—on a patch of unburned skin. While I waited, I took one of the dirtied storage bags to the nearby river, rinsed it out, and carried it back and attached it to the filter, then stuck the filter’s hose into a clean, empty bag.
An hour later, and my skin remained fine. No redness. No itching. No signs that I’d applied anything abnormal but for a clinging trace of stickiness.
Next came the tongue test.
Cutting off a fresh piece of fruit, I barely touched it to the tip of my tongue, then I sat and waited, assessing. No burning. No stinging. My breathing remained free and easy. It tasted a little sweet, and a lot bitter, but the flavor wouldn’t matter if I got hungry enough.
Setting it aside, I ate half of a nutrient bar, then shoved all my food stores into the last clean storage bag, tied a rope around it, and went to go hang it from a tree someplace a good distance away from my camp.
I figured what was good practice to follow in bear country could be life saving here. Except, that also left the bag of goodies free for curious and intelligent critters like the one by the river to get into. So I compromised. I hung it from a tree away from
my campsite, but well within range of the sling I fashioned from another torn strip of emergency blanket and a couple of long, thin, flexible vines.
Exhausted beyond imagining, I sat in front of my unlit fire pit, debating whether to get it going. I wasn’t cold, and I had no food to cook, so why waste time, effort, and—most importantly—fuel?
With a sigh, I wrapped myself in the undamaged emergency blanket, curled up at the back of my shelter, and tried to sleep.
OceanofPDF.com

Chapter Eight
ZYJII
I shouldn’t do it.
Even as I wove together a rudimentary basket and filled it with a variety of good, edible fruit, I knew I shouldn’t do it. But she needed help, and I wanted to help her.
I’d watched my sky-fallen female lick a fruit but not eat it, and that’d been after she’d rubbed it against her furless skin. It took me too long to realize what she was doing and, more importantly, why she did it.
Of course
she wouldn’t know which fruits were safe to eat, and which would make her sick. She was different enough that I agonized over my selection until well after Vobe left the sky, and Meerya, the first daughter, appeared.
This didn’t mean anything, I reminded myself as I crouched on the branch she’d hung one of her odd bags from. I wasn’t leaving her a mating offer. That would be ridiculous. I didn’t know for certain what she was. I knew only that she’d fallen from the sky, and she didn’t know which fruits were safe to eat.
So I looped a second vine around the branch, tied my basket to it, and firmly ignored the instincts that whispered
mine. Mate.
Basket carefully hung, and both lines smeared with a salve that’d keep insects and other smaller pests from getting at the
stored food, I retreated to another tree to rest. I started to settle down, but hesitated. I finally decided she’d be safe enough I didn’t need to be closer. The resourceful little sky-fallen female could likely handle anything that disturbed her rest. Probably.
I grimaced. Maybe I should keep watch until Vobe’s rising. Just in case.
A familiar scent on the air startled me awake. My eyes darted immediately to the sky-fallen female’s den to find her still safe and sleeping. Sitting away from the trunk I’d slumped against when I quite unintentionally fell asleep, I stretched my working arms above my head and my wing-arms out to the side, all my fingers spread wide as I yawned. I shook my head hard enough to make my ears flap, readjusted the grip my tail had on the branch—an instinctive thing to keep a sleeping Mrriiv from tumbling out of the trees while wandering in the realm of dreams—and slumped back, fully intending to return to sleep.
“Zyjii!”
The quiet hiss barely sounded above the singing night insects, but it caught my attention like a tail-smack to the face.
I jerked to my feet, spinning around and looking to the next branch up to find Phex crouched above me, her tail lashing and a ferocious scowl on her face.
“I’ve been looking all over for you,” she hissed. “Why are you sleeping out here and not in your den?”
Normally, I was happy to see Phex. Aside from my sister, she was the only one of my friends who continued to seek me out after my exile. Today…
I slanted my ears in a sheepish expression, desperately searching for a plausible answer to her question that didn’t
involve the sky-fallen female. Fortunately, I spotted the perfect excuse in the not-so-far distance.
I pointed over her shoulder with a wing-finger, my working hands busy rubbing the lingering traces of sleep from my eyes.
“The blossoms close up and disappear during the day,” I said. “I didn’t
mean
to fall asleep here—” which was the truth “—but I must have nodded off while waiting for Vobe to rest.”
Phex glanced over her shoulder, spotted the dappled glow of the ee vynla patch, and scowled.
“What do you want with those?” she asked.
“I want to know how they glow.”
Her lips parted in disbelief, ears pressed flat against her head. Shaking out her wing-arms, she snapped her tail at me in annoyance.
“It doesn’t matter
how
they glow,” she said. “That’s what ee vynlas
do
. They glow. We should harvest some to take back to the village. The dyers will be most pleased.”
A quick check of Meerya’s position in the sky told me all I needed to know.
“There’s not enough time,” I said. She tossed me a questioning look, and I shrugged my wing-arms. “Vobe will rise before we get back and their glow will fade, taking any color they produce with it. The dyers need most of the night to extract the pigments.”
“That’s why you’re all the way out here looking at flowers,” Phex guessed.
I bobbed an ear in agreement. “The patches closest to the village have already been picked over, and these ones are too far away to be of use.”
Leaving me free to study them at my leisure.
That they grew so close to the sky-fallen female’s den was merely happy coincidence.
Phex leveled her ears at me, a smile curving one corner of her thin lips. I braced myself. I knew what inevitably came with that particular slant of her ears.
I tried to head it off, gesturing with a working-hand. “Come join me. This is a small patch, and I don’t see any sign of the glow bugs you usually see fluttering about ee vynla. Maybe there’s a reason for that.”
“I’m surprised your curiosity hasn’t gotten you into trouble yet, Zyjii.” She reached for me with a working hand, and I let her take one of mine. “Come back to the village with me, where it’s safe.”
I stiffened, my wing arms twitching open before I could control myself. “Have the elders called me back, then?”
Her expression shuttered, ears pressed against her skull as she released me and stepped back.
“No,” she admitted. “But if you’d just come and apologize⁠—”
“I did apologize. Over and over.” Every time something went wrong, even when it wasn’t my fault. I became known as the troublemaker, and so all troubles were blamed on me. “It’s safer for all of us if I stay far away from the village.”
“If you’d just promise to keep your tail to yourself, they’d⁠—”
“No,” I hissed hotly, though I kept my voice low. I didn’t want to risk waking the sky-fallen female and have her catch Phex's notice. That’d lead to all sorts of bad things. “I’m not going to change who I am to please the village.”
“Then what about me?” she demanded. Her pain didn’t touch her expression, but the way her tail wrapped around her ankle and squeezed gave it away.
Seeing her hurt made my chest ache, but I wouldn’t relent. I
couldn’t
relent. There was still so much for me to discover and learn. I wasn’t ready to give that up.
When I remained silent, struggling to find the words to convey everything I was and everything I felt, she shuffled closer.
“Come back for me,” she breathed. “Let us knot our threads together.”
I reared back, wing-arms snapping out. My immediate, visceral reaction hit her like a tail-slap to the face. I saw the impact. Was helpless to stop it as her expression shuttered, her whole body going still so she gave nothing of her inner pain away.
“I
can’t
, Phex,” I said, a plea in my voice I knew she heard. One she chose to ignore. “You want me to change, and I
can’t
.”
“Won’t, you mean,” she spat.
I clenched my wing-arms in tight, my tail wrapped around my ankle the same way hers had been just moments ago. Spinning on her clawed toes, she threw herself from the branch, wing-arms snapping out to carry her away.
A soft sound of distress escaped me before I could stop it. Dropping into a crouch, I wrapped my working arms around my knees, my wing-fingers over my head, and tried to keep my heart from caving in on itself. Why didn’t she understand that this was for the best? Why couldn’t she be happy with me as I was?
I peered out between my arms, toward the sky-fallen female’s den. More trouble? Or would she prove to be something good? Something the village might see as beneficial.
Would I have any chance of convincing them of that?
Somehow, I doubted it.
OceanofPDF.com

Chapter Nine
LYNN
Imagine
my surprise when I got up the next morning and discovered someone—my mysterious possible local, probably—had added a hanging basket full of fruit to my food stores.
I pulled up the basket first, my stomach gurgling as I picked up a strawberry-shaped thing the size of both my fists. The bright, radioactive green skin made it look like something to avoid, but that was Earth rules, and
everything
in this jungle had insane amounts of pigmentation.
My stomach growled again, louder. I could choke down another nutrient bar. Or…
I pulled one of the fruits from the basket, sniffed it, and took a tentative bite. The skin was sour enough to make my eyes water, with a thick, bitter rind like an orange. Beneath those, though, the flesh was deep magenta, juicy, and sweet.
God, I hope it didn’t poison me, because that was
good
.
Tucking the basket under my arm, I took in the jungle around me, making a special point of looking up.
“Thank you,” I said.
A twitch of motion in the corner of my eye. I whipped toward it, certain I’d seen a striped tail, but there was nothing there. If
my local friend had decided to hang around and watch me accept his gift, then he’d hidden himself well.
That was fine. I’d scared him yesterday, yet he’d brought me a nice variety of fruits to try. And it had to be him. Who else could it be?
I’d coax him out of hiding eventually, I decided. But first, breakfast. Then I needed to find a rock sharp enough to carve my stick into a proper spear. And set traps. Anything that’d put fresh meat on the metaphorical table.
Ten minutes later, I still felt fine, so I scarfed down the rest of the radioactive green fruit—after carefully peeling it—then selected a couple more options to try out later.
With food and temporary shelter secured, I rehung the basket, retrieved my staff, then set out to explore my new home.
Muddy riverbanks.
I narrowed my eyes at the expanse of brown slop, munching on the last of the fruit I’d brought with me. This one was kind of flat and tasted a bit like jackfruit, only with a bit more punch. It was also dusty purple speckled with neon orange and mint green. Ugly to look at, but it sure tasted good.
Back to the mud. Rivers in Montana were relatively safe places, since the only big predators were land predators, and if you bumped into a bear at a creek, it was probably more interested in catching fish than chasing you. Mountain lions were ambush predators. If you saw one…well, you weren’t
safe
, exactly, but they probably weren’t planning to eat you unless you did something stupid like run away.
My papa didn’t raise me as a fool, though. I knew perfectly well there were places in the US where muddy riverbanks
meant crocodiles. Or alligators. Or crocodiles
and
alligators. In places like the African Savannah, watering holes were especially dangerous, because all animals needed water, and it was easier to set up shop next to the only source of said water for miles and miles and wait for your next meal to stroll into your mouth.
This humid hellscape of a jungle was not a desert savannah. Water was plentiful and easy to come by. That did not make the river safer.
Ever heard about fresh water sharks? How about piranha? Those things
loved
jungle rivers.
But it was the mud that bothered me. I’d seen no animal sign at all around the rocky shore I’d explored yesterday. Aside from the danger snails and lacy tailed frogs, I hadn’t seen anything in the water, either.
I crouched within the coiling fronds of a fern-like plant with leaves as long as I was tall and studied the mud. Tracks churned it into an illegible mess. I didn’t see any signs of gore, or logs moving against the current, or anything else to raise an alarm. From my very careful observation, it looked like a well-used river access.
With a grunt of satisfaction, I settled in, sling at the ready.
The hardest part of hunting was the waiting, holding yourself as still as possible for extended periods of time. As the foreign sun rose higher in the sky, the temperature skyrocketed, even in the shade, making the wait that much harder. Moisture wafted from the leaves of plants overhanging the river in visible wisps, adding to the general mugginess.
I was in the middle of slowly flexing and relaxing my calves to keep them from cramping when the undergrowth nearby rustled. I froze, then slowly checked to make sure my sling was loaded and ready. I moved my arm back, wrist cocked and ready to whirl, but then the creature stepped out of the shadows into
the light dappling the muddy riverbank, and all thoughts of fresh kebabs for lunch fled.
The creature had cloven hoofs like a goat or deer, and the sloping back and short tail of a hyena. Its head was definitely on the equine side, with a single massive horn protruding from its brow.
I barely managed not to wheeze. It was a unicorn. An actual fucking unicorn. Ugly as sin, sure, with bulging muscles more suited to a rhino than a horse, and it had a tiny set of auxiliary…hands? Limbs. Kind of like the depiction of T-Rex in that one movie from the nineties. But it could definitely be classified as a unicorn.
It was also roughly the size of an African elephant. No way in hell would my little pea sling phase it. It probably wouldn’t even annoy it.
I watched, breathless, at the giant unicorn stepped daintily through the mud and bent its massive head to drink. Its hind legs actually lifted off the ground, dangling uselessly in the air, and I bit the side of my fist to keep from giggling. It used its little auxiliary limbs to push itself upright, gave its head a shake, and then strode slowly back the way it came.
Okay. So. That happened. Holy wow.
I shook my head. Of all the strange-ass alien shit I’d seen in the year since I’d left Earth, a giant mutated unicorn definitely topped the list.
It kind of made me wonder what other weirdness the jungle had in store for me.

Chapter Ten
ZYJII
It took
me a while to figure out what my sky-fallen female was doing.
I decided to keep an eye on her when she set out from her den that morning, and not at all because she’d spoken to me. I didn’t understand the words, but her intent had been clear. She accepted the fruit I’d left for her, looked out in the forest, and offered words of what I hoped were gratitude.
Following her was just good sense. Watching was the first step to understanding, or so the hunting tales told.
So when she hunkered down near a dangerous swath of muddy riverbank, I perched on a branch above her and out of her immediate view, and I watched. And I puzzled. She just…sat there. Occasionally she’d check the rock she’d placed in the interesting tangle of vine and cloth she carried. Sometimes she stood slowly and moved about a bit. But mostly she just crouched there, still and staring.
Perhaps she was doing what I liked to do. She certainly seemed surprised and awed when a pryn strode past her hiding place to get a drink from the river. I tried to see it as she must,
but…it was just a pryn. Big. Amiable. Only dangerous when they came together in large groups to breed and raise their young.
Pryn were so safe that young kits often made a game of riding on their sloped shoulders to see how far they could go before a hunter spotted them and sent them home again.
My sky-fallen remained still and hidden as the pryn took her drink and strode back into the forest.
More denizens of the forest followed. A trigiz, all waddling movement and bristling quills, its two noses snuffling and waving independently of each other. It skirted close to the sky-fallen female’s hiding place, but she didn’t move, and it didn’t notice her. Or, more like, it trusted in its dangerous quills enough to not view her as a threat. It, too, took a drink and retreated back into the forest.
A family of vriiyn came. The father did a poor job of keeping the curious kits tucked safely in his pouch, their antics making me smile. The mother sniffed at the mud and sang a warning, sending her mate and kits scampering back into the forest’s sheltering shadows.
I tensed at the haunting cry. Vriiyn were small, as predators went. That made them as wary as they were curious, and more intelligent than the big hunters. The warning cry at the edge of the mud meant something lurked nearby.
Muddy shores were always dangerous, especially in places where zeeving grass didn’t grow. I frantically searched the undergrowth around my sky-fallen female, readying my spear as I did, but I saw nothing out of place.
I should just drop down there and warn her. Tell her it wasn’t safe. But how? If I found her liquid words strange and unknowable, then it’d be the same for her. I could try to scare her off, but after her fierce reaction to me poking through her things yesterday, I thought it more likely I’d receive a whack from her stick for my troubles.
A pair of kiclucks ventured out from the undergrowth, noses high. My sky-fallen female had chosen her perch well, since they didn’t scent her. They also didn’t scent the danger lurking along the river’s edge.
The faintest shift of leaves caught my attention, and my ears whipped to pinpoint the spot, but the sky-fallen chose that moment to move. A twitch of her wrist set the tangle of vines, cloth, and rock swinging, then spinning. Her eyes never left the kiclucks as her contraption snapped straight out. There was a sharp sound of rock striking flesh. I gaped down at the kiclucks, one dropped where her rock had struck it, the other already disappeared into the undergrowth.
H-how?
I stared, amazed, as my sky-fallen female wound up the vine and cloth creation that was clearly some kind of weapon. One that let her launch small rocks with lethal force with minimal movement on her part.
Taking up her stick—though in her hands, I suspected it became something more like a headless spear—she finally left the relative safety of her hiding spot.
I tensed, my tail wrapping tight around the branch I perched on as I leaned out, frantically searching. No, no, no. She was a stranger from beyond Vobe’s glaring eye. There was no way she could know about mlyrks. About how they baited their traps with smaller prey. The fact she baited the trap herself wouldn’t matter to it. It⁠—
My clever little female stabbed her headless spear into the mud. How did she know to do that? I wondered, but quickly realized that just because she knew
to
look didn’t mean she knew what to look
for
, because she didn’t see the reeds shift at the edge of the mud.
“Look out!” I shouted.
Stupid! She wouldn’t understand! My tail snapped free of the branch as I launched myself downward, spear held in the hunting grip my sire had taught me.
She might not understand me, but my little sky-fallen was
fast
. She paused in reaching for her kicluck prize just as the mlyrk burst from the mud, its maw gaping open as it lunged straight for her. Letting out a shriek not all that different from the vriiryn, she swung her headless spear around and—
crack!
The mlyrk was too big for her to stop it, but that cracking blow gave her the chance to twist out of the way of its teeth-filled maw. The instant her feet settled, she whipped her headless spear around again, striking hard enough the force of the blow sent her staggering back.
Crack!
The mlyrk flopped, hissing and snarling. It’s disorientation gave me the split second I needed to adjust the angle of my fall, and then I struck. The shard-rock head of my spear slipped cleanly into its neck, right at the back of its head, severing its spine. The hissing stopped, but the flailing became more wild, unpredictable.
A mlyrk’s death throes were nearly as dangerous as the living creature. Mrriiv hunters had been caught and crushed by its massive, flat tail.
Leaving my spear in place, I leapt away, snagging the sky-fallen female by the waist. I carried her to the nearest tangle of tunjun roots, where it was safe. Then I carefully set her down and put distance between us, giving her my most charming smile in the hopes she wouldn’t whack me the way she’d whacked the mlyrk. Unlike the massive river beast, I didn’t think I’d survive it.

Chapter Eleven
LYNN
I eyed the local,
who I pegged as the same one who’d gotten into my makeshift pack yesterday. Probably also the same one who’d left me a basket of fruit to eat. And he’d definitely just saved my ass from a river monster.
He grinned at me, all sharp teeth and charm. The snap of his ridiculously long tail gave away his nerves, though. Assuming I was reading the body language right.
Whatever. He’d dropped out of nowhere to spear the monster, then hauled me away before a thrashing coil of solid muscle could squish me flat. I figured that meant he didn’t plan to hurt me. It also meant I owed him one.
Deciding the best course of action for the moment was to ignore him in preference for the bigger threat, I focused on the felled river monster.
Yeesh. Talk about terrifying creatures from the pit of Hell. Its face looked vaguely like a cuttlefish and crocodile had a lovechild, all teeth and short tentacles—arms? whatever a cuttlefish’s face limbs were—and forward facing alien eyes. The body’s thrashing eventually stopped, settling in the mud to keep twitching as the last nerve impulses sputtered out. It’s long
narrow body had way too many legs and was topped with a forest of spines that looked suspiciously like the river reeds growing nearby.
No wonder I hadn’t seen the thing. I’d been looking for plants that looked displaced, but those had blended in too well.
Why pick me to munch on? It certainly hadn’t reacted to any of the previous animals that came traipsing along.
Once it stopped twitching, I took a step toward it. The local chattered and hopped past me, indicating it was probably safe now. I followed more cautiously, staff held at the ready. Just in case.
Studying the creature’s remains, I finally decided it probably determined edible prey based on weight. Its tail ended in a broad disk that matched the remains of a depression in the mud, right in the center of the accessible portion of the riverbank. It hadn’t gone after the unicorn because it’d been too big. The quilled thing must have been too much of a threat, even for the monster. It hadn’t gone after the little lizard rodents because they were too small. The wolf size catlike fox marsupial things had fled before stepping on the pressure-plate tail. Lucky me, I hit the thing’s Goldilocks zone. I was just the right size.
My new friend chattered at me some more, my brain implant picking out individual sounds and separating them into distinct words. His language sounded a lot like Hirza’s native tongue. Lots of growls, but where hers featured yips and yaps and barks, his had drawn out vowels and short, sharp cheeps and peeps.
It was, dare I say, a very cute language.
He pointed at the monster’s flat tail and held one hand out flat, palm up. He then walked two fingers of his other hand over it, and then closed his fingers around the ‘legs,’ trapping them. He followed it with a chomp of his impressively sharp teeth.
Message received. The mud monster’s tail wasn’t just a pressure plate, it’d snap up and curl around its prey, holding it
in place long enough to make a quick kill. I’d clearly missed that part, what with being busy whacking the thing over the head to keep its teeth-tacles away from me.
I narrowed my eyes at it. “But is it edible?”
My friend’s two big ears snapped straight up, leaning toward me so hard they quivered. I lifted an eyebrow at him, and he tipped his head to the side, one ear relaxing to emphasize the puzzled puppy head-tilt.
He gestured from himself to the mud monster, then reached forward with both hands, clasped them around nothing, and pulled them back toward himself.
“You want to help me haul this thing out of the mud,” I guessed.
His ears did that straight-up quiver thing again, and I had to bite the inside of my lip to keep from smiling. His tail stilled, except for the tip, which wiggled exactly like a cat getting ready to pounce. The rest of him remained crouched, muscles relaxed. Trying to make himself look smaller, I realized. Less intimidating.
He was too damn cute to be a lethal hunter, yet there we were, with the evidence of his skills lodged firmly at the base of the mud monster’s skull.
I found it surprisingly hard to be wary of him. It was the ears, I decided. They were, without a doubt, the most expressive thing about him, and they all but shouted hopefulness and curiosity and interest.
Plus, there was no way I could butcher that massive mud monster on my own.
So I jerked my head to the monster and said, “All right, then. Come give me a hand.”
“American crocodiles can weigh up to two thousand pounds,” I told my companion as he wrapped several vines around the mud monster’s beaver-flat tail and rear most limbs. “Think this guy is that heavy?”
My local friend chattered back at me. Hauling the ends of the vines over his shoulder, back to the monster, he braced himself with his feet spread wide. Every muscle covering his finely sculpted torso flexed, thighs bunching and wings partially unfurling, and the massive monster shifted. One inch. Then more, until my friend had enough momentum to haul the thing well-away from the water and into a nearby tangle of roots.
Tossing the vines up over a root as thick around as my waist, he caught the ends and gave them a solid tug. The mud monster carcass shifted a fraction and my lemur friend grunted. Shifting his grip on the vine, he braced his feet, partially spread his wings, and
heaved
. And actually lifted the massive monster into the air.
Definitely stronger than he looked.
I rushed to help, gathering the trailing ends of the vines and finding a solid aerial root to wrap them around. And so it went. Each time he hauled the carcass a little higher, I took up the slack, using friction and tension to hold the line steady while he readjusted his grip for the next heave.
As soon as the mud monster left the ground completely, my friend grunted and chittered something, too rapid-fire for my translator to pick up. I didn’t need him to tell me what to do, though. I quickly tied each vine in a taut line hitch, and made sure they were good and tight.
“We’re good,” I said.
He glanced at me over his shoulder, studied my knots, and slowly released the vines. My knots held. Of course they did. Daddy taught me well.
Confident the monster wouldn’t fall back to the ground, my friend hurried toward me. I tensed, but his attention was on the vines. No, on my knots. He touched one, tail lashing whip quick, head tilting one way, then the other, like a very big, very puzzled puppy.
Adorable.
“Would you like me to show you how to do it?” I asked.
His ears swiveled in my direction a split instant before his head followed. He blinked large moss green eyes at me, all but quivering with excitement.
Lips twitching, I found a length of vine he’d discarded and brought it back to him. Looping it around the root, I handed one end to him and mimed pulling it taut. His ears flicked, but he did it. As soon as there was tension, I looped my end over the part he held twice, then back around the bottom in the taut line hitch. I slid the knot, taking up all the slack, then let go. He tugged on his end, but the knot remained solidly in place.
His eyes went absolutely huge with delight. Chattering excitedly, he handed me his end of the line and crouched to play with the knot, sliding it up and down the line. He unraveled it, then attempted to tie it himself.
“Almost,” I said. “Like this.”
I showed him again, then untied it for him. He got it on the second try.

Chapter Twelve
ZYJII
My sky-fallen
female watched me intently as I pulled one of my shard-stone knives from its sheath and began the messy process of gutting and skinning the mlyrk. But first…
I carefully dug the tip of my knife into the mlyrk’s gums and pried out one of its big front fangs, then offered the trophy to the female. She stared at it for a moment before her eyes shifted to my hunter’s pride—a necklace strung with the big claw from a juvenile grrn and smaller fangs from the other kills I’d made.
Her hand went to her own throat, where a strangely shiny thread hung, then shrugged and accepted her trophy.
“Traditionally we use claws to mark our first hunt,” I told her as I went back to work butchering the mlyrk. “But mlyrk claws aren’t as impressive as their teeth, and that’s the important part.”
She shifted closer to me. I flicked one ear toward her before I glanced down to find her staring hard at my back-up knife. Shard-stone knives had sharp edges, but dulled quickly and chipped easily. A smart hunter always carried at least a pair of knives, along with some unshaped shard-stone stowed away in a pouch just in case.
Lifting her eyes—not quite blue, but not grey either—to mine, she gave me her lilting, song-like words. The lift at the end made it sound like a question.
I glanced down to find her pointing at my spare knife.
“You’re welcome to take it out for a look, though you’ll have to get it yourself.” At her confused head-tilt, I lifted my hands, already stained with mlyrk blood. “I’d make a mess of the sheath, and I don’t want to do that.”
She approached me cautiously. When I got back to work, pretending to pay her no attention, she started fiddling with the toggle and strap that held the knife in its sheath.
Did she even know what a knife was?
I tried to keep one eye on the little sky-fallen female without being obvious about it. She handled my shard-blade with care, keeping her strange clawless fingertips away from the sharpened edge. Convinced she had some idea what a knife was
and
how to handle it without hurting herself, I returned my attention to my work.
And was more than a little startled when the sky-fallen female joined me, working with practiced efficiency to help me dress and butcher the carcass.
“Are you a hunter for your village, then?” I asked. If she was, maybe she’d lost her hunter’s pride in her fall from the sky?
I found it unnerving that her ears didn’t move at all. They were small things stuck to the sides of her head. So flat. So expressionless. The two dark lines of fur on her brow more than made up for the lack, though.
At my question, they winged upward, then dropped, coming together in a way that furrowed the skin between them, creating intriguing little wrinkles. I started to reach up to my own face to see if my brow moved like that, but stopped when I caught a whiff of the rich blood saturating the fur of my hands.
“My sire is a hunter,” I explained, though I knew she couldn’t understand a single word I said. “He taught me everything he knows.” I paused, then admitted, “At my dame’s insistence.”
Ah, Zyjii. My curious, clever little vriiryn
, she used to say.
You’d never be happy working a single trade, would you?
No, I wouldn’t. She’d known me so well. Had loved me despite my oddities. In her mouth, the word
vriiryn
had been an endearment. One that made me giggle and attempt to catch her tail, just like a vriiryn kit, and she’d smile and catch me up to hug me close.
In the mouths of the villagers,
vriiryn
became something cruel, a denouncement that cut my soul and left my heart bleeding. Because to them, it meant
trouble
.
A light touch settled on my shoulder, and I glanced down to find the sky-fallen female frowning up at me with an expression easily read as concern, even without the signals from ears, tail, and hand-wings I was used to.
Managing to smile for her, I forced my ears upright into a more cheerful position. She eyed me, like she didn’t believe the change in my body language, but she let her hand drop. Only to make a dismayed sound. I followed her gaze to the streaks of brackish red marring my fur.
I wrinkled my nose. She babbled, the words rich in tone and lyrical in rhythm as she wrapped her hand in the strange fabric of her waist-wrap and attempted to pat away the smears of blood.
“It’s fine,” I reassured her. “I’ll need to take a swim in the river to wash off afterward anyway. See?”
I stepped back and spread my arms to reveal the inevitable spatters of blood and gore dotting my chest. She made a strange expression—nose wrinkled up and lips twisted to one side as the lines of brow fur crashed down—but bobbed her head and returned to the task of butchering the mlyrk.
As soon as her back was to me, I stroked a finger over the mark she’d left on my arm. Perhaps I imagined it, but I swore I could still feel the heat of her. It seeped into my fur and made my skin tingle.

Chapter Thirteen
LYNN
After butchering the monster,
my local friend wrapped up the chunks of raw meat in big flat leaves, tying each parcel shut with a length of vine before tying a stack together. While he did that, I gathered the hide and some other key useful bits, setting them aside in a tidy pile.
He chattered at me the entire time he worked, sometimes gesturing with his hands, other times with his tail, or his wings. And all the while, his ears danced, swiveling back and forth, drooping to stick straight out at the sides of his head, pinning back.
My original impression seemed to be correct. Whatever my friend was, his people expressed most of their emotion through ear movement.
Hefting several of the stacks of meat over his shoulder, he gestured to the remaining one and spoke at me. Frowning, I went to it and tested its weight. Not too heavy. I quickly used one of the broad leaves and a slender vine to wrap up the bits I intended to save for later use, then slung that and the stack of meat packets over my shoulder and followed him.
He led the way further up the river. He remained on constant alert, ears up and swiveling, often in the opposite direction of his face as he checked both aurally and visually for threats. I followed his example, keeping my staff at the ready.
We made it to a rocky patch of river without incident. He studied the ground around the bank before approaching the river. Whatever he saw made him relax. He waved me over, and when I joined him, he reached into the water and lifted a section of plant up for me to see.
I recognized it. It was the teal and purple grass that’d grown where I took my dip yesterday.
My translator picked up one highly relevant word out of the constant stream of his chatter.
Safe
.
“I’m going to go out on a limb and say that grass deters predators,” I said. “And since you brought us here with stacks of raw meat, I’m assuming that applies to land predators as well as water.”
Good to know.
I didn’t know if it was my tone of voice or my body language, but he flicked one ear. After a quick wash to get the worst of the blood off his hands and arms, he rose and started gathering wood. for a fire.
I followed his example. I probably should have taken the time to scrub the rest of me clean, too, but that could wait. I was starting to get hungry, and the thought of fresh roasted meat had me salivating.
I helped my friend collect dry wood. I wasn’t at all surprised when he produced a pair of stones and bits of fluff for tinder from one of his pouches. Striking the rocks produced the expected sparks, and he had a good sized fire going in short order.
He’d set aside a number of long branches, and, pulling a small ball of twine from yet another pouch, he tied them
together, creating two tripods. He set one on either side of the fire, then settled the final, longest branch into the crooks at the top, creating a kind of spit. One too tall to do any roasting on, but it was perfect for draping thin strips of meat to smoke.
Food preservation, primitive style. Something I’d done a fair amount of back home in Montana.
I watched silently as he untied another pouch from his belt. He rubbed the contents over a strip of meat before hanging it over the spit, the scent of herbs and spice pungent in the best way.
Not about to let him do all the work, I set to making skewers so we could eat sooner.
Once I got the first skewer done, I nodded toward his pouch of herbs. “Can I borrow some of whatever that is?”
He tipped his head, one ear flopping to the side a little.
“To season these,” I explained.
I hefted the skewer, pointed at the pouch, then made a sprinkling gesture with the fingers of one hand.
Understanding brought his ears to attention. Looking more intrigued than ever, he nudged the pouch toward me, and I scooted closer to make use of it.
We worked in companionable silence until we had all the meat either roasting, smoking, or steaming in their leaf packets with the addition of some wild mushrooms and something that looked a little bit like a cross between a garlic bulb and fresh strawberries. By unspoken consent, we headed to the river afterward to clean up.
I stepped straight into the water, since I was caked up to my thighs in mud, blood, and gore. Once I scrubbed the worst of the gunk off my hands and arms, I sat and hoisted one foot up, bracing it on my knee, so I could get at the mud stuck between my toes. And to check on the blisters. They⁠—
A long fingered hand wrapped around my ankle and yanked it up, startling me out of my thoughts and nearly sending me toppling backward into the water.
“Hey!” I spluttered. “Haven’t you ever heard of personal space?”
My friend twitched at the sharp snap of my voice. His eyes went to my face, flicked down to my ankle in his hand, then back to my face again and widened. He immediately released me, babbling something as he pointed at my foot before he lifted one of his own to show me. Which was more than a little distracting, because unlike me he wasn’t sitting. He was crouched next to me, now balanced on a single foot with his body bent and contorted like some kind of circus performer.
It took me a second to understand what he was trying to get at. His foot was very,
very
different from mine.
For one thing, he had six (6!) toes, grouped into two, wide spaced clusters—like a permanent Vulcan salute—with disproportionately large thumb-like digits on each side. At least, disproportionate for a human hand. I had the vague memory of lemur feet looking something like that. No need to wonder why. The thick, super long digits were made for gripping tree branches. I’d already noticed he was semi-digitigrade. He stood with his heels resting on the ground, but walked on the balls of his feet, and I bet he could jump like no one’s business.
Actually, I’d already seen him clear a twenty-feet high leap with ease, so yeah. He was definitely a product of his jungle environment.
As I studied the offered foot, he made a smug kind of sound and gently tapped the top of the foot he’d initially grabbed.
“Oh,” I said. “You wanted to get a closer look at my feet because they’re so different.”
I could understand that. What with my intent staring at his own strange-to-me foot.
I narrowed my eyes at him. “Fine. But not more grabbing. That shit’s not okay.”
He didn’t really understand me, but he gave me a charming grin and hopeful puppy-dog eyes.
Sighing, I braced myself back on my hands and wiggled my toes in invitation.
